Chapter 10 Projective Techniques: Inkblots, Pictures, Words and Drawings

In this chapter
  1. 10.1 What Makes a Technique Projective: The Projective Hypothesis: explain the projective hypothesis, the features every projective technique shares, how projective techniques differ from objective inventories, and what they are claimed to reveal.
  2. 10.2 Families of Projective Techniques: Lindzey's Classification and Others: sort any projective technique into Lindzey's five families and know the other classifications, including Frank's.
  3. 10.3 The Rorschach I: Hermann Rorschach, the Ten Cards and How the Test Is Given: tell the story of the inkblots, describe the ten cards, and explain the response phase, the inquiry and testing the limits.
  4. 10.4 The Rorschach II: Scoring Location, Determinants, Form Quality and Content: code a Rorschach response for location, developmental quality, determinants, form quality, content and popularity.
  5. 10.5 The Rorschach III: Reading the Record, from Klopfer and Beck to Exner and R-PAS: interpret the main scores and ratios, and know the scoring systems from the five American schools to Exner's Comprehensive System, R-PAS and the Holtzman.
  6. 10.6 The Thematic Apperception Test: explain how the TAT was built and given, how its stories are read through needs, press and themes, and how it differs from the Rorschach.
  7. 10.7 Apperception Tests for Children and Other Groups: The CAT and Its Relatives: describe the Children's Apperception Test and the other picture-story tests built for particular ages and groups, including the Indian adaptations.
  8. 10.8 Completion and Verbal Techniques: Word Association, Sentence Completion and the Picture-Frustration Study: explain word association tests, sentence completion tests and the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study, and how each is scored.
  9. 10.9 Expressive and Choice Techniques: Drawings, Play, the Szondi and the Hand Test: explain projective drawings (Draw-a-Person, House-Tree-Person, Kinetic Family Drawing), play and art techniques, and the choice and ordering techniques.
  10. 10.10 How Good Are Projective Techniques? Reliability, Validity and Their Place Today: weigh the evidence on reliability, validity and incremental validity, explain illusory correlation and the Barnum effect, and judge when projective techniques are still worth using.

Weightage. 44 of the 108 questions mapped in this book (40.7%) test this chapter, 26 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 10 sections are High yield.

In the autumn of 1945 the surviving leaders of Nazi Germany sat in a row of small cells in the prison of the Palace of Justice at Nuremberg, waiting to be tried by the International Military Tribunal for crimes against peace, war crimes and crimes against humanity. The American army needed to know whether these men were sane and fit to stand trial, and it gave the task to a young army psychiatrist, Major Douglas M. Kelley (1912 to 1958). Kelley worked with Gustave M. Gilbert (1911 to 1977), an army psychologist who spoke fluent German and served as the prison's psychologist and interpreter. Their orders were to observe and evaluate the prisoners, not to treat them. The cells were so cramped that the two men sometimes interviewed a prisoner sitting on his cot, with the prisoner squeezed between them. They also gave tests. Gilbert gave a German version of the Wechsler-Bellevue intelligence scale, and the scores were all above the average of 100, most of them far above it: Hjalmar Schacht, once president of Germany's central bank, scored highest, at 143, and Hermann Göring, the most senior Nazi in the dock, scored 138.

The two men also gave the prisoners the Rorschach inkblots: ten cards, shown one at a time, each with the question "What might this be?" Kelley knew the test well. Three years earlier he had written the clinical contributions to Bruno Klopfer's manual The Rorschach Technique (1942), whose subtitle called it "a projective method of personality diagnosis". The hope behind the testing was simple and bold. If an inkblot draws out the hidden person, the records of these men might show what kind of personality could plan a war of conquest and the murder of millions. Perhaps there was a Nazi personality, and the Rorschach could see it. Yet the two men who saw the same prisoners and the same kind of records came to opposite conclusions. Kelley, in 22 Cells in Nuremberg (1947), judged the defendants sane and found no specifically Nazi pathology: they were creatures of their environment, he argued, and people like them could be found in any country of the world. Gilbert, in Nuremberg Diary (1947) and The Psychology of Dictatorship (1950), saw disturbed personalities; in 1948 he published a journal paper on Göring under the title "Hermann Göring: amiable psychopath".

Kelley and Gilbert had planned to write a book together and quarrelled instead, and the Rorschach records stayed largely unpublished for almost thirty years. When the psychologist Florence R. Miale and her co-author Michael Selzer finally printed them in The Nuremberg Mind (1975), they read in them the signs of deeply disturbed men. A year later the American clinical psychologist Molly Harrower (1906 to 1999) put the question to an experiment. She took eight of the Nazi records and eight records from people who had nothing to do with Nazism, among them clergymen and patients, chosen to match the Nazis for their overall level of mental health. She removed every name and every clue and sent all sixteen records to ten Rorschach experts, who were asked to sort them into groups and to judge how well adjusted each person was. The experts did not pick out the Nazis. The Nazi records spread across the same range, from well adjusted to inadequate, as the records of the clergymen and the patients (Journal of Personality Assessment, 1976). In 1989 a reanalysis by Eric A. Zillmer and his colleagues, using John Exner's modern scoring system, reached a similar verdict: the Nazi leaders could not be grouped into any one mental disorder.

Hold on to the Nuremberg Rorschachs, because they contain this whole chapter in miniature. A projective technique rests on a bold idea: that a person who is asked to make sense of something vague will reveal, in what he makes of it, parts of himself that he could not or would not tell you. The idea is attractive, and in some uses it holds up. But the records of the same men were read as normal by one expert and as psychopathic by another, and the experts who did not know whose records they were found no Nazi personality at all. A response may reveal the person who gave it; it may also reveal the expectations of the person who reads it. This chapter teaches what projective techniques are, how the Rorschach, the Thematic Apperception Test and the other techniques are given, scored and read, and what the evidence says about them. Remember the prisoners of Nuremberg; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

10.1 What Makes a Technique Projective: The Projective HypothesisMedium yield · 6 Qs

Suppose you want to know what another person is really like: what he wants, what he fears, how he copes when things go wrong, how he sees the people around him. The obvious method is to ask him. Psychologists have been asking, in the most careful ways they can devise, for more than a century, and the questionnaires they have built are among the best tools in this book. Yet every clinician soon meets the limits of asking, and projective techniques were invented to get round those limits. So the limits come first. After them comes the idea that named the field, the projective hypothesis, traced back to its sources in Freud and forward to the features that every projective technique shares; then what these techniques claim to reveal and to offer; and finally a short history that sets out the chapter's route through inkblots, pictures, words and drawings.

Why not simply ask? The limits of self-report

The direct way to assess personality is the self-report inventory, also called an objective personality inventory: a printed list of statements about oneself ("I often feel that people are talking about me behind my back"), each answered in a fixed format (true or false, agree or disagree, a rating from 1 to 5), and scored with a key, so that any two scorers who add up the same answer sheet obtain exactly the same score. Notice what the word "objective" means here. It describes the scoring, which leaves no room for the scorer's judgement; it does not mean that the person's answers are objective, honest or true. Inventories such as the Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory and the Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire are the subject of Chapter 9. They are efficient, they can be given to a hundred people at once, and their scores can be checked for reliability and validity in the ways Chapters 3 and 4 describe. But every one of them depends on a chain of assumptions: that the person understands the statement, knows the answer about himself, and is willing to give it. Each link can break.

  • Social desirability. People tend to answer in the way that makes them look good, often without any intention to deceive. Asked "I sometimes feel resentful when I do not get my way", most people who are honest with themselves would mark "true", yet many mark "false" because resentment is not an attractive trait. This tendency to give the socially approved answer is called social desirability, and Chapter 2 shows how test builders try to measure and control it.
  • Deliberate faking. Some people have a reason to lie. A job applicant or a parent fighting for custody of a child may engage in faking good, presenting himself as healthier and more virtuous than he is; a person claiming compensation for an injury, or hoping to be found unfit to stand trial, may engage in faking bad, exaggerating his symptoms. An inventory item whose purpose is obvious invites both.
  • Defensiveness. A person may keep threatening feelings out of his own awareness as well as out of his answers. Someone who cannot bear to know that he hates his father does not lie when he marks "false" to "I have often been angry with my father"; he has kept the anger from himself first. Psychoanalysts call the strongest form of this repression (Book 1, Section 7.8.4).
  • Lack of insight. Some of what drives a person is not open to introspection at all. Freud's picture of the mind as an iceberg, most of it below the waterline, and the modern evidence on the unconscious are set out in Book 1, Section 5.5.3. Whatever the details, a person cannot report a motive or a conflict that he does not know he has.

Two invented people show how these failures look in practice. Harsha, aged 26, is applying for a management trainee post at a firm in Bengaluru, and the selection process includes a personality inventory. When he reads "I sometimes feel resentful when I do not get my way", he can see at once what the item is about and what kind of trainee the firm wants, and he marks "false". Mohit, aged 40, has been referred to a hospital psychology department in Lucknow after a run of quarrels with colleagues and two written warnings at work. He sincerely marks "false" to "I am often angry with people", because he does not experience himself as an angry man: in his account, other people keep provoking him. On paper both men look calm. Harsha's answer is a deliberate choice; Mohit's is an honest report of a self he does not fully see. A self-report inventory, however carefully built, can only score the answers it receives.

Psychologists took two roads out of this difficulty. The first was to make the asking cleverer. In 1945 Paul E. Meehl (1920 to 2003) argued, in a paper on what he called "structured" personality tests, that an item's surface meaning matters less than what the answer predicts: if people with a particular disorder tend to answer an odd-looking item in a particular way, the item is useful whether or not anyone can see why. Items chosen in this way are harder to see through. Inventories also acquired validity scales that detect careless answering, faking and defensiveness, and forced-choice formats that pit equally attractive answers against each other. That road leads to Chapters 2 and 9. The second road was to stop asking altogether. Instead of a question whose purpose shows, give the person a task with no right answer and no obvious purpose: tell me what this inkblot might be, make up a story about this picture, finish this sentence, draw a person. Then study what he does with it. Ask Mohit about his anger and he may tell you nothing; ask him to tell a story about two men in an office and he may show you. That second road is the subject of this chapter.

Exam trap

In "objective test", objective describes the scoring: a fixed key, so the score does not depend on who scores it. It does not mean the test is unbiased, fake-proof or accurate. The older pair of names, current in the 1940s (Meehl's 1945 paper is about "structured" tests), is structured tests (inventories with fixed items and fixed answers) versus unstructured tests (projective techniques). If a question pairs "structured" with "projective", it is setting a trap: the projective technique is the unstructured one.

Lawrence K. Frank names the field (1939)

By the late 1930s the second road already had travellers. Clinicians in Europe and America were using lists of words to which a patient answered with the first word that came to mind (Section 10.8), Hermann Rorschach's ten inkblots (Section 10.3), the pictures of the new Thematic Apperception Test (Section 10.6), and children's play with toys, finger paintings and drawings (Section 10.9). What they lacked was a common name and a common theory. Both came from an unexpected direction.

Lawrence K. Frank (1890 to 1968) was not a clinician, and he never gave anyone a Rorschach. Trained in economics at Columbia University, he was a social scientist who spent much of his career in American foundations, deciding which research deserved money: from 1923 he worked on the staff of the Laura Spelman Rockefeller Memorial until it was merged into the Rockefeller Foundation in 1929, then carried on the same work for the Rockefeller philanthropies, and from 1936 to 1942 he was vice-president of the Josiah Macy Jr Foundation. From these posts he did much to fund and shape research on child development and parent education, and he knew many of the people who were trying the new methods with children and patients. In October 1939 he published a paper in the Journal of Psychology called "Projective methods for the study of personality". The tests it discussed were not his. What was his was the idea that they belonged together (he expanded the argument into a short book, Projective Methods, in 1948), and the name he gave them: projective methods, or, as they came to be called, projective techniques. (The phrase had already appeared in print a few months earlier, in a December 1938 paper by Ruth Horowitz and Lois Barclay Murphy on projective methods in the study of children; but it was Frank's paper that defined the category, and the name is credited to him.)

Frank's argument began with a view of personality. A personality, he said, is not a bundle of separate traits to be measured one at a time; it is a dynamic process, a person's characteristic way of organising experience and of giving meaning to the events and people around him. The standardised tests of his day did something useful but different: they measured how much of a trait a person had and placed him in relation to a group, telling you where he stood but not how he organised his own private world of meanings and feelings. To see that organisation, Frank proposed, you should present the person with a field (objects, materials, experiences) that has relatively little structure of its own and little cultural patterning, so that he must organise it himself; in doing so he projects onto it his way of seeing life, his meanings and his feelings. Such methods, he argued, could reveal what a person cannot or will not say, often because he does not know it about himself.

The phrase "little cultural patterning" deserves a moment, because it explains why the stimuli of projective tests look so strange. Many things in the world carry a meaning fixed by culture. Show a hundred people in Chennai a red traffic light, a wall clock or a cricket bat, and almost all of them will say the same thing, which tells you about the culture they share and almost nothing about any one of them. An inkblot, a blurred picture or the words "My father..." have no culturally agreed meaning, or only a loose one, so what a person makes of them has to come from somewhere else: from him. Frank himself compared the new methods with the techniques by which medicine and physics study an inner structure without cutting into it, the X-ray among them, and the enthusiasts of the 1940s and 1950s made "an X-ray of the personality" their favourite image of a projective test. They tended to forget the warning Frank attached to it: a personality, unlike a bone, does not simply cast its shadow on a plate; it actively organises whatever material it is given. Keep the image, and notice its weak point. A radiologist reads an X-ray against a detailed knowledge of normal anatomy. Against what does a psychologist read a story or an inkblot response? That question, about norms, reliability and validity, runs through the rest of this chapter and is answered in Section 10.10.

Frank also proposed the first classification of the methods, five kinds named by what the person does with the material (constitutive, constructive, interpretive, cathartic and refractive). It was later replaced by Gardner Lindzey's scheme of five families, and both are taught in Section 10.2.

Exam trap

The term projective methods (projective techniques) was introduced by Lawrence K. Frank in 1939. Freud gave us the word "projection", but as the name of a defence, not of a kind of test. Hermann Rorschach, who died in 1922, never called his inkblot test projective: the word was applied to it only in the late 1930s, long after his death. Henry Murray's writings of the 1930s helped attach the word to these methods, and Horowitz and Murphy used the phrase in print in 1938, but the paper that named and defined the category, and the answer every paper expects, is Frank's. Frank is not a test author: he wrote no inkblot, picture or drawing test.

The projective hypothesis

Frank's argument rests on an assumption that every projective technique shares. It is worth stating as carefully as possible, because nearly every examination question on projective tests is built on it.

Remember: the projective hypothesis

The projective hypothesis holds that when a person is faced with an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus, he must impose structure on it in order to respond; because the stimulus itself supplies little of that structure, the structure in the response is taken to come from the person. The response therefore reveals his needs, motives, conflicts, defences, perceptual style and characteristic ways of organising experience, usually without his knowing what he is revealing.

Read slowly, the statement makes three separate claims. The first is about perception: when a stimulus does not dictate what it is, the perceiver has to do the work of organising it. That much is uncontroversial. Book 1, Section 4.2 shows that even ordinary perception is an active organisation of incomplete information, and an unstructured stimulus, one whose form or meaning is deliberately left vague, simply makes that work larger and easier to see. The second claim is that the work is not random: the structure a person supplies comes from inside him, from what he needs, fears and expects and from what he has lived through. The third claim, the one on which everything practical depends, is that an examiner can read the process backwards, from the response to the person. The first claim is a fact about perception; the second is plausible and has some laboratory support; the third has to be proved, test by test and score by score, and much of Section 10.10 is about how far it has been.

Novelists understood the idea long before psychologists named it. In Herman Melville's Moby-Dick (1851), Captain Ahab nails a gold coin, a doubloon, to the mast of his ship as a reward for the first man to sight the white whale. One by one the crew stop to look at the images stamped on it, among them a tower, a volcano and a crowing cock, and each man reads in them his own concerns, from a religious lesson to the price of cigars. Ahab sees only Ahab, and concludes that "this round gold is but the image of the rounder globe, which, like a magician's glass, to each and every man in turn but mirrors back his own mysterious self." Notice that Melville's coin was not even ambiguous: it carried clear pictures. The projective hypothesis predicts that the less definite the stimulus, the more of the viewer will show in what he sees. Figure 10.1 sets out the chain of inference that the hypothesis allows, together with the checks that a careful examiner puts at the end of it.

A worked example: separating the picture from the person. Suppose a psychologist shows a picture invented for this example (it is not a card from any published test): a young man stands alone on a railway platform at night, holding an opened letter, while a train pulls away. Harsha's story runs: "He has just read that his father is very ill. He should have gone home months ago, but he kept putting it off because of his job. Now the train has gone and he is standing there thinking it is too late. He will take the morning bus and reach in time, but he will never forgive himself for the delay." Mohit's story runs: "This fellow has got his appointment letter. He waited two years for it. He has not even noticed that the train has left; he is planning what he will tell everyone. He will do very well in the new job." Same picture, two different stories. How does the examiner decide what each story says about its teller? Five steps, in order.

Step 1: list what the picture itself supplies. A man, a platform, night, a letter, a departing train. Every story must deal with these, so their presence in a story says nothing about the teller.

Step 2: list what each teller added. Harsha added illness in the family, delay, guilt and self-blame, softened by a rescue (he will reach in time). Mohit added success after a long wait, pride and the expectation of doing well.

Step 3: find out what most people add. Every stimulus has a stimulus pull, a tendency to draw particular themes from almost everyone because of what it shows. A picture of a letter and a departing train pulls for stories about news and journeys, whoever tells them. Suppose the psychologist has a norm group: stories to this picture collected from 200 young adults, each story sorted by its main theme. The share of a theme is simply the number of stories with that theme divided by the total number of stories, multiplied by 100 to turn it into a percentage. Of the 200 people, 124 told a story about leaving or missing a journey, 46 told a story about bad news from home, and 30 told a story about good news such as a job or an examination result (check: 124 + 46 + 30 = 200). As shares of the whole group: 124 ÷ 200 = 0.62, that is 62 percent; 46 ÷ 200 = 0.23, that is 23 percent; and 30 ÷ 200 = 0.15, that is 15 percent. So a story about bad news from home is common (almost one person in four tells one), and a story about good news is not rare either. Now suppose that only 6 of the 200 stories, of whatever main theme, mentioned guilt about having stayed away from home: 6 ÷ 200 = 0.03, that is 3 percent, about one person in thirty-three. Harsha's guilt and self-blame are the unusual part of his story; the bad news itself is not.

Step 4: form a hypothesis from the unusual additions, not the common ones. The rarer a theme is in the stories of people in general, the more likely it is to say something about the teller rather than about the picture. Harsha's story suggests a hypothesis: he may be preoccupied with guilt about family obligations he feels he has neglected. Mohit's story, whose good-news theme is shared by 15 percent of the norm group, suggests a weaker one: achievement and recognition may matter a great deal to him. Notice the word "may".

Step 5: check the hypothesis against everything else. One story is one observation. Harsha's father may really have had surgery last month, in which case the story reports a recent event rather than a hidden conflict; or Harsha may simply have watched a film with this plot. A careful clinician looks for themes that recur across many stimuli (a dozen pictures, a set of sentence stems), especially in stimuli that do not pull for them, and then tests them against the interview, the history and other tests. If guilt about home turns up again and again, the hypothesis grows stronger; if it appears once, it stays a guess.

Remember

Every projective response is made of two parts:

response = what the stimulus pulls from almost everyone + what this person adds

Only the second part is projective. To separate the two parts you must know what most people say to the same stimulus, which is why projective techniques need norms as badly as any other test (Chapter 5), and why a common response tells you little about the person who gave it.

Exam trap

The projective hypothesis is a hypothesis, an assumption about how people respond to ambiguous material, not a proven law. If asked what it states, give all three parts: an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus; the person imposes his own structure on it; and in doing so he reveals needs, conflicts, defences and unconscious processes. A statement that people "respond to ambiguous stimuli in the same way", or that ambiguity is used "to test intelligence" or "to measure perception of the stimulus only", is not the projective hypothesis.

Projection: from Freud's defence to Bellak's apperception

The word "projective" was borrowed from psychoanalysis, and its history explains a confusion that examiners love to exploit. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) first used projection in the 1890s, while trying to explain paranoia. In his 1911 analysis of the memoirs of Daniel Paul Schreber, a senior German judge who had developed a paranoid illness, Freud described projection as a process in which an inner perception is suppressed and its content, after some distortion, comes back into consciousness as if it were a perception of the outside world. As a defence mechanism, projection means attributing one's own unacceptable impulses, feelings or wishes to someone else. A man who cannot admit his hostility towards a colleague becomes convinced that the colleague is hostile to him; the feeling has been moved from inside to outside, where it is easier to bear and even seems to justify him. Anna Freud (1895 to 1982) listed projection among the defences of the ego in 1936, and the whole vocabulary of defences is set out in Book 2, Sections 3.2.9 and 3.4.1.

If the tests depended on projection in this defensive sense, they would be strange instruments. They could reveal only the impulses a person disowns, and every response would be a defence. Yet the early testers wanted to see much more than that: how a person organises what he sees, what he openly wants, how he handles his feelings, how clearly he thinks. Freud himself had supplied the wider meaning they needed. In Totem and Taboo (1913), discussing why people in many societies come to fear the spirits of the dead, he added that "projection is not specially created for the purpose of defence, it also comes into being where there are no conflicts" (in A. A. Brill's 1918 translation). The outward projection of inner perceptions, he went on, is a primitive mechanism that influences even our sense perceptions, so that it normally has the largest share in shaping our picture of the outer world. This is the sense the projective hypothesis needs: not a defence against an unbearable wish, but the ordinary colouring of perception by the perceiver, which goes on in everyone, all the time, with or without conflict.

Leopold Bellak (1916 to 2000), the psychiatrist and psychoanalyst whose work on the Thematic Apperception Test and the Children's Apperception Test appears in Sections 10.6 and 10.7, pushed the argument a step further. In a paper of 1944 he tried to test Freud's account of projection experimentally, and by 1950, in Projective Psychology, the book he edited with Lawrence Abt, he had concluded that "projection" was the wrong word for most of what happens in a test. He preferred apperception, an older term for perception that is interpreted in the light of a person's past experience: not the bare registration of a stimulus, but the meaning a person gives it. Every response to a picture, Bellak argued, is an apperceptive distortion, a departure in some degree from a purely objective description of what is there, and the degrees form a continuum. At one extreme lies the inverted projection of the paranoid patient, Freud's defence in its full form, in which a feeling is denied, turned into its opposite and attributed to someone else. At the other extreme lies purely cognitive perception, with almost no personal colouring, as when a person simply describes what a picture plainly shows. In between lie milder distortions, among them what Bellak called externalisation, in which a person tells a story without realising that it is about himself but can recognise it as his own once it is pointed out ("Now that you mention it, that is just how I felt when my brother left home"). Bellak's reformulation is worth knowing for two reasons: it explains the word "apperception", the "A" that Morgan and Murray had already put into the name of the TAT in 1935 and that Bellak kept in the name of the CAT, and it frees the projective hypothesis from the narrow idea of a defence.

Is there any evidence that needs and feelings really colour what people see in ambiguous material? Some of the best comes from the laboratory work of the late 1940s known as the New Look in perception, described in Book 1, Section 4.8.2. Sailors who had gone longer without food reported more food-related objects in dim or even blank slides (David McClelland and John Atkinson, 1948), and poor children overestimated the size of coins more than rich children did (Jerome Bruner and Cecile Goodman, 1947). Henry Murray had reported something similar as early as 1933, when five eleven-year-old girls, just frightened by a game played in the dark, judged photographs of faces to be more malicious than they had at a calmer time. Findings like these support the second claim of the projective hypothesis, that what a person brings to an ambiguous stimulus shapes what he sees in it. They do not, by themselves, support the third claim, that a clinician can read a particular need or conflict back from a particular response of one person. That is a much harder thing to show, and it is the point on which projective techniques have been criticised most often.

Exam trap

Do not confuse the defence of projection (attributing one's own unacceptable impulses to others, as in paranoia) with the projective hypothesis (everyone's perception of an ambiguous stimulus is coloured by his own needs, conflicts and style). The tests need the second, broader sense, which Freud described in Totem and Taboo (1913) and Bellak renamed apperceptive distortion. So "projective tests allow people to use the defence mechanism of projection" twists the theory: a projective test does not hand the client a defence; it is meant to see past his defences. And the theoretical assumption behind the TAT, as behind every projective technique, is that people project their needs and conflicts onto ambiguous stimuli: not that behaviour is shaped only by reinforcement (Skinner's operant conditioning), not that intelligence is a single general factor (Spearman's g), and not that traits are stable and biologically fixed.

What every projective technique shares

Inkblots, pictures of people, half-finished sentences, blank sheets of drawing paper, a sand tray full of toys: at first sight the projective techniques have nothing in common but their name. Look at how they work, though, and a family likeness appears. Gardner Lindzey, whose classification of the techniques is taught in Section 10.2, summed it up in 1961. A projective technique, in his description, is especially sensitive to covert or unconscious aspects of behaviour, permits a wide variety of responses, is highly multidimensional and yields unusually rich data, while the person has little awareness of what the test is for; very often, too, its stimulus is ambiguous, its interpretation is holistic, it evokes fantasy, and its answers are neither right nor wrong. Taken apart, the description gives six defining features.

  1. An ambiguous or unstructured stimulus. The material is deliberately vague, incomplete or open to many readings: an inkblot with no intended meaning, a picture in which it is unclear what is happening, the first words of a sentence, a blank page. The less the stimulus dictates, the more room there is for the person.
  2. Few or no restrictions on the response. The person may answer in almost any way: he may see one thing in a blot or ten, tell a short story or a long one, draw a large figure or a tiny one. Instructions are brief and general ("What might this be?", "Tell me a story about this picture", "Draw a person"), and the examiner usually adds that there are no right or wrong answers. Because the number of possible responses is practically unlimited, a response cannot be scored against a key of correct answers.
  3. A disguised purpose. The person does not know how his responses will be interpreted. He may know that a psychologist is studying his personality, but he cannot tell which features of his response matter (whether he used the whole blot or a small part of it, whether his heroes succeed or fail, whether his drawn figures have hands), so he cannot easily steer them. This disguised purpose is the source of the techniques' most important claimed advantage.
  4. Global, holistic interpretation. The aim is a picture of the whole personality and of how its parts fit together, not a score on one trait. The interpreter attends to the content of each response, the way it was produced, the person's behaviour during testing and the pattern across all the responses. This is called global interpretation.
  5. A clinically trained interpreter. Because responses cannot be scored with a simple key, interpretation depends on an examiner trained in a particular scoring system and in clinical judgement. Two examiners can therefore reach different conclusions from the same record, as Kelley and Gilbert did at Nuremberg; Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9 and Section 10.10 show how serious this problem is and how structured scoring systems try to reduce it.
  6. Rich, qualitative data. A single Rorschach or TAT record can run to many pages of the person's own words, each response open to analysis on many dimensions at once. The data are mainly qualitative, although some systems, such as John Exner's for the Rorschach (Section 10.5), convert them into counts and ratios.

These features make projective techniques mainly tools of idiographic assessment, the study of one individual in his uniqueness, as opposed to nomothetic assessment, which places individuals on dimensions that everyone shares and compares them with norms. The pair of terms goes back to the German philosopher Wilhelm Windelband (1848 to 1915) in 1894, and Gordon W. Allport (1897 to 1967) brought them into personality psychology in 1937. Objective inventories are nomothetic by design: a given score on a scale means the same thing for everyone who obtains it. Projective techniques were built, in Frank's spirit, to capture how one particular person organises his world, although every serious scoring system also tries to compare a record with norms. Table 10.1 sets the two approaches side by side.

Table 10.1 Projective techniques and objective self-report inventories compared.
FeatureProjective techniquesObjective self-report inventories
StimulusAmbiguous, unstructured: an inkblot, a picture, a sentence stem, a blank pageClear statements or questions about oneself
Response formatOpen: any answer, of any lengthFixed: true or false, yes or no, a rating
Purpose as the person sees itDisguised: he cannot tell what will be read from his answerOften transparent: he can see what most items ask
ScoringCoding and interpretation by a trained examiner; several competing systemsClerical or computer scoring with a key; every scorer gets the same score
InterpretationGlobal and configural; mainly idiographicScale by scale against norms; nomothetic
What is measuredClaimed: unconscious needs, conflicts, defences, perception and thoughtSelf-described traits, symptoms and attitudes
Main strengthsLess open to social desirability and obvious faking; rich material; usable with children and across languagesHigh reliability; good norms; quick group testing; easy to research
Main weaknessesVariable scorer agreement; low retest reliability; weak norms; many signs of doubtful validity; slowOpen to faking, social desirability and response sets; limited by insight; needs reading
ExamplesRorschach, TAT, sentence completion, Draw-a-PersonMMPI-2, 16PF, NEO-PI-R, EPQ

The difference between the two approaches is not all or nothing. Tests vary continuously in how much structure they impose, and it helps to place them on a line (Figure 10.2). At one end stands the true-false inventory item: the statement is fixed, and so are the two possible answers. A rating scale loosens the answer a little. A sentence completion stem fixes the beginning of a thought but leaves its ending entirely to the person, which makes it the most structured of the projective techniques. A picture-story test shows people in a scene but leaves it to the person to say who they are, what is happening and how it will end. An inkblot has no intended content at all. At the far end stands the blank card of the TAT, card 16, on which the person must first imagine a picture and then tell a story about it.

Structure in fact has two parts, and techniques can differ on each. One is the structure of the stimulus: how definite its meaning is. The other is the structure of the response: how free the person is in answering. The true-false item fixes both; the inkblot leaves both open. A sentence stem gives a partly structured stimulus but an open response. The choice techniques of Section 10.9, in which a person picks the photographs he likes and dislikes, reverse the pattern: the meaning of the stimulus is open, but the response is restricted to a choice, which is why some authors regard them as a half-way house between projective and objective tests (Section 10.2).

One more point about names. The labels "objective" and "projective" have been criticised by the very psychologists who use the tests. In 2006 Gregory J. Meyer and John E. Kurtz, writing in the Journal of Personality Assessment, argued that both words should be retired, because each implies something untrue: "objective" suggests that self-report inventories are free of bias, although their answers depend on a person's possibly distorted view of himself, and "projective" suggests that every response is a projection, although much of what an inkblot test codes, such as how accurately a person perceives the blot, is nothing of the kind. Many authors now describe the methods by what they ask a person to do. Self-report inventories ask him to describe himself; performance-based measures ask him to perform a task (see the blot, tell the story, draw the figure) while the examiner observes how he does it. The newest Rorschach system carries the word in its name: the Rorschach Performance Assessment System (Section 10.5). Examination papers in India still use the old words, so learn both pairs.

Exam trap

The four features that papers ask for are the ambiguous stimulus, the open response (no right or wrong answers), the disguised purpose and global interpretation; a trained interpreter and rich qualitative data follow from them. So if an option says "a structured response format", "a scoring key of correct answers" or "trait-by-trait measurement", it describes an objective inventory, not a projective technique. On the continuum, sentence completion is the most structured projective technique and the blank TAT card (card 16) the least; an inkblot is less structured than a picture of people.

What projective techniques claim to reveal, and which tests are projective

Supporters of projective techniques have made large claims for them, and examination papers test whether you know what the claims are. The central one is that projective techniques reveal unconscious processes: the needs, wishes, fears and conflicts of which the person is unaware, and which he therefore could not report on an inventory even if he wanted to. Around that centre the claims fan out, and different techniques are claimed to be best at different things (Table 10.2). Read every row with the word "claimed" in front of it; whether each claim holds up is the business of Section 10.10.

Table 10.2 What projective techniques are claimed to reveal.
Claimed targetWhat it meansTechniques chiefly claimed for it
Unconscious processesWishes, fears and conflicts outside awarenessAll projective techniques: the core claim
Needs and motivesWhat the person strives for: achievement, affiliation, power, aggressionTAT and the other picture-story tests (10.6, 10.7)
Conflicts and defencesOpposing wishes, and how the person handles themTAT, sentence completion, drawings
Perception and thoughtAccuracy of perception, reality testing, disordered thinkingRorschach (10.3 to 10.5)
Emotional lifeHow feelings are experienced and controlledRorschach colour responses (10.4, 10.5)
Interpersonal styleHow other people are seen and related toTAT, Rorschach human content, family drawings
Self-concept and body imageHow the person pictures himselfDraw-a-Person, House-Tree-Person (10.9)
Reactions to frustrationWhere blame is directed when the person is thwartedRosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (10.8)

Just as important is what projective techniques do not claim to measure. They are tests of personality, and in the distinction drawn by Lee J. Cronbach (1916 to 2001) they are tests of typical performance, which ask what a person usually does, feels and thinks, rather than tests of maximum performance, which ask what he can do at his best (Chapter 1). Three things that examiners like to offer as wrong answers belong elsewhere.

  • Intellectual attainment, what a person has learned in subjects such as mathematics or reading, is measured by achievement tests, and intellectual ability by intelligence tests; both are tests of maximum performance with right and wrong answers (Chapters 6 and 8). A Rorschach record can hint at intellectual level (Section 10.5), but no projective technique is built to measure attainment.
  • Parenting style is a pattern in how parents treat a child, such as the authoritative, authoritarian and permissive styles described by Diana Baumrind (1927 to 2018). It is assessed by questionnaires given to parents and children and by observing families, not by asking someone what an inkblot looks like. A child's drawing of his family may show how he experiences his parents (Section 10.9), which is a different thing.
  • Career aptitude, a person's potential to succeed in a line of work, is measured by aptitude batteries and interest inventories (Chapter 8).

Which tests, then, are projective? Every test in this chapter: the Rorschach and Holtzman inkblots, the TAT and its relatives such as the Children's Apperception Test, word association, sentence completion, the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study, the drawing techniques, play and the choice techniques. Examination papers test the boundary by mixing one or two of these with the best-known objective inventories, all of which are taught in Chapter 9. Table 10.3 gives each inventory one identifying line; what they share is fixed items, fixed answers and a scoring key.

Table 10.3 The objective inventories that appear as distractors (taught in Chapter 9).
InventoryAuthors and yearAnswer formatWhat it measures
Eysenck Personality Questionnaire (EPQ)Hans and Sybil Eysenck, 1975Yes or noExtraversion, neuroticism, psychoticism; a lie scale
Sixteen Personality Factor Questionnaire (16PF)Raymond Cattell, 1949Three choicesSixteen primary source traits
Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory-2 (MMPI-2)James Butcher and colleagues, 1989, revising Hathaway and McKinley's MMPI (1943)567 items, true or falseClinical scales and validity scales
NEO Personality Inventory-Revised (NEO-PI-R)Paul Costa and Robert McCrae, 1992Five-point ratingsThe Big Five domains and their 30 facets
Myers-Briggs Type Indicator (MBTI)Isabel Briggs Myers and Katharine Cook Briggs, from the 1940sForced choiceSixteen types based on Jung's psychological types
Millon Clinical Multiaxial Inventory-III (MCMI-III)Theodore Millon, 1994175 items, true or falsePersonality disorders and clinical syndromes

Two other kinds of method complete the boundary. A standardised intelligence test, such as a Wechsler scale, presents structured problems with correct answers and measures maximum performance (Chapter 6); Gilbert's Wechsler-Bellevue scores at Nuremberg measured ability, while the Rorschach was the projective part of the testing. Direct observation, in which a trained observer records what a person actually does in a defined situation (a child's hitting in the playground, a patient's behaviour on the ward), is the method of behavioural assessment (Book 2, Section 2.8). It uses no ambiguous stimulus at all, because it studies behaviour itself rather than responses to a test.

Exam trap

Which is projective? Set against the 16PF, MMPI-2, NEO-PI-R, EPQ, MBTI or MCMI-III, the projective answer is always the inkblot or picture test: the Rorschach or the TAT. So in an item that lists "i) Rorschach and TAT, ii) Eysenck Personality Questionnaire, iii) 16PF, iv) MCMI-III", the answer is only i. The MMPI and the MBTI, which appear as distractors in questions about inkblots, are inventories and use no pictures at all.

Which method uses ambiguous stimuli to uncover unconscious thoughts? Projective tests: not personality inventories (clear items, fixed answers), not standardised intelligence tests (problems with correct answers) and not direct observation (no test stimulus at all). What do projective tests claim to reveal? Unconscious processes: not intellectual attainment, parenting style or career aptitude. And the TAT is classified as a projective personality test, never as an objective or self-report inventory, an achievement test, an intelligence test or a neuropsychological battery.

The claimed advantages, and their price

Why would a clinician choose a slow technique that is hard to score when a quick inventory is available? Supporters give six reasons, each of which follows from the features above.

  1. They avoid self-report biases. Because the purpose is disguised and there are no right answers, the person cannot easily tell which response would make him look good, so social desirability and deliberate faking have less to work with. This is the advantage named most often, and the one examiners mean when they ask for "the main advantage". Harsha could see through the inventory item about resentment; it is much harder to know how to tell a "healthy" story about a man on a railway platform.
  2. They are harder to fake. A close cousin of the first: a person who wants to look well, or ill, has no key to aim at. But "harder" is not "impossible". Whether and how far projective records can be faked has been studied directly, and the answer is less comforting than the early enthusiasts believed (Section 10.10).
  3. They reach what the person cannot report. Because they do not depend on self-knowledge, they are claimed to reach motives and conflicts outside awareness, Mohit's anger for example. The strongest modern support for this claim concerns motives: achievement imagery scored from picture stories predicts long-term behaviour, yet correlates only weakly with what people say about their own motives (Section 10.10).
  4. They can be used where questionnaires cannot. Pictures, blots and drawings need little or no reading, so they can be used with young children, with people who cannot read or write, and across languages. A child who cannot describe his feelings may play or draw them.
  5. They build rapport. A task that feels like a game or a puzzle can relax a guarded client and open a conversation. Even the abnormal psychology textbook of David Barlow and Mark Durand, which is sceptical of projective diagnosis, grants that the ambiguous stimuli can be valuable when used as icebreakers, to get people talking about how they feel.
  6. They give idiographic richness. They produce a picture of how one person thinks and feels, in his own words, which can suggest hypotheses that no inventory would have raised.
Clinical link

Return to Mohit, the 40-year-old who sincerely denied being angry. On a sentence completion form he writes "People at work... take credit for what I do" and "If someone cheats me... I make sure he regrets it"; in four of the six picture stories he tells, the hero is wronged by a superior and takes revenge. None of this proves anything about Mohit by itself. What it gives the psychologist is a hypothesis, a recurring theme of grievance and retaliation, and a way into the interview: "Several of your stories were about people being treated unfairly. Does that feel familiar?" If the theme fits the referral, the history and the reports from his workplace, it becomes part of the case formulation (Book 2, Section 2.9); if it does not, it is dropped. Used this way, as a structured aid to the interview and a source of hypotheses to be checked, a projective technique can earn its place. Used as the sole basis of a diagnosis, it cannot.

Every one of these advantages has a price, and it is paid in the currency of Chapters 3 and 4. The openness that makes a response hard to fake also makes it hard to score consistently, so two examiners may code the same record differently. The freedom that yields rich material means that the number and length of responses vary from person to person, which complicates every score. Norms are missing, old or drawn from unrepresentative samples for many techniques; retest reliability is often low; and the validity of many favourite interpretive signs has not survived testing. These problems, and the evidence that some scores do survive, are weighed in Section 10.10; the story of the Rorschach's scorer reliability in particular is told in Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9.

Indian context

The claim that pictures and drawings travel across languages matters in a country with 22 languages in the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution and many more spoken at home. An inkblot needs no translation, and a child in a Kannada-medium school can draw a family as readily as a child in Delhi. But "no translation" is not "no culture". The people, clothes, houses and situations in a picture-story card, and the themes a person thinks natural to tell about them, are shaped by culture, so Indian psychologists have built adapted versions with Indian figures and settings: Uma Chowdhury's adaptations of the TAT and the CAT (Sections 10.6 and 10.7) and Udai Pareek's adaptation of the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Section 10.8) are the best known. Indian norms matter as much as Indian pictures: a theme that is rare in American stories may be common in Indian ones, and the base-rate reasoning of the railway platform example works only with norms from the right population.

Exam trap

"The main advantage of projective tests such as the Rorschach" is that they avoid self-report biases (social desirability, faking, defensiveness), because their purpose is hidden. Take the wrong options one at a time. "They allow people to use the defence mechanism of projection" twists the theory: the test is meant to see past defences, and the projection it relies on is everyday perception, not a defence offered to the client. "They have superior retest reliability" is false: retest reliability is a known weakness of projective techniques and a strength of objective inventories (Chapter 3, Section 3.2). "They provide numerical quantitative data" is also a strength of inventories; projective techniques yield rich qualitative material, and even Exner's numbers are counts of coded responses.

One last idea completes the logic of projective assessment, and it is easiest to see by contrast. A projective technique treats a response as a sign: an indicator of something underlying it, a need, a conflict or a trait, which is assumed to show itself in many situations. When a boy sees two animals fighting in an inkblot, the traditional assessor reads the percept as a sign of an aggressive drive. In 1972 Marvin R. Goldfried and Ronald Kent set this against the approach of behavioural assessment, which treats a response as a sample of the very behaviour of interest: rather than interpret the fighting animals, count how often the boy hits other children in the playground. The inference from a sample to the behaviour it samples is short; the inference from a sign to an inner cause is long, and every link in it needs evidence. Book 2, Section 2.8.1 tells the story of the contrast and of the behaviour therapists who drew it. Keep it in mind through the rest of this chapter. It is worth noticing, when you reach Section 10.10, that the Rorschach scores with the best evidence behind them, such as those for the accuracy of perception and for disordered thinking, are the ones closest to being samples of how a person actually perceives and thinks.

A short history of projective techniques

Where did these techniques come from, and how did a method so widely doubted become so widely used? The full story of each technique is told in its own section; here is the outline, and Figure 10.3 sets it out as a timeline.

Before the name. Painters knew the effect long before psychologists did: Leonardo da Vinci (1452 to 1519) advised painters in search of ideas to look at walls marked with stains, in which they could find landscapes, battles and figures. The first systematic technique, however, was a list of words. In 1879 Francis Galton (1822 to 1911) went through a list of words one at a time, timed the ideas each word called up in his own mind, and was struck by how many of them went back to his boyhood (Book 1, Section 1.3.4 tells his life, and Section 10.8 the experiment). From 1904 Carl Gustav Jung (1875 to 1961) and his colleagues at the Burghölzli hospital in Zurich turned word association into a clinical instrument, reading delays and odd answers as signs of emotionally charged "complexes" (Section 10.8; Book 2, Section 3.8.2), and in 1910 Grace Kent and Aaron Rosanoff published a standard list of 100 words together with the answers ordinary people give to them. In 1921 the Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach (1884 to 1922) published his ten inkblots in Psychodiagnostik (Section 10.3), and in 1935 Christiana D. Morgan (1897 to 1967) and Henry A. Murray (1893 to 1988) of the Harvard Psychological Clinic introduced the Thematic Apperception Test (Section 10.6).

The name and the boom. Frank's paper of 1939 gave the scattered methods a single name and a theory just as American clinical psychology was about to grow enormously. The Second World War and its aftermath created a huge demand for the assessment of soldiers and veterans, and the projective test became the signature skill of the new profession (Book 2, Sections 1.4.2 and 1.4.4 describe the testing era and the wartime growth of the profession). Manuals appeared, among them Klopfer and Kelley's Rorschach manual of 1942. New techniques multiplied within a few years: John Buck's House-Tree-Person (1948), Karen Machover's Draw-a-Person (1949), the Children's Apperception Test of Leopold and Sonya Bellak (1949) and many more (Sections 10.7 and 10.9). In 1950 Abt and Bellak's Projective Psychology gathered the field into one book. The Nuremberg testing belongs to this confident moment, when it seemed reasonable to hope that a set of inkblots could find a Nazi personality.

The critique. The confidence did not survive contact with the statistics. From the 1950s psychologists trained in measurement began to test the techniques as they tested any other: did two scorers agree, did a person obtain the same score a month later, did the scores predict anything? The answers were often discouraging. Reviewing the Rorschach in the Mental Measurements Yearbook, Hans J. Eysenck (1916 to 1997) in 1959 and Arthur R. Jensen (1923 to 2012) in 1965 were scathing; Jensen suggested that the rate of scientific progress in clinical psychology might be measured by how quickly and thoroughly it got over the Rorschach. In 1959 Lindzey brought order to the techniques with his classification (Section 10.2), and in 1967 and 1969 Loren and Jean Chapman showed how both students and clinicians could "see" relationships between test signs and symptoms that were not there (Section 10.10). From the 1960s behavioural assessment, with its samples instead of signs, offered clinicians another way of working, and over the following decades the share of clinicians' time spent on projective testing fell.

Repair and argument. The techniques did not disappear; they were rebuilt. In 1974 John Exner published the first volume of his Comprehensive System, which combined the best-supported parts of the five American Rorschach systems with norms and strict rules for administration and scoring (Section 10.5). Critics answered with major reviews, above all Scott Lilienfeld, James Wood and Howard Garb's "The scientific status of projective techniques" (2000), which concluded that a few scores were well supported and most were not. Defenders answered with meta-analyses, and in 2011 a group led by Gregory Meyer launched the Rorschach Performance Assessment System, keeping the scores with the strongest evidence. The argument is still going on, and Section 10.10 shows where it stands; in India the Rorschach, the TAT and their relatives remain part of clinical training and practice.

Exam trap

Firsts. The first formal projective technique is usually said to be word association (Galton, 1879), not the Rorschach (1921), which was the first inkblot test. The term "projective methods" is Frank's (1939). Some Indian textbooks add that Leonardo da Vinci chose his pupils by what they saw in ambiguous shapes, and place him in the "14th century". Treat both details with care: Leonardo lived from 1452 to 1519, in the fifteenth and early sixteenth centuries, and the pupil-selection story is a second-hand report that cannot be traced to his own writings; what his notes do record is advice to painters about stained walls. If a question asks for the earliest projective-type practice, Leonardo is the answer those books give; if it asks for the first projective technique, answer Galton's word association.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The term "projective methods" was introduced in 1939 by .
  2. The hypothesis holds that a person faced with an ambiguous stimulus imposes his own structure on it and so reveals his needs, conflicts and ways of organising experience.
  3. In the phrase "objective personality inventory", the word objective describes the of the test, not the honesty of the answers.
  4. As a defence mechanism, means attributing one's own unacceptable impulses to someone else.
  5. Leopold Bellak argued that "projection" should be replaced by the broader term apperceptive .
  6. The tendency of a stimulus to draw particular themes from almost everyone who sees it is called stimulus .
  7. The study of one individual in his uniqueness is assessment; placing individuals on shared dimensions and comparing them with norms is assessment.
  8. The main advantage usually claimed for projective tests is that they avoid biases such as social desirability and faking.
  9. Goldfried and Kent (1972) contrasted treating a response as a of an underlying trait with treating it as a of behaviour.
  10. In a norm group of 250 people, 40 tell a story about revenge to a particular picture. The share of people who tell that theme is percent.

Answers: (1) Lawrence K. Frank, (2) projective, (3) scoring, (4) projection, (5) distortion, (6) pull, (7) idiographic, nomothetic, (8) self-report, (9) sign, sample, (10) 16: 40 ÷ 250 = 0.16, and 0.16 × 100 = 16 percent, about one person in six, so a revenge story to this picture is a fairly common theme and says less about the teller than a theme told by only 1 or 2 percent of people.

10.2 Families of Projective Techniques: Lindzey's Classification and OthersMedium yield · 3 Qs

On her first morning as a trainee in the psychology department of a large teaching hospital, Naina was handed a key and a simple job by her supervisor, Parul: tidy the test cupboard. Behind the door she found a box of ten inkblot cards, a folder of grey pictures of people in uncertain situations, pads of printed half-sentences, a sheaf of cartoon frames in which one character's speech balloon had been left empty, a pack of photographs of faces, a tin of crayons with a stack of blank paper, and a tray of sand beside a basket of toy people, animals and houses. Every item carried the same label in the department's register: projective. Naina's first thought was to arrange them by what they looked like, the pictures on one shelf, the word forms on another, the toys on a third. Parul stopped her. "Don't sort them by what you show the person," she said. "Sort them by what you ask the person to do." That piece of advice is, almost word for word, the most influential classification of projective techniques ever proposed. This section explains why it works, sets out its five families, shows you how to place any technique in its family, and then looks at the older scheme it replaced, at a simpler three-way grouping, and at one past-paper item whose answer key got the classification wrong.

Why classify, and by what?

Section 10.1 showed what every projective technique has in common: an ambiguous stimulus, a free response, a purpose the person cannot easily see through, and an interpretation that aims at the whole personality. Beyond that shared core the techniques are astonishingly varied. A response to an inkblot may be four words long; a story told to a picture may run to three hundred; a drawing may contain no words at all; and a person sorting photographs makes no response except a choice. These differences are not cosmetic. They decide what kind of material the technique produces (a percept, a narrative, a finished sentence, a ranking, or a product together with the way it was made), how much language and literacy it demands, how easily it can be scored, and therefore how much evidence can ever be collected about its reliability and validity. A good classification gathers together the techniques that make the same kind of demand, so that once you know a technique's family you can predict a great deal about it before you have seen it, in the way that a botanist who knows a plant's family can predict the shape of its flowers before they open.

The difficulty is choosing what to classify by. The obvious basis is the stimulus: inkblots, pictures, words, toys. It fails at once, because the same stimulus can be used to ask for quite different things. Show a person a set of inkblots and ask "What might this be?", and you have the Rorschach; print four possible answers under each of the same blots and ask which fits best, and you have a multiple-choice inkblot test, in which the person invents nothing at all. Show a picture and ask for a story, and the person must construct a plot; show four pictures and ask which is liked most, and the person only chooses. Pictures appear in techniques as different as the Thematic Apperception Test, the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study and the Szondi Test, and words appear both in word association and in sentence completion. A shelf arranged by stimulus would put very different tasks side by side and split up techniques that ask for exactly the same thing.

The classification that solved the problem came from Gardner Lindzey (1920 to 2008), an American psychologist who took his doctorate at Harvard in 1949. Most students know Lindzey as an author rather than as a test specialist: with Calvin S. Hall he wrote Theories of Personality (1957), for decades the standard textbook of personality theories, and he edited the Handbook of Social Psychology. He went on to chair the psychology department of the University of Texas, was president of the American Psychological Association in 1967, and from 1975 to 1989 directed the Center for Advanced Study in the Behavioral Sciences at Stanford. In March 1959 he published a paper in Psychological Bulletin with the plain title "On the classification of projective techniques". He began by noting that there are many different ways of classifying projective tests, for example by the attributes of the test material itself, by the way the test was constructed, or by the kind of response the test asks the person to make. He argued that the last of these was the most useful, because it is the response, not the material, that defines what a technique really asks of a person and what the examiner then has to interpret. He developed the scheme in his book Projective Techniques and Cross-Cultural Research (1961), and it has passed, with small variations of wording, into almost every assessment textbook since.

Remember

Lindzey's principle (1959): classify a projective technique by the response it asks for, not by the stimulus it shows. Ask "What is the person asked to do?", and the family follows.

Exam trap

The five families (association, construction, completion, choice or ordering, expression) are Gardner Lindzey's, 1959. Lawrence K. Frank coined the phrase "projective methods" in 1939 and proposed a different five-part scheme (constitutive, constructive, interpretive, cathartic, refractive; Section 10.2.4). Some answer explanations credit the five response families to Frank, or to Bruno Klopfer; both attributions are wrong. If an item asks who classified projective techniques by the type of response they require, the answer is Lindzey.

The five families

Lindzey's five families are named by the response. A convenient way to hold them in memory is by five verbs. The person may be asked to associate (say what the stimulus calls up), to construct (make up a story or a similar product), to complete (finish something left unfinished), to choose (pick or rank among alternatives), or to express (make something freely while the examiner watches how it is made). Figure 10.4 lays the families out with their best-known members, and the paragraphs below take them one at a time.

Association techniques ask the person to respond to a stimulus with the first word, image or percept it calls to mind. In the words of Lindzey and his colleague Joseph S. Thorpe, writing in the International Encyclopedia of the Social Sciences (1968), these are the instruments that "minimize ideation and emphasize immediacy of response": the person is not asked to think, plan or elaborate, only to report what comes. Because the response is immediate, its formal properties carry as much weight as its content. In word association the time taken to answer is itself data, and in the Rorschach the examiner cares less about what the person saw than about which part of the blot was used and which feature of the blot (its shape, its colour, its shading, an impression of movement) produced the percept. The family's founding members are the Rorschach Inkblot Test (Hermann Rorschach, 1921; Sections 10.3 to 10.5) and the word association test in its several forms (Francis Galton's experiments of 1879, Carl Gustav Jung's diagnostic association studies and the Kent-Rosanoff list of 1910; Section 10.8). Two techniques that appeared after Lindzey's paper have been slotted in beside them by later authors: the Holtzman Inkblot Technique (Wayne Holtzman, 1961; Section 10.5) and, in most lists, the Hand Test (Edwin Wagner, 1962; Section 10.9), in which the person says what a pictured hand might be doing.

Construction techniques require the person to go beyond simple association and create a more elaborate product, almost always a story. The stimulus is usually a picture of people or animals in a scene whose meaning is left open, and the person must supply everything the picture does not: who the characters are, what led up to the moment shown, what they are thinking and feeling, and how it all ends. What the examiner reads is chiefly the content of the finished product: its themes, the wants of its characters and the obstacles they meet, and the way the story turns out. The prototype is the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT; Christiana Morgan and Henry Murray, 1935; Section 10.6), and its relatives all belong here: the Children's Apperception Test, the Senior Apperception Technique, the Roberts Apperception Test for Children, the Blacky Pictures, and the Make-A-Picture-Story test, in which the person first places cut-out figures on a background scene and then tells a story about it (Section 10.7).

Completion techniques present some kind of incomplete product and ask the person to finish it in any way he or she wishes. The unfinished part may be a sentence ("When I am alone, I ..."), the opening of a story, or a cartoon in which one character's reply is missing. Completion is more structured than association or construction, because the part supplied steers the response towards a topic (the family, fears, ambitions, a frustration) while leaving its direction open. The members are the sentence completion tests, such as the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (Julian Rotter and Janet Rafferty, 1950); story completion methods for children; and the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Saul Rosenzweig, 1945), whose cartoon frames each show one person frustrating another and leave an empty speech balloon for the frustrated person's reply (all three in Section 10.8). Notice that the P-F Study uses pictures and still belongs here, because what the person does is complete the frame.

Choice or ordering techniques ask the person to choose from a number of alternatives, or to arrange them in order, according to some criterion: which is correct, which is most relevant, which is most attractive, which is liked or disliked. The person adds nothing of his or her own. In the Szondi Test (Leopold Szondi, 1947) the person picks, from sets of photographs of faces, the ones he or she likes most and dislikes most; in the Tomkins-Horn Picture Arrangement Test (Silvan Tomkins and Daniel Horn) the person arranges sets of sketches into the order that makes the most sense (both in Section 10.9); and multiple-choice versions of the Rorschach and the TAT, which Lindzey and Thorpe also placed here, turn association and construction tasks into choices. Because the response is restricted to the alternatives on offer, this is the least projective of the five families and the easiest to score objectively, and some writers treat it as a step from projective towards objective testing.

Expression techniques, often called expressive techniques, ask the person to produce something freely through a medium (a drawing, a painting, play with toys, a dramatic role, handwriting), and they differ from construction techniques in one crucial respect: they place as much emphasis on the manner and style of production as on the finished product. Two children may both draw a house. One presses so hard that the paper tears, rubs out the door three times and leaves the drawing tiny in a corner of the sheet; the other fills the page with quick, confident lines. For an expressive technique those differences are the data. The members are the projective drawings (Karen Machover's Draw-a-Person, 1949; John Buck's House-Tree-Person, 1948; Robert Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman's Kinetic Family Drawing, 1970), finger painting, play techniques with dolls and toys, psychodrama, handwriting, and the Bender-Gestalt designs when they are copied and read for personality rather than for brain function (all in Section 10.9).

Step back and look at the five families together, and Parul's advice to Naina makes sense. Pictures turn up in four of them: in association (the hands of the Hand Test), construction (the TAT), completion (the P-F Study) and choice (the Szondi photographs). Words turn up in two: association (word association) and completion (sentence completion). Only the response sorts the techniques cleanly. The two families most often confused are construction and expression, because both ask the person to make something. The way to tell them apart is to ask what the examiner reads. In a construction technique it is the content of the finished story; in an expressive technique it is as much the way the thing was made as what it shows.

Exam trap

TAT = construction (RML 2022). The person builds a new and elaborate product, a story with a past, a present and a future. It is not an association technique (that asks for a first word or percept, as in the Rorschach), not a completion technique (that finishes a supplied stem or frame, as in sentence completion or the P-F Study), and not an expressive technique (that studies the manner of a free production, as in drawing or play). Rorschach = association (RML 2022): of the options sentence completion, Rorschach, TAT and Draw-a-Person, only the Rorschach asks for the immediate percept; the other three are completion, construction and expression, in that order. The P-F Study uses pictures but is completion; the Szondi uses pictures but is choice.

Sorting a technique yourself

A classification is only useful if you can apply it to a technique you have never met. When Naina had finished the cupboard, Parul tested her with six descriptions, some of real techniques and some invented for the purpose. The method Naina learned is to ask a short series of questions about the response, in this order, and to stop at the first "yes":

  1. Is the person asked only to pick, sort or rank alternatives that the examiner supplies? If so, the technique is choice or ordering.
  2. Is the person given something unfinished (a sentence stem, the opening of a story, an empty speech balloon) to finish? If so, it is completion.
  3. Is the person asked to make something freely, with the way it is made observed as closely as the result? If so, it is expression.
  4. Is the person asked to create an elaborate product, usually a story, read mainly for its content? If so, it is construction.
  5. Is the person asked for the first word, image or percept that the stimulus calls up? If so, it is association.

The order matters only for borderline cases. Asking about choice and completion first stops a picture stimulus from misleading you, and asking about expression before construction forces you to decide what the examiner actually reads. Here are Parul's six cases, worked one at a time.

Case 1. A child is shown a photograph of a vague smudge of smoke against the sky and asked, "What could this be?" No alternatives are supplied, nothing is left unfinished, nothing is made, and no story is asked for: the child reports what the smudge calls up. Association.

Case 2. A college student is shown a drawing of a woman standing at a half-open door with her back to the viewer and is asked to tell a story with a beginning, a middle and an end. The student must invent characters, a history and an outcome, and the examiner will read the story's themes. Construction.

Case 3. A printed form contains forty lines such as "My father ..." and "What I fear most is ...", and the person writes the rest of each line. Every line is an incomplete product to be finished. Completion.

Case 4. A man is given twelve cards showing faces and asked to lay them out in a row from the face he likes most to the face he likes least. He creates nothing; he orders what he is given. Choice or ordering.

Case 5. A six-year-old is given a lump of clay and told to make anything she likes, while the examiner notes how she handles it: whether she pounds it, pinches off tiny pieces, hesitates for a long time, or smears it across the table. The manner is studied as closely as the product. Expression.

Case 6. A cartoon (invented for this example, not taken from any published test) shows a bus conductor handing back a torn ten-rupee note and saying, "I can't take this one; you'll have to pay some other way." The passenger's speech balloon is empty, and the person is asked to write what the passenger says. It is a picture, but the task is to finish the frame. Completion, and in fact exactly the format of the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study.

Real techniques are not always so obliging, and four borderline cases are worth knowing. A figure drawing is sometimes listed under construction, because the person makes a product; but Lindzey and Thorpe placed drawing and painting, including the human-figure drawings of Buck and Machover, under expression, because the interpreter attends to size, pressure, erasures and placement as much as to what is drawn. Margaret Lowenfeld's World Technique, in which a child builds a scene in a sand tray with miniature toys, is placed under construction in some books and under expression in others, because both the finished scene and the way it was built are studied. The Make-A-Picture-Story test involves arranging figures, which can look like ordering, but the arrangement is only a step towards a story, so it is construction. And word association is association, not completion: the person is not finishing anything, only answering a single word with the first word that comes. Table 10.4 sorts every technique in this chapter into its family.

Remember

Five verbs, five families, five anchor tests: Associate (Rorschach), Construct (TAT), Complete (sentence completion), Choose (Szondi), Express (Draw-a-Person). If you know what the person does, you know the family.

Clinical link

A technique's family tells a clinician what it can ask of a particular client. Written completion techniques need literacy and comfort with the language of the stems, so they suit a college student better than a farm labourer with little schooling. Expression techniques need almost no language, which is why drawings and play are the usual first tools with young children and with people who cannot or will not talk; but they need a working hand, and they tell you little that is specific. Association and construction techniques sit in between: they need speech but not reading. Choosing a family before choosing a particular test is part of fitting the assessment to the person in front of you.

Exam trap

Borderline placements to know: Draw-a-Person, House-Tree-Person and Kinetic Family Drawing are expression (Lindzey placed figure drawing there), even though a product is made. MAPS is construction, even though figures are arranged. Word association is association, not completion.
