The Lowenfeld World Technique is construction in some books and expression in others: if an option list offers only one of the two, choose that one.

Frank's earlier scheme, and other ways of grouping

Lindzey's was not the first classification. Twenty years earlier, in the 1939 paper in which he named the field (Section 10.1), Lawrence K. Frank had also sorted the methods he was describing. Frank grouped them by what the person does with the material, which in practice meant the psychological process he thought each method set in motion. He proposed five categories:

  • Constitutive methods, in which the person imposes a structure or form on material that has little or none of its own: clay, finger paint, or an inkblot, which means nothing until the person makes it mean something.
  • Constructive methods, in which the person arranges or builds given materials of definite shape into larger constructions, as in building a scene from toys or blocks, laying out mosaic pieces, or drawing a figure on instruction.
  • Interpretive methods, in which the person says what a situation or a picture means to him or her; the TAT, in which a picture must be interpreted as a story, is the standard example.
  • Cathartic methods, in which the person discharges feeling and acts out tension, as in free play with dolls and toys; the emotional release itself is what is studied.
  • Refractive methods, in which personality shows itself through the idiosyncratic way the person bends a conventional medium of communication, as light is bent passing through glass. Handwriting, the material of graphology, was the model case.
Table 10.5 Frank's five categories (1939), with where Lindzey's scheme would put the same examples.
Frank's categoryWhat the person doesExampleLindzey's family for that example
ConstitutiveGives form to unformed materialClay, finger paint, an inkblotExpression (clay, paint); association (inkblot)
ConstructiveBuilds given materials into larger constructionsBlocks, mosaics, a scene of toys, a directed drawingExpression, or construction for scenes
InterpretiveSays what a situation meansThe TATConstruction
CatharticDischarges feeling, acts out tensionFree play with dolls and toysExpression
RefractiveBends a conventional medium in a personal wayHandwritingExpression

Frank's scheme was a brave first attempt, but it was never widely adopted, and its weakness is easy to see in Table 10.5. Its categories are defined by an inner process that the examiner cannot observe directly, so the same technique can be put into several boxes depending on what you believe is happening inside the person. A figure drawing is constitutive if the person is given a blank sheet and a free hand, constructive if told to draw a house, refractive if the examiner studies the line quality, and cathartic if a child scribbles furiously to get rid of anger. The Rorschach is constitutive (the person gives form to an unformed blot) and also interpretive (the person says what the blot means). Notice, too, the last column of the table: three of Frank's five categories collapse into a single Lindzey family, expression. Lindzey's families, defined by the observable task, overlap far less; most techniques fall into one family, and the few that sit on a border can be named, as Section 10.2.3 did. That is the main reason Lindzey's scheme replaced Frank's.

Several Indian textbooks, among them A. K. Singh's widely used text on tests and measurement, add a simpler practical grouping into three kinds. In pictorial techniques the stimulus is a vague or ambiguous picture or blot: the Rorschach, the Holtzman, the TAT, the CAT and the P-F Study. In verbal techniques the stimulus is presented in words: word association and sentence completion. In expressive techniques the person reveals personality through a task done with materials, and the manner of doing it is the basis for appraisal: drawing, painting, play, role play and handwriting. This grouping is by stimulus and medium rather than by response, so it lacks the logic of Lindzey's, but it answers one practical question well: can this person take this test? A client who cannot read can take a pictorial or an expressive technique but not a written verbal one. The order of this chapter is itself a loose grouping of this kind: inkblots in Sections 10.3 to 10.5, pictures in 10.6 and 10.7, words in 10.8, and drawings, play and choices in 10.9.

Exam trap

Do not mix the two five-part schemes. Frank's constructive means building or arranging given materials (toys, blocks, mosaics, a directed drawing); Lindzey's construction means making up a story or a similar product. So the TAT is interpretive for Frank but construction for Lindzey. Frank's model of the refractive category is handwriting, and his cathartic category is play. If an option list mixes terms from the two schemes, check which author the stem names before answering, and remember that "association, construction, completion, choice or ordering, expression" belongs to Lindzey alone.

Is word association projective?

One technique deserves a closer look, because its status is sometimes doubted: word association. Put it beside the Rorschach, the TAT and sentence completion and ask which of the four is not projective, and a surprising number of students pick word association. By the standard classification all four are projective, and it is worth seeing exactly why, because the same reasoning settles any question of this kind.

Start with Lindzey. The word association test is not a borderline member of the association family; it is one of its two founding members, named beside the Rorschach in Lindzey's own account of the family. Then take history. Francis Galton's word association experiments of 1879, in which he timed his own associations to a list of words and was struck by how many of them reached back into his boyhood, are usually named as the first projective technique of modern psychology (Book 1, Section 1.3.4, tells Galton's story; Section 10.8 tells this one). From 1904 Carl Gustav Jung and his colleagues at the Burghölzli hospital in Zurich turned word association into a diagnostic tool, reading long reaction times, odd responses and failures to respond as signs of emotionally charged "complexes" (Book 2, Section 3.8.2, describes how Jung used it in therapy). So central was word association to the early projective tradition that Hermann Rorschach, describing his inkblot method, called it a "perceptual-diagnostic experiment", a phrase that echoes the title of Jung's Diagnostic Association Studies (Section 10.3.2), and compared some of his responses to the "complex" associations of Jung's experiments. Finally, check the defining features of Section 10.1 one at a time. A single stimulus word has no single correct response; the person may answer with any word at all; the purpose is disguised, since the person does not know that a slow or strange answer will be read as a sign of conflict; and the answers are interpreted for what they reveal about the person's preoccupations. Word association has every defining feature of a projective technique.

Why, then, is it ever doubted? There are several understandable confusions, none of which survives a closer look. First, many students picture projective tests as pictures and blots, and a list of ordinary words does not look ambiguous; but the ambiguity of a projective technique lies in the openness of the response, not only in the stimulus. Second, the Kent-Rosanoff list of 1910 was designed as a psychiatric screening device and was scored by comparing each response with how often a thousand people had given it, which looks more like psychometric counting than like projection; but scoring a projective technique against frequency norms no more makes it an objective test than the Rorschach's tables of common percepts make the Rorschach one. Third, the name is shared with a quite different test. The Controlled Oral Word Association Test associated with Arthur Lester Benton (1909 to 2006), in which the person says as many words as possible beginning with a given letter within a time limit, is a verbal fluency test used in neuropsychology (Chapter 11), and it really is not projective. Fourth, "free association", the fundamental rule of psychoanalysis, in which a patient says whatever comes to mind, is a method of therapy and not a test at all (Book 2). Keep these apart and word association takes its place where Lindzey put it, beside the Rorschach.

Exam trap

RML 2021: "Which is not a projective test?" Options: word association, Rorschach, TAT, sentence completion. The paper keyed word association. The defensible answer is that all four are projective: word association is Lindzey's association technique and, with Galton (1879) and Jung, the classic early projective method. Learn it as projective. If this exact item reappears with these exact options, mark word association, only because that is the paper's known key. If an item of this kind includes a genuine objective inventory (MMPI-2, 16PF, NEO-PI-R, EPQ, MBTI, MCMI-III), that inventory is the answer (Section 10.1). And an option reading "Controlled Oral Word Association" names a fluency test, which is not projective.

10.3 The Rorschach I: Hermann Rorschach, the Ten Cards and How the Test Is GivenHigh yield · 10 Qs

Klecksography, the art of making pictures out of inkblots, was a popular pastime in the nineteenth century: you dropped ink on a sheet of paper, folded it, opened it, and looked for creatures, faces and landscapes in the mirror-image stain. One schoolboy in the Swiss town of Schaffhausen even went by the game's name: his school fraternity called him Klex, "blot", most likely for his quick skill with pen and ink. Some twenty years later that boy, by then the deputy director of a psychiatric hospital in eastern Switzerland, published a book of 174 pages and ten printed blots, arguing that the way a person looks at an accidental form reveals how that person's mind works. He died the next year, at 37, before he could see what his blots would become: one of the most famous, most widely used, most fiercely attacked and most fiercely defended psychological tests ever made. This section tells his story, describes the ten cards as far as test security allows, and then follows an examiner through the three phases of giving the test. How the responses are coded is Section 10.4; how a whole record is read, and the scoring systems that grew up after Rorschach's death, are Section 10.5.

Blots before Rorschach

The idea that a shapeless stain can set the imagination working is far older than psychology. In his notes on painting, Leonardo da Vinci advised artists who were short of ideas to look at walls stained with damp, or at stones of mixed colours, where they would find landscapes, battles and strange faces waiting to be seen. In the nineteenth century klecksography (from the German Klecks, a blot) turned the same idea into a parlour game. Its best-known practitioner was Justinus Kerner (1786 to 1862), a German physician and poet who, as his eyesight failed, began making blots from the ink that fell on his paper, worked them up into small pictures and wrote a poem for each. His collection, the Klecksographien, put together in the 1850s, was published only in 1890, long after his death. It has often been suggested that Kerner inspired Rorschach, but the evidence points the other way: years after building his test, Rorschach wrote that he had heard of Kerner's experiments and would be grateful if someone could find him the book.

Psychologists soon saw a use for the game. In 1895 Alfred Binet (1857 to 1911), who would later produce the first practical intelligence test (Chapter 6), and his collaborator Victor Henri (1872 to 1940) included inkblots in their programme for an "individual psychology", as a way of studying a person's imagination, and over the next two decades several investigators in Europe and America used blots for the same purpose. In 1917 a Polish medical student in Zurich, Szymon Hens, completed a doctoral thesis in which he gave eight blots to schoolchildren, healthy adults and psychiatric patients in order to test their fantasy. What all of this work shared was its question. It treated the blot as a stimulus to imagination and asked what people saw, and how much: the richness, the originality or simply the number of their ideas. That question, as it turned out, led nowhere in particular. Rorschach's achievement was to ask a different one.

Exam trap

Rorschach did not invent inkblots, or even their use in psychology: Kerner made klecksographs in the 1850s, and Binet and Henri proposed blots for studying imagination in 1895. What Rorschach invented, in 1921, was the inkblot test: a fixed, standard series of ten blots scored chiefly for how a person perceives rather than what is seen. The usual "first projective technique" in exam lists is not an inkblot test at all but Galton's word association (1879).

Hermann Rorschach and the form interpretation experiment

Hermann Rorschach was born in Zurich on 8 November 1884, the eldest surviving child of Ulrich Rorschach (1853 to 1903), a painter who earned his living as a drawing and painting teacher in Schaffhausen. The boy grew up drawing, and at school in Schaffhausen his fraternity brothers nicknamed him "Klex". The name is usually told as a sign of an early fondness for klecksography, but his biographer Damion Searls (The Inkblots, 2017) points out that in the students' slang it praised a boy who was quick and skilful with pen and ink. A second famous story also needs care. Later accounts say that, torn between art and science, the young man wrote to the German biologist Ernst Haeckel (1834 to 1919) and was advised to choose science; the one letter Searls found, written at seventeen, asked something more practical, whether a freethinker should become a teacher when the post meant teaching religion. His father died in 1903, and watching that illness, Searls suggests, made Hermann want to become a doctor. He chose medicine, studying at Zurich, Berne and Berlin, and graduated in 1909. At Zurich the great influence on him was Eugen Bleuler (1857 to 1939), the director of the Burghölzli hospital, who had coined the word "schizophrenia" (Book 3) and in whose hospital Carl Jung had carried out his word association research. Rorschach's doctoral dissertation, on "reflex hallucinations" (hallucinations in one sense set off by real stimulation of another), was completed under Bleuler in 1912.

His working life was spent in psychiatric hospitals. From about 1909 to 1913 he worked at the cantonal asylum of Münsterlingen on Lake Constance, and it was there, in 1911, that he first experimented with inkblots: with a close friend, Konrad Gehring, who taught in the next village, he showed freshly made blots to patients and to Gehring's pupils, aged about 12 to 15, and some of these early blots, with the answers written on them, survive in the Rorschach archive in Berne. He married a Russian physician, Olga Stempelin, and at the end of 1913 the couple moved to Russia, where he worked in a private clinic near Moscow; in June 1914 he returned to Switzerland, and Olga, who stayed on in Russia after the First World War broke out, followed in 1915. He then spent about a year at the Waldau clinic near Berne, absorbed in research on Swiss religious sects and their founders, and in 1915 he became deputy director of the cantonal psychiatric hospital at Herisau, in the canton of Appenzell, where he stayed for the rest of his short life.

The spur to return to the blots was Hens's thesis of 1917. Hens had concentrated on what people saw; Rorschach became convinced that the more revealing question was how they saw it. Within little more than a year he built a series of blots, tried them on patients and on healthy people, kept those that worked and threw the rest away. A draft of August 1918, titled (in Searls's translation) Investigations of Perception and Apprehension in the Healthy and the Ill, already described the final ten blots in their final order, and in November 1919 he presented the experiment to the Swiss psychiatric association. His friend Walter Morgenthaler (1882 to 1965), a senior psychiatrist at the Waldau who edited a series of books on applied psychiatry for the Berne publisher Ernst Bircher, proposed publishing it in that series. Even the title became a negotiation. Rorschach wanted the plain description "methodology and results of a perceptual-diagnostic experiment", a phrase that echoes the title of Jung's Diagnostic Association Studies. Morgenthaler thought that too modest, since the blots were "more than a mere experiment", and pressed for Psychodiagnostik, "psychodiagnostics", a word Rorschach thought went "too far" and sounded "extremely arrogant". Morgenthaler won the main title and Rorschach kept his description in the subtitle. The book appeared in 1921 as Psychodiagnostik: Methodik und Ergebnisse eines wahrnehmungsdiagnostischen Experiments (Deutenlassen von Zufallsformen), "Psychodiagnostics: methodology and results of a perceptual-diagnostic experiment (eliciting interpretations of accidental forms)", with the ten plates issued alongside it as test material. Rorschach himself usually called his method the form interpretation experiment (Formdeutversuch).

Turning hand-made blots into printed plates was a struggle of its own, and two stories about it need care. The first is about the number. He had made and discarded dozens of blots along the way, and an often-repeated account, which goes back to the psychiatrist and historian Henri Ellenberger (1905 to 1993), whose study of Rorschach's life published in 1954 was for decades the classic source, says that his manuscript had fifteen plates and that the publisher accepted only ten. Searls, working from Rorschach's letters, calls that claim false: the 1918 draft and Rorschach's letters already speak of ten cards, so the number ten was his own choice, not a printer's cut. The second story is about shading. According to Ellenberger, the dark areas of the hand-made originals were a uniform black or grey, and it was only in the printed plates, through the printer's imperfect work, that gradations of shading appeared; when Rorschach saw the proofs he realised the possibilities that the new nuances offered, and shading later became one of the determinants scored in every system (Section 10.4). Searls, by contrast, shows Rorschach correcting the proofs closely and insisting that they reproduce "the tiniest dissolvings of the paint and the ink", so treat the shading story as Ellenberger's account rather than settled fact. The book itself attracted little attention. On 2 April 1922 Rorschach died at Herisau of peritonitis, probably following a ruptured appendix. He was 37, and Psychodiagnostik had been in print for less than a year.

What survived him was a method whose scoring he had not finished working out, and which had no owner. His last paper, a lecture on applying the experiment in psychoanalysis, was edited and published after his death, in 1923, by his friend and colleague Emil Oberholzer. When Bircher went bankrupt in 1927, a former employee, Hans Huber, who had helped with the original printing of the plates, bought the rights and refounded the business as the Berne publisher Hans Huber (its successor, Hogrefe, publishes the cards today), and an English translation by Paul Lemkau and Bernard Kronenberg appeared only in 1942. By then the test had been taught in the United States for more than a decade, and several rival American schools of scoring had grown up around it. That story, and John Exner's attempt to unite the schools, belongs to Section 10.5.

Key study: Rorschach's form interpretation experiment (1921)

Question. Does the way a person perceives an accidental form differ systematically between healthy people and people with different mental illnesses? Method. Rorschach gave his ten plates to 405 respondents, 117 without and 288 with a psychiatric diagnosis, and examined every record with four questions, in a deliberate order: how many responses were given, how quickly, and how often a plate was refused; whether each response was determined by form alone or also by movement or colour; whether the blot was grasped as a whole or in parts, and which parts; and only last, what was seen. Result. The formal features separated his groups. His intelligent healthy respondents, for example, gave a high proportion of well-seen forms, many whole responses and at least a few responses in which they saw movement. Colleagues, above all Emil Oberholzer, sent him the records of people he had never met, and he tried to diagnose them from the records alone, without being told their age, sex or illness. Conclusion. He called the method a "perceptual-diagnostic experiment" and argued that it could indicate a person's type of experience (introversive or extratensive; Section 10.5) and serve as an aid to differential diagnosis. Criticism. Rorschach himself wrote that his totals were "far too small", drawn largely from the patients of one hospital serving a rural canton; his groups were defined by clinical judgement; he reported no figures on reliability; and he described the test as "primarily a qualitative examination" (in the 1942 English translation). Almost every later argument about the test begins with these limitations.

Exam trap

Hermann Rorschach: a Swiss psychiatrist (not a psychologist, and not German or Austrian), 1884 to 1922, who published Psychodiagnostik in 1921 (not 1911, which is the year of Bleuler's book on schizophrenia) while working at Herisau, and died the following year aged 37. He trained under Eugen Bleuler. He left no complete scoring system: the Comprehensive System is John Exner's (1974), not Rorschach's.

What the Rorschach is designed to assess

What exactly did Rorschach think his blots measured, and what do clinicians use them for today? The answer has shifted over a century, and the shifts explain several common confusions. Rorschach thought of his method as an experiment in perception. In the theoretical part of his book he borrowed Bleuler's account of perception as a sensation joined to memories by association, and argued that interpreting a blot is a perception with one difference: the fit between the blot and anything the person remembers is so loose that the person feels the effort of fitting them together, and it is this awareness of effort that gives the perception the character of an interpretation. (Many respondents, he added, feel no such effort and simply name what they see.) How a person makes that effort, carefully or carelessly, using the whole blot or picking at its corners, letting its colour or an impression of movement shape the percept, struck him as a window on how that person's mind works in general. Notice what is missing from this account. Rorschach did not describe his method as a key to the unconscious, and the content of a response was the last thing he considered. The projective reading, in which the percept is a screen onto which a person throws needs and conflicts, was added after his death, when Lawrence Frank placed the Rorschach at the centre of his 1939 account of "projective methods" (Section 10.1).

Modern users hold both readings at once. Treated as a problem-solving task, the Rorschach samples how a person handles an ambiguous situation with little guidance; treated as a projective technique, it gives the person room to reveal concerns that he or she would not, or could not, report directly. Put together, the purposes for which the test is given are these:

  • Perception and reality testing: how accurately the person's percepts fit the blots, which Section 10.4 codes as form quality.
  • Thought processes and thought disorder: whether the person's explanations of what was seen are logical or show the slips and strange combinations of disordered thinking. Rorschach's own clinical interest centred on schizophrenia, which he described as the commonest diagnosis in the hospitals of his day.
  • Emotional functioning: how the person responds to colour and shading, read as signs of how feelings are handled (Section 10.5).
  • Self-perception and relationships: how often human figures are seen, and how they are seen.
  • Personality structure and coping style: the overall balance of the record, including Rorschach's own "experience type" (Section 10.5).
  • Help in diagnosis: Rorschach found, without having looked for it, that his records helped to tell apart normal, neurotic, schizophrenic and organically ill respondents, and the test is still used as one source of evidence in diagnosing psychopathology, especially psychosis.
  • Unconscious processes: in the projective tradition, unconscious needs, conflicts and intentions.

Just as important is what the Rorschach is not. It yields no IQ. Rorschach linked some features of a record, such as well-seen forms and whole responses, to a clinical impression of intelligence, but no system turns a Rorschach record into an intelligence quotient (Section 10.4 makes the same point about the Comprehensive System). It is not a test of memory, of physical illness or of brain structure, and it is not a way of analysing dreams. Table 10.6 sorts the commonest claims about the test into true and false. Section 10.10 shows that the strongest evidence today supports exactly the uses Rorschach began with, the perceptual and thinking variables, and that much weaker evidence supports many of the others.

Table 10.6 Common claims about the Rorschach, sorted into true and false.
ClaimVerdictWhy
Uses inkblots to assess personalityTrueTen symmetrical inkblots; the only inkblot test among the usual options
Assesses thought processes; helps detect thought disorderTrueRorschach's own clinical aim; coded through form quality and special scores (10.4)
Assesses cognition and personality; helps identify mental illnessTrueRorschach saw it as an aid to differential diagnosis
Reveals unconscious intentions, needs and conflictsTrue (projective view)The reading added by the projective tradition after 1939
Interprets dreamsFalseDream analysis is a psychoanalytic method, not a test
Records conscious desiresFalseConscious wishes are simply asked about, in an interview or inventory
Measures brain size; diagnoses physical illnessFalseIt is a psychological test, not a medical or anatomical one
Gives an IQ; tests memory recallFalseNo IQ score; intelligence and memory scales do that (Chapters 6 and 11)
Employs 12 inkblotsFalseTen
Exam trap

"Which test uses inkblots to assess personality, or thought processes?" Always the Rorschach (RML 2020, IHBAS 2024). The distractors use no inkblots: the MMPI is a true-false self-report inventory (Starke Hathaway and J. Charnley McKinley, 1943), the MBTI is a forced-choice self-report questionnaire that sorts people into Jungian "types", and the TAT uses pictures of people in ambiguous scenes (Chapter 9 and Section 10.6). Do not let the word "types" in a stem pull you to the MBTI; the question is about the stimulus. "What is the Rorschach designed to measure?" (RML 2021): unconscious intentions, not dreams, conscious desires or brain size. "Main purpose?" (RML 2025): to measure thought disorder and identify mental illness through the perception of inkblots, not to diagnose physical illness, assess IQ or test memory. In the RML 2022 "EXCEPT" item, "introduced in 1921 by Hermann Rorschach", "a projective method" and "assesses cognition and personality and helps diagnose psychological conditions" are all true; the false statement is "12 inkblots".

The ten cards

Rorschach described how to make a blot in two sentences: drop a few large blots of ink on a sheet of paper, fold the paper, and spread the ink between the two halves of the sheet. Figure 10.5 shows the process with a blot invented for this book. The hard part, he warned, was not making blots but choosing them. A usable blot must be fairly simple, because complicated pictures make the scoring too difficult. Its parts must be arranged with a certain "spatial rhythm", or it lacks a picture-like quality and people dismiss it as "simply an inkblot". And each blot, and the series as a whole, must be thoroughly tried out before it can serve as a test. Folding makes the blots bilaterally symmetrical, the left half a mirror image of the right, and Rorschach weighed the symmetry carefully. It has one disadvantage: it makes interpretations somewhat stereotyped. Against that, it creates the same conditions for right-handed and left-handed people, it helps inhibited and blocked respondents to begin, and it encourages people to see whole scenes. Asymmetrical blots, he found, were rejected by many people.

The finished test, the Rorschach Inkblot Test (also called the Rorschach technique or, in some papers, Rorschach Psychodiagnostics), consists of ten cards, each a stiff white card about 18 by 24 centimetres with one symmetrical blot printed on it. The cards are numbered with Roman numerals, I to X, and are always given in that fixed order, an order Rorschach settled on the basis of experience with how people responded. They differ above all in colour. Five cards are achromatic cards: their blots are printed in black and shades of grey only, without any hue (Cards I, IV, V, VI and VII). The other five are chromatic cards, which means they contain colour in the strict sense, a hue such as red, green or blue; but they contain it in two different ways. Two cards (II and III) are black and grey with patches of bright red. Three cards (VIII, IX and X) are fully multicoloured, printed in several pastel colours. Table 10.7 sets the ten out in order.

Table 10.7 The colour composition of the ten Rorschach cards (colour only; the blots themselves are not described, for test security).
CardColour on the cardChromatic colour present?Group
IBlack and greyNoAchromatic
IIBlack and grey with redYes (red)Black and red
IIIBlack and grey with redYes (red)Black and red
IVBlack and greyNoAchromatic
VBlack and greyNoAchromatic
VIBlack and greyNoAchromatic
VIIBlack and greyNoAchromatic
VIIISeveral pastel coloursYesMulticoloured
IXSeveral pastel coloursYesMulticoloured
XSeveral pastel coloursYesMulticoloured
TotalTen cards5 yes, 5 no5 achromatic, 2 black and red, 3 multicoloured

Read the table as a sequence and it is easy to remember. The series opens with a black card, brings in red on the next two, returns to black and grey for four cards in the middle, and ends with three cards in full colour. So the person meets colour early (Card II), loses it for the long middle of the test, and meets it again, far more richly, at Card VIII, the first fully coloured card. Clinicians watch those two moments of arrival closely, for reasons Section 10.3.7 explains. And the arithmetic is simple: 5 achromatic plus 2 black and red plus 3 multicoloured makes 10, and 2 plus 3 gives the 5 cards that carry colour.

That is as much as this book will say about the cards, and the restraint is deliberate. The real plates are copyrighted test material, and a person who has studied them, and the answers commonly given to them, before being tested can produce a record that no longer means what it would otherwise mean. When the ten plates and lists of common responses were posted on Wikipedia in 2009, many psychologists protested for exactly that reason; Book 2, Section 1.9.6, tells the story and sets out the ethics of test security. Every blot drawn in this chapter is therefore invented, and no card is described beyond its number and its colours, which are standard textbook facts.

Exam trap

Ten cards, by Hermann Rorschach, 1921: not 12 (RML 2022), and not "20 cards by Henry Murray" (20 is the number of TAT cards classically given to one person), "15 by John Exner" (Exner wrote the Comprehensive System for scoring the same ten cards) or "5 by Carl Jung" (Jung's test was word association). There is no blank card: "10 inkblots and 1 blank card" borrows the blank card 16 of the TAT. How many are coloured? Five (RML 2022): two black and red plus three multicoloured; "3" counts only the multicoloured cards, and "10" and "4" are simply wrong. So "5 chromatic and 5 achromatic" is right (Amity 2023), and "3 chromatic and 7 achromatic" is the trap of forgetting that the red of Cards II and III is a hue; "3 achromatic and 7 chromatic" turns the numbers round and is wrong twice over, since five cards (I, IV, V, VI and VII) have no hue at all. RML 2025 keyed "five black and white and five multi-coloured cards". The five-five split is right and it is the only correct option ("only black and white", "only coloured" and "ten multicoloured" are all false), but its wording is loose: the achromatic cards are black and grey, and strictly only three cards (VIII, IX, X) are multicoloured, while two (II, III) are black and red. Choose it, and know the exact composition: 5 achromatic, 2 black and red, 3 multicoloured.

Giving the test: the setting and the response phase

A Rorschach record is only as good as the way it was collected, and the procedure is laid down precisely so that records collected by different examiners can be compared. Figure 10.6 shows the whole procedure in outline: a response phase, in which the person looks at all ten cards and says what they might be; an inquiry phase, in which the examiner goes back over every response to find out how it was seen; and, when it is needed, a final phase of testing the limits. Coding and interpretation come only afterwards. We follow an examiner through each phase in turn.

Materials and seating. The examiner needs the ten cards, stacked face down in order within the examiner's reach but out of the person's; a record form on which every response will be written; a location sheet, which carries small printed reproductions of the ten blots on which the examiner later outlines the part of the blot each response used; and, in the older systems, a stopwatch. The room is quiet, and the person is put at ease first. The Rorschach is usually given as one part of a larger assessment, after an interview has built some rapport, and the examiner explains in a sentence or two what is going to happen. In the Comprehensive System of John Exner (Section 10.5), examiner and client sit side by side rather than face to face. The point of the arrangement is to reduce the cues an examiner gives without meaning to, such as a nod, a raised eyebrow or a lean forward when an interesting answer arrives, cues to which a person anxious to "do well" is very sensitive.

The instruction. Rorschach's own instruction was a single question, "What might this be?", and the Comprehensive System keeps exactly that: the examiner hands over Card I and asks, "What might this be?", and nothing more. Some of the older schools used longer instructions; Bruno Klopfer and Douglas Kelley's The Rorschach Technique (1942), for example, told the person that people see all sorts of things in these inkblots and asked what this one might be for them, and what it made them think of. The brevity matters. Every added word (look carefully, use your imagination, find as many things as you can) changes what people do, and a short standard question keeps the situation the same for everyone. This first phase is often called the free association phase, and in some older textbooks the performance proper; "free" here means only that the responses are not restricted, and it has nothing to do with the free association of psychoanalysis.

Recording. Everything the person says is written down verbatim, word for word, with no paraphrase, because the exact words decide the coding: "a dancer" and "a dancer spinning round" will be coded differently. The examiner also notes the position in which the card was held for each response, using a small set of symbols (one common set uses ∧ for the card upright, v for upside down, and arrowhead marks for the card turned on one side or the other), together with any remarks, exclamations and gestures. In Rorschach's own procedure and in the older American systems the examiner also noted the reaction time, the seconds from handing over the card to the first response, and the total time spent on each card. The person may turn the card any way he or she likes, and if asked, the examiner says only something like "It's up to you". Other questions get the same non-directive treatment. "How many things should I find?" "Most people see more than one thing." "How long should I look?" "As long as you like." The examiner does not prompt, suggest, praise or criticise, and never says that an answer is right or wrong, because there is no right answer.

How many responses? The number of responses in a record, written R, varies enormously between people, and that creates a problem, because almost every later score is affected by it: a person who gives eight responses and a person who gives sixty are not taking quite the same test. The Comprehensive System therefore manages R. If the person gives only one response to Card I, the examiner encourages him or her, once, to look a little longer. If the person gives five responses to Card I, the examiner takes the card back and moves on to the next, and repeats this on the following cards for as long as the person keeps giving five. And if the finished record has fewer than 14 responses, it is treated as too short to interpret: the examiner explains this and goes through the cards again, asking for more responses. (The newer Rorschach Performance Assessment System manages R in a different way, with a "prompt" and a "pull"; Section 10.5 explains it.) A person who hands a card back saying there is nothing in it has made a card rejection. The examiner does not accept it at once but encourages the person to take more time, and records the rejection, which is itself an important observation.

A worked example. Shaurya is a 26-year-old accounts executive referred to the hospital's psychology department after months of worry and poor sleep, and Naina, now three months into her training, gives him the Rorschach under Parul's supervision. To keep the real cards secure, imagine that the invented blot of Figure 10.5 is one of the cards in his series. Naina hands it to him upright and asks, "What might this be?" After seven seconds he answers, and over the next two minutes he gives three responses, turning the card once. Table 10.8 shows Naina's record.

Table 10.8 Naina's response-phase record for Shaurya's responses to the invented blot of Figure 10.5 (invented example).
PositionTimeWhat Shaurya said and did, written verbatim
∧7 seconds to first response"A big rangoli pattern, the kind they draw at Diwali. It's the same on both sides."
∧"And these two up here are like the horns of a bull."
Asks, "Can I turn it?" E: "It's up to you." Turns the card over.
v"Now it's a dancer in a big flowing skirt, spinning round. This light bit in the middle is her face."
About 2 minutes on the cardHands the card back.

Notice what Naina did and did not do. She wrote every word, including "It's the same on both sides", which will matter at the inquiry. She noted the position in which each response was given, recorded Shaurya's question about turning the card, and answered it without guiding him. She did not ask what made the pattern look like a rangoli, or whether the dancer was moving, because those questions belong to the next phase. And she did not say "good". At this stage the record is raw material: it contains the responses, but not yet the information needed to code them, and collecting that information is what the inquiry is for.

Indian context

In Indian clinics the Rorschach is often given in Hindi, Kannada, Tamil, Bengali or whichever language the client is most at ease in, and a careful examiner keeps the instruction as short in translation as it is in the original (in Hindi, something like "Yeh kya ho sakta hai?"). Responses are written down verbatim in the language actually spoken, including words switched mid-sentence into English, and translated only for coding, because a word such as chamakta (shining) or mulayam (soft) may be exactly the key word that the inquiry needs to follow up. The cultural content of responses (a rangoli, a temple lamp, a festival mask) should be read against the client's own world, not against expectations formed elsewhere; Section 10.10 returns to the question of norms.

Exam trap

The response phase is also called the free association phase or performance proper; it is not psychoanalytic free association. The standard instruction is Rorschach's "What might this be?" In the Comprehensive System the examiner sits side by side with the client, records verbatim, and treats a record of fewer than 14 responses as too short to interpret. The cards are always given in the order I to X; the person, not the examiner, decides whether to turn them.

The inquiry

When all ten cards have been seen, the examiner begins the inquiry (also spelt enquiry), and its purpose is narrow and precise. It is not a chance for the person to give more responses, and it is not the scoring itself. It is the collection of the information without which the responses cannot be scored: for each response, where on the blot the person saw it (its location), what about the blot made it look that way (its determinants: the shape, the colour, the shading, an impression of movement), and, if it is unclear, what exactly was seen (its content). These are the three main dimensions that Section 10.4 will code.

The examiner introduces the inquiry briefly: they are going to go back through the cards together; the examiner will read out each thing the person said, and wants the person to show where it is and what makes it look like that, so that the examiner can see it the same way. Then, card by card and response by response, the examiner reads each response back verbatim and lets the person explain. The basic questions are only two, "Where is it?" and "What makes it look like that?", and the art of the inquiry lies in asking no more than is needed and never leading. A leading question is one that suggests its own answer. "Was it the colour that made it look like that?" invites the person to agree, and a colour determinant obtained that way tells you about the examiner, not about the client. What the examiner may do is follow up the person's own key words, the words in a response or in the inquiry that hint at a determinant the person has not yet explained, by repeating them neutrally: "You said it looks soft?" Table 10.9 sets leading and non-leading questions side by side.

Table 10.9 Leading questions to avoid in the inquiry, and their non-leading alternatives.
Leading (avoid)Non-leading (use)
"Was it the colour that made it look like a rangoli?""What makes it look like a rangoli?"
"Is the dancer moving?""Help me see the dancer the way you see her."
"Do you mean the whole blot?""Where on the blot is it?"
"Is it the shading that makes it look soft?""You said it looks soft. Show me what makes it look soft."
"Can you see anything else in it now?"Do not ask: the inquiry collects no new responses.
"Good. And the horns?"No praise. Read the next response back.

Watch Naina at work with Shaurya's three responses. She reads back the first: "You said, 'A big rangoli pattern, the kind they draw at Diwali. It's the same on both sides.'" Shaurya runs his finger round the outline. "The whole thing. It's the same on both sides, the way they draw it out from the centre. And these darker bits are where the colour is filled in thicker." Naina outlines the whole blot on her location sheet. "Darker bits" is a key word, so she repeats it: "You said darker bits?" "Yes, here and here; it's more filled in." She writes his answer down and asks nothing more. She now knows the location (the whole blot), that the outline and its symmetry shaped the percept, and that the darker patches contributed something; Section 10.4 will decide how that is coded. For the horns, Shaurya points to the two projections at the top and says, "Just the shape, curved and pointed", and there is nothing more to ask. For the dancer, Naina asks where she is and what makes her look like a dancer, and Shaurya, turning the card upside down again, shows the skirt flaring out on both sides, "the way it does when you twirl", and the light patch in the centre, "for her face". He has now said, in his own words, that he saw movement and that he used the white space inside the blot, and Naina did not have to suggest either. Had he said halfway through, "Actually, now it also looks like two parrots", Naina would have noted it but not added it to his record, because a response that first appears during the inquiry is not scored as part of R.

The timing of the inquiry was itself once debated. David Rapaport, Merton Gill and Roy Schafer (Section 10.5) inquired after each card, while the response was still fresh. Most Rorschach workers, and the Comprehensive System, inquire only after all ten cards, so that the inquiry questions, which show the person what the examiner is interested in, cannot shape the responses to the cards still to come. Both approaches agree on the essentials: the questions are non-directive, brief and precise, and a response that is already clear enough to code needs no questions at all.

Clinical link

A Rorschach record is a sample of behaviour in one particular situation, and the examiner is part of that situation. A smile at an interesting answer, an impatient "anything else?", or an inquiry question that hints at colour can all change what goes into the record, and once a determinant has been suggested it cannot be taken back out. That is why trainees are supervised closely on their first records, why the procedure is rehearsed until it is automatic, and why a good report says how the person approached the task (eagerly, cautiously, with irritation, with distress) as well as what was coded. If a client becomes distressed, the client's welfare comes before the completeness of the record.

Exam trap

The inquiry (enquiry) phase (RML 2025) is the detailed going-over and clarification of each response without leading questions, to establish location and determinants. It is not a phase in which the cards are "randomly shuffled" (the cards are always shown in the fixed order I to X, in every phase); it is not an "initial exposure to the inkblots without recording" (that describes nothing in the procedure: the first phase is the response phase, and every word is recorded verbatim from Card I onwards); and it is not "scoring the responses only" (the inquiry gathers what scoring needs; the coding of Section 10.4 is done afterwards, away from the client). The order is response phase, then inquiry, then (optional) testing the limits.

Testing the limits, and what the examiner watches for

The third phase is optional, and it comes only after the inquiry is complete. In testing the limits, a procedure associated above all with Bruno Klopfer's school, the examiner drops the rule of non-direction and asks direct questions, to find out whether something missing from the record is missing because the person cannot see it or merely because he or she did not report it. A person who gave only whole responses, perhaps believing that responses to the whole blot are "better", may be asked whether anything can be seen in a part of a blot. A person who reported no human figures anywhere may be shown a card again, told that some people see a person on it, and asked whether he or she can find one; the same can be done on a card where the person gave nothing that most people report. The answers can matter clinically. A person who cannot see a commonly reported percept even when it is pointed out is in a very different position from one who sees it at once and had simply passed it over. Two rules govern the phase. It must never come before the inquiry, because its suggestions would contaminate everything that followed. And what it produces is not added to the record or coded with the main responses, because it was obtained under quite different, directive conditions.

Throughout the administration the examiner is also watching behaviour that no single code captures. Rorschach himself noticed that some respondents were thrown by the arrival of colour, and called the reaction colour shock: a disruption at the coloured cards, especially the first ones a person meets (Card II, and Card VIII, the first fully coloured card), shown by a much longer reaction time, a sudden fall in the number or quality of responses, a rejection of the card, or exclamations and signs of discomfort. He linked colour shock with neurotic respondents, and later clinicians read it as a sign that emotionally arousing situations disorganise the person. A parallel disturbance on the dark, heavily shaded cards came to be called shading shock and was read as a sign of anxiety. Examiners also note rejections, long silences, repeated turning of the card, apologies and self-criticism ("I'm no good at this"), and comments on the blots themselves ("It's just ink", "These are ugly"). All of these are clinical observations rather than scores, and the meanings attached to them belong to clinical lore: the evidence that colour shock, as a sign, tells you anything reliable about a person's emotional life is weak (Section 10.10).

A worked example: counting R and looking for colour shock. Suppose Shaurya's full response phase on the ten real cards produced the numbers of responses and reaction times shown in Table 10.10 (invented figures, separate from the single invented blot used above). Two questions can be answered with nothing more than addition and division. Is the record long enough to interpret? And are his reaction times longer on the coloured cards? First the two quantities in words. R is simply the total number of responses across the ten cards. A mean reaction time for a group of cards is the sum of their reaction times divided by the number of cards in the group, and the informal colour-shock comparison is the difference between the mean for the chromatic cards and the mean for the achromatic cards.

Remember

R = rI + rII + rIII + ... + rX = Σr

Mean RT of a group of cards = ΣRT ÷ n

Colour-shock difference = mean RTchromatic − mean RTachromatic

Here r is the number of responses to one card (rI to Card I, and so on), Σ means "add them all up", RT is a card's reaction time in seconds, and n is the number of cards in the group (5 chromatic and 5 achromatic). The colour-shock difference is an informal clinical comparison, not a formal score in the Comprehensive System.

Table 10.10 Shaurya's numbers of responses and reaction times on the ten cards (invented figures for the worked example).
CardColour groupResponses (r)Reaction time (seconds)
IAchromatic26
IIBlack and red114
IIIBlack and red29
IVAchromatic111
VAchromatic15
VIAchromatic112
VIIAchromatic18
VIIIMulticoloured124
IXMulticoloured117
XMulticoloured212
Total13118

Step 1: R. Add the responses card by card: 2 (Card I), plus 1 makes 3, plus 2 makes 5, plus 1 makes 6, plus 1 makes 7, plus 1 makes 8, plus 1 makes 9, plus 1 makes 10, plus 1 makes 11, plus 2 makes 13. So R = 13. Thirteen is fewer than 14, so under the Comprehensive System this record is too short to interpret, and Naina must explain this to Shaurya and go through the cards again, asking for more responses, before anything is coded.

Step 2: the achromatic mean. The achromatic cards are I, IV, V, VI and VII, with reaction times of 6, 11, 5, 12 and 8 seconds. Add them: 6 + 11 = 17; 17 + 5 = 22; 22 + 12 = 34; 34 + 8 = 42 seconds. There are 5 cards, so the mean is 42 ÷ 5 = 8.4 seconds.

Step 3: the chromatic mean. The chromatic cards are II, III, VIII, IX and X, with reaction times of 14, 9, 24, 17 and 12 seconds. Add them: 14 + 9 = 23; 23 + 24 = 47; 47 + 17 = 64; 64 + 12 = 76 seconds. (Check: 42 + 76 = 118, the total in the table.) The mean is 76 ÷ 5 = 15.2 seconds.

Step 4: compare. The difference is 15.2 − 8.4 = 6.8 seconds, and the ratio is 15.2 ÷ 8.4 ≈ 1.81 (rounded to two decimal places), so on average Shaurya took nearly twice as long to respond to a coloured card. The sharpest jumps come exactly where colour arrives: from 6 seconds on Card I to 14 on Card II, more than double, and from 8 seconds on Card VII to 24 on Card VIII, three times as long.

Step 5: interpret, carefully. In the older clinical lore this is the classic picture of colour shock. Before Naina writes anything, though, she should consider at least four cautions. The multicoloured cards are busier and more complex than the achromatic ones, and many people slow down on them, so a delay means something only when it is compared with what is usual for people in general, not with no delay at all. A single slow card can drive a mean: leave out Card VIII and the chromatic mean becomes (14 + 9 + 17 + 12) ÷ 4 = 52 ÷ 4 = 13 seconds, still higher than 8.4 but less striking. Reaction time is also affected by care, fatigue, language and simple unfamiliarity with the task. And the evidence that colour shock predicts anything about emotional life is weak. So what goes into Naina's notes is an observation ("reaction times markedly longer on the first red card and the first multicoloured card") to be weighed with the interview and the other tests, not a conclusion that Shaurya is emotionally disorganised by feeling. Because the record must in any case be given again, the reaction times of this first run remain behavioural observations rather than part of the coded record.

Exam trap

Testing the limits (associated with Klopfer's school) comes after the inquiry, never before it; it allows direct questions, and what it yields is not scored with the main record. Colour shock is a disruption (longer reaction time, rejection, fall in the number or quality of responses) on the coloured cards, especially Card II and Card VIII; Rorschach linked it with neurotic respondents, but as a sign it has weak support. A card rejection is a refusal to respond to a card, encouraged against but recorded. The coloured cards are II, III, VIII, IX and X; the achromatic cards are I, IV, V, VI and VII.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Gardner Lindzey (1959) classified projective techniques by the kind of they ask the person to make, not by the stimulus they show.
  2. Because the person builds a story with a past, a present and a future, the TAT belongs to Lindzey's family.
  3. The Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study uses pictures, but it belongs to the family, because the person finishes an empty speech balloon.
  4. The Szondi Test, in which the person picks the most liked and most disliked faces, is a or ordering technique.
  5. In Lawrence Frank's 1939 scheme, handwriting was the model of the category, and play the model of the cathartic category.
  6. Hermann Rorschach, a Swiss psychiatrist trained under Eugen Bleuler, published Psychodiagnostik in and died the following year at the age of 37.
  7. Of the ten Rorschach cards, are achromatic, two are black and red, and three are multicoloured.
  8. The phase in which the examiner reads each response back and asks, without leading questions, where it was seen and what made it look that way is the .
  9. Asking a person directly, after the inquiry, whether he or she can see a percept that was not reported is called .
  10. A client's reaction times are 5, 7, 9, 6 and 8 seconds on the five achromatic cards and 12, 10, 20, 14 and 9 seconds on the five chromatic cards. The chromatic mean exceeds the achromatic mean by seconds.

Answers: (1) response, (2) construction, (3) completion, (4) choice, (5) refractive, (6) 1921, (7) five (Cards I, IV, V, VI and VII), (8) inquiry (enquiry), (9) testing the limits, (10) 6: the achromatic total is 5 + 7 + 9 + 6 + 8 = 35, so the mean is 35 ÷ 5 = 7 seconds; the chromatic total is 12 + 10 + 20 + 14 + 9 = 65, so the mean is 65 ÷ 5 = 13 seconds; and 13 − 7 = 6 seconds.

10.4 The Rorschach II: Scoring Location, Determinants, Form Quality and ContentMedium yield · 3 Qs

When a Rorschach administration ends, the examiner is left holding a few pages of handwriting. On them are perhaps twenty or thirty responses, each taken down word for word with the number of the card and the way it was held, and under each response the notes from the inquiry, in which the person explained where on the blot the thing was and what made it look like that (Section 10.3). What can anyone do with such a pile of words? Read them like a short story and trust your intuition? That is roughly what many clinicians did in the test's early decades, and it helped give the test its reputation for saying whatever the interpreter wanted it to say. The answer of every serious system, from Hermann Rorschach's own to the present day, is different: before anything is interpreted, every response is translated into a short line of symbols, a code, that records not so much what the person saw as how they saw it.

Rorschach himself regarded these formal features of a response as more informative than its content. Two people may both see "a moth", but one may use the whole blot and fit its shape carefully, while the other seizes on a tiny corner and calls it a moth because it is grey. The codes are built to catch exactly that difference. They record which part of the blot was used (location), how the parts were organised (developmental quality), which features of the blot shaped the percept (determinants), how well the percept fits the blot (form quality), what kind of thing was seen (content), and whether it is an answer that many people give (popularity), together with a few special features of the wording. This section teaches you to write those codes. It uses the symbols of the Comprehensive System of John E. Exner Jr (1928 to 2006), which became the standard way of coding the test in most of the world, and wherever the older systems of Bruno Klopfer (1900 to 1971) and Samuel J. Beck (1896 to 1980) wrote the same thing differently, it says so. Section 10.5 then shows what is done with the codes once they are written. Every blot and every response in this section is invented; the real plates and their common answers are protected test material, for the reasons given in Book 2, Section 1.9.6.

Why every response is coded before anything is interpreted

Think of a cricket scorer. Every ball bowled is entered in the scorebook in a fixed notation: a dot for no run, a figure for runs, a W for a wicket, a mark for a wide. Nobody calculates a strike rate or an economy rate while the ball is in the air. The averages come later, and they are only as good as the ball-by-ball record beneath them. Rorschach coding is the ball-by-ball record of the test. Each response gets its line in the sequence of scores, a sheet with one row per response and a fixed order of columns, and only when every row is filled in are the codes counted and turned into the percentages, ratios and indices of the Structural Summary (Section 10.5).

There are two reasons for insisting on this order. The first is comparability. A code means the same thing wherever it is written: a "W" coded in a hospital in Bengaluru and a "W" coded in a clinic in Chicago refer to the same behaviour, so a person's record can be compared with norms collected from thousands of other people. Without a shared code there are no norms, and without norms an interpreter has nothing but personal experience to judge by. The second reason is reliability. Coding is a judgement, and wherever there is judgement two coders may disagree. If they disagree about the codes, every number built from the codes inherits the disagreement. Exner made scorer agreement a hurdle for every category he admitted to his system, and the debate over whether his codes really reach that standard is told in Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9; here it is enough to know that coding is the point at which the test's reliability is won or lost.

The coder works from what the person actually said. The response is what was given in the response phase; the inquiry is used only to clarify it, to find where the object was and which features made it look that way. Three rules follow. A new percept that turns up for the first time during the inquiry is not coded as a response. A feature that appears only because the examiner's question suggested it ("Was the colour important?") is not coded either, which is why Section 10.3 insists that inquiry questions be non-leading. And when the coder is unsure, the person's own key words decide: "because of the shape" points one way, "because it is red" another, "it looks soft" a third.

Every coded response comes out as a single line, read from left to right in the same order every time. Figure 10.7 takes one invented response apart. The first slot gives the location and its developmental quality; then come the determinants, joined by full stops when there is more than one, with the form quality attached to the end of them; then a 2 if the person saw a pair; then the content codes, separated by commas; then a P if the answer is one of the popular responses; then an organisational score called Z; and last any special scores. The rest of this section takes the slots one at a time.

Exam trap

Keep the six questions apart. Location asks where on the blot; developmental quality asks how the parts were organised; the determinant asks what features of the blot made it look like that (shape, movement, colour, shading); form quality asks how well it fits; content asks what it is; popularity asks how common it is. "A red butterfly" has an animal content, but its determinants are form and colour. A paper that offers "content" as the answer to "what made it look like that?" is testing exactly this confusion.

Location: where on the blot?

The first question about any response is the simplest: which part of the blot did the person use? The answer is given by one of four location codes, and the inquiry exists largely to make it possible ("Show me where you saw it"). Figure 10.8 marks all four on an invented blot.

A whole response, coded W, is scored when the person uses the entire blot, every part of it, for the percept: "The whole thing is a moth, with its wings spread out." The rule is strict. In the Comprehensive System a W must use all of the blot; if the person leaves out even a small piece ("all of it except these bits at the bottom"), the response is not a W. Bruno Klopfer's system allowed a "cut-off whole" for exactly this case, a whole response that omitted minor details, but Beck objected that coders could not agree on how much could be cut off before a whole stopped being a whole, and Exner sided with Beck. (How a record rich in well-formed W responses is read, as organisational and integrative effort in the Comprehensive System's terms, is explained in Section 10.5.)

A common detail response, coded D, uses a part of the blot that many people pick out: a clearly separated side lobe, a coloured patch, a long projection. Whether an area counts as a D is not left to the coder's taste. For each real card the Comprehensive System provides a location table, built from the frequency with which areas were used in large numbers of records, in which the common areas are numbered (D1, D2 and so on). An area on the list is a D. An unusual detail response, coded Dd, is anything else: a tiny knob on the edge, an odd slice cut across two natural areas, an area that few people ever use. Logically, then, Dd is defined by exclusion: a response that is neither W nor D is Dd. The tables number the better-known unusual areas as well, and any area not listed at all is written Dd99, so in a coded record you will see strings such as "Do2" (common area number 2) and "Ddo99" (an unlisted unusual area).

The fourth code is different in kind. A white space response, coded S, is scored when the person uses the white background, the spaces inside or around the ink, as part of the percept: "a lamp, and this white part in the middle is the glass". The white space is never coded on its own. It is always attached to one of the other three codes, so the location is written WS, DS or DdS according to how much of the blot was involved. A response that uses a central white patch plus the ink around it might be DS; a response that uses all the ink and the white within it is WS.

The older systems carved the blot more finely. Klopfer split the detail responses into large usual details (D), small usual details (written with a small d) and four kinds of rare detail: tiny areas, edges, inside areas and rare odd selections. Exner collapsed them all into a single Dd, partly because coders disagreed about which kind of Dd they were looking at, partly because each kind was so rare that it could not carry any interpretation by itself (Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9 tells this part of the story as a lesson in scorer reliability). The numbered area tables of the Comprehensive System follow the area coding that Beck had promoted, in which the common areas were fixed by counting how often they were used.

Two older location codes deserve attention even though they have faded from use. A confabulated whole response, coded DW (the "confabulatory" response of the older literature), is one in which the person notices a single detail, sees something in it, and then extends the percept to the entire blot without checking whether the rest of the blot fits: "These are claws, so the whole thing is a crab", when nothing else on the blot looks like a crab. The detail comes first and the whole is dragged after it. The same leap from an unusual detail to a larger common area was written DdD, the confabulated detail, and a leap from an unusual detail to the whole was written DdW. The classical systematisers mostly read confabulation as a sign of poor contact with reality: the percept is not tested against the stimulus. Exner's first volume (1974) still listed DW, but later editions of the Comprehensive System kept only the four codes W, D, Dd and S; such a response is now coded as a W, its failure shows up where it belongs, in a minus form quality (described below), and the leap from detail to whole is recorded with a special score of its own, CONFAB (confabulation), described with the special scores below. Section 10.5 explains why a record full of such answers is read as impaired reality testing. Table 10.11 collects the location codes.

Table 10.11 The location codes, with invented examples.
CodeNameCoded whenInvented example
WWholeEvery part of the blot is used"A moth, wings spread out" (all of it)
DCommon detailAn area listed as common in the location table"A green chilli" (one side lobe)
DdUnusual detailAny area that is neither W nor D"A bird's beak" (a tiny knob on the edge)
SWhite spaceWhite background used; always with W, D or Dd"A lamp; the white is its glass" (DS)
DW (older)Confabulated wholeOne detail seen, then stretched to the whole blot"Claws, so the whole thing is a crab"
DdD (older)Confabulated detailAn unusual detail stretched to a common area"A tooth, so this whole lobe is a jaw"
Exam trap

S is never coded alone: it is always WS, DS or DdS. Dd is defined by exclusion (neither W nor D), not by being small, although it usually is. In the Comprehensive System a W must use all of the blot; Klopfer's cut-off whole is not allowed. The confabulatory response is DW: a single detail generalised to the whole blot, the classic sign of poor reality testing (IHBAS 2020); the later Comprehensive System codes the same answer as a W with minus form quality and the special score CONFAB. It is not a sign of "regressed ego", "cognitive immaturity" or "lack of empathy". Do not confuse it with the clinical sense of confabulation in amnesia, filling memory gaps with invented stories: the Rorschach term borrows the word for a perceptual leap, not a memory error.

Developmental quality: how the percept is organised

Location tells you where a percept is; it does not tell you how much mental work went into it. Compare two whole responses to the same invented blot: "Some ink, just splashed about" and "Two children on either side, pulling a kite string between them, and the kite flying up at the top". Both are W. The first takes the blot as a formless lump; the second picks out several parts, gives each a definite shape and ties them together in a meaningful relationship. The developmental quality code, abbreviated DQ, records this difference. The idea came from developmental scoring of the Rorschach worked out by Howard Friedman in papers of 1952 and 1953 on the basis of the theory of Heinz Werner (1890 to 1964), for whom development, in a child or in a perception, moves from global and diffuse towards differentiated and integrated. The Comprehensive System's developmental quality codes descend from that line of work. There are four of them, written directly after the location letter, and Table 10.12 collects them.

The key to all four is the notion of form demand. An object has form demand if it has a definite, recognisable shape that the blot must match: a person, a crab, a lantern, a temple bell. An object has no form demand if it can take any shape at all: a cloud, smoke, blood, a puddle, a splash of paint. With that idea in hand, the codes follow from two questions: how many objects are there, and does at least one of them have form demand?

  • Synthesised (+): two or more objects are seen as separate but related, and at least one has form demand. "Two children pulling a kite string" is W+; "two women dancing and holding hands" is D+ if it uses a common area. The relationship is essential: two crabs listed side by side with no connection between them are not synthesised.
  • Ordinary (o): a single object with form demand. "A moth" is Wo; "a green chilli" in a side lobe is Do. A pair of identical objects with no relationship between them ("two crabs, one on each side") is also coded ordinary.
  • Vague-synthesised (v/+): two or more objects seen as separate but related, none of which has form demand. "Clouds drifting across the sky over a lake" is Wv/+: there is organisation, but nothing in it needs a particular shape.
  • Vague (v): a single object without form demand, or several such objects with no relationship between them. "Some ink, just splashed about", "smoke", "blood": Wv or Dv.
Table 10.12 The four developmental quality codes of the Comprehensive System.
CodeNameHow many objects; form demand?Invented example
+SynthesisedTwo or more, related; at least one has form demand"Two children pulling a kite string"
oOrdinaryOne with form demand (or several, unrelated)"A temple bell"
v/+Vague-synthesisedTwo or more, related; none has form demand"Smoke rising from a fire"
vVagueOne without form demand (or several, unrelated)"A splash of paint"

Developmental quality is claimed to reflect the quality of a person's information processing: synthesised responses suggest a mind that analyses and integrates, a predominance of vague ones a mind that settles for global, undifferentiated impressions, as young children and some people with serious disorders often do. Neither Klopfer's nor Beck's system had a separate developmental quality code; the nearest relative in Beck's system was his Z score for organisational activity, which the Comprehensive System also kept (described below with the special scores).

Exam trap

Developmental quality and form quality are independent. DQ asks how the percept is organised; form quality asks how well it fits the blot. A response can be beautifully synthesised and still fit badly (W+ with minus form quality), or simple and accurate (Wo with ordinary form quality). And "v" means vague, not "very good"; "+" in DQ means synthesised, which is a different "+" from the elaborated form quality "+" described below.

Determinants I: form and movement

The determinant is the heart of Rorschach coding and the hardest part to learn. The question it answers is: which features of the blot made it look like that, to this person? A blot offers only a few kinds of raw material. It has a shape. It has colour, or the blackness, greyness and whiteness of the ink. It has shading, the variations of light and dark within the ink. And it has nothing that actually moves, although many people see movement in it. Rorschach's book of 1921 coded only form, movement and colour. He began to score shading himself, with the symbol (C) for chiaroscuro, in his last paper, a lecture of 1922 published after his death in 1923, and his successors then worked out the separate shading codes as they studied what people did with the light and dark tones (Section 10.3 tells how the printing of the plates put the shading there). The Comprehensive System uses 24 determinant symbols, grouped into nine families, all collected in Table 10.13 below, after the shading codes. The coder finds them in the person's own words, mostly in the inquiry: "it's the shape", "they are dancing", "because it is red", "it looks furry".

Start with the simplest. A pure form response, coded F, is one determined by the shape or outline of the blot alone. Vaishali, a trainee clinical psychologist in a hospital psychology department, shows an invented black blot to her client Chirag. He says, "A maple leaf." In the inquiry she asks what made it look like a maple leaf, and he answers, "Just the shape: the pointed edges here, and this is the stalk." Nothing about colour, shading or movement enters, so the determinant is F. Form is the most common determinant in almost every record. Note one rule at once: F is coded only when nothing else contributes. If colour, shading or movement also played a part, the form is carried inside another code (FC, FT and so on) or is taken for granted (movement responses assume form), and F is not written separately. F is therefore never part of a combination of determinants. Pure form answers are traditionally read as handling the blot in a matter-of-fact, impersonal way, with feeling and fantasy kept out; a record made almost entirely of F is read as a narrowed, economising style, which Section 10.5 measures with the ratio called Lambda.

Movement was among Rorschach's most original ideas. The blots are motionless ink, yet many people see figures running, dancing, fighting or bowing, and Rorschach believed that this "kinaesthetic" seeing came from inside the perceiver. The Comprehensive System codes three kinds of movement, according to who or what is moving.

A human movement response, coded M, is scored when the person describes human activity: "two women dancing", "a man bending to tie his shoe", "two old men arguing", and also quiet human states such as sitting, thinking or looking. M is coded as well when an animal or a fictional character is described doing something only humans do: "two rabbits riding a scooter" and "two crows chatting over tea" are M, not animal movement, because neither rabbits nor crows do such things. Rorschach linked M to the inner life: imagination, the capacity to put oneself in another's place, and the ability to delay action and think instead. That is why human movement sits on one side of his experience type, which Section 10.5 explains.

An animal movement response, coded FM, is scored when an animal does something natural to its kind: "a frog leaping", "two dogs fighting", "a bird perched on a branch". The code was introduced by Klopfer; Beck refused to treat animal movement as a determinant at all and coded such answers by their form, one of the clearest differences between the two classical systems. FM is traditionally read as a sign of basic urges and need states, pressing for attention but less integrated into thought than M.

An inanimate movement response, coded m (a small m), is scored when the movement belongs to objects or natural forces: "a flag fluttering in the wind", "a waterfall pouring down", "an explosion", and also objects held in tension, such as "a coat hanging on a hook" or "a rope pulled tight". The code goes back to Zygmunt Piotrowski (1904 to 1985), and Klopfer took it up. It is read as a sign of situational stress, a feeling of being pushed by forces outside one's control.

Each movement code also carries a small letter showing whether the movement is active or passive movement: a for active, p for passive, often printed as superscripts (Ma, FMp) and written on the line simply as Ma or FMp. Running, fighting, dancing and lifting are active. Sitting, sleeping, standing, looking and, by convention, talking are passive. When one response contains both, the code is written a-p. "Two women dancing" is Ma; "a bird perched on a branch" is FMp; "a flag fluttering" is ma.

Exam trap

A response determined by the shape of the blot alone, with no colour or movement, is F, pure form (2024 paper): not FC (form plus colour), not M (human movement), not C (pure colour). M is human movement only, not "any movement": FM is animal movement, m is inanimate movement. But an animal doing something only people do ("two rabbits riding a scooter") is coded M, and an object under tension ("a coat hanging on a hook") is coded m. F is never combined with another determinant.

Determinants II: chromatic and achromatic colour

Five of the ten cards carry colour (Section 10.3), and the colour codes ask two questions about every response to a coloured area. Did the colour contribute to the percept? And if it did, which led the way, the shape or the colour? The answer places the response on a short ladder that runs from form-dominant to colour-dominant, shown in Figure 10.9.

A form-colour response, coded FC, is one in which form comes first and colour is added to elaborate or identify the object. Shown an invented blot with a green patch, Chirag says, "A green parrot: here is its beak, here are its wings, and it's green, like a parrot." The parrot needs a definite shape, the shape is there, and the green confirms it. A colour-form response, coded CF, reverses the order: colour leads and form is vague and secondary. "Fire: all this red and orange, roughly the shape of flames here." Fire has no fixed shape; the colour carries the percept and the shape is only loosely used. A pure colour response, coded C, uses colour with no form at all: "Blood, because it's red." Asked where, Chirag points vaguely at the red patch; asked what made it look like blood, he says only that it is red. There is no shape in the answer, so the code is C. If he had said, "A splash of blood, red, and spread out at the edges the way a splash spreads", the shape would have entered in a loose way, and the code would be CF.

The fourth chromatic code is not on the ladder at all. In colour naming, coded Cn, the person simply names the colours as if that were the answer: "Red. And this is green. And blue." No object is seen; the colour is reported instead of interpreted. An exclamation such as "What lovely colours!" is not Cn, because the person is commenting, not answering. Cn is very rare in ordinary records and has traditionally been associated with serious disorganisation or brain damage, a piece of lore with little modern research behind it.

Rorschach linked colour to emotion, and the ladder carries that idea. Responses in which form controls colour (FC) were read as feeling that is felt but kept in hand; colour that overrides form (CF) as feeling that is less restrained; colour with no form at all (C) as emotion discharged without control. In one of the specimen records printed in his book, Rorschach described the person's feelings as lively but stabilised by "good affective adaptability" (in a recent English translation), and the evidence he pointed to was two FC responses.
