How the three codes are weighted and summed, and set against human movement, is the business of Section 10.5.

Black, white and grey can also be used as colours, and the Comprehensive System codes this achromatic colour response with the symbol C' (read "C prime"), in the same three steps. "A black crow: here is its beak and its tail, and it's black" is FC', form first. "A dark storm cloud, all black" is C'F, the blackness leading and the shape vague. "Coal, because it's black" is C', achromatic colour alone, a rare response. Rorschach's own book had no separate code for black and white used as colours; Klopfer introduced one, and the Comprehensive System kept it. Achromatic colour is traditionally read as painful feeling that is held in rather than expressed. The line between C' and the shading codes below is drawn by what the person uses: C' when the blackness, greyness or whiteness itself is named as a colour, a shading code when the variations of light and dark are used.

One more colour phenomenon has its own code, but not as a determinant. Sometimes a person sees chromatic colour in an area that has none: "a pretty purple flower" on an all-black blot. This colour projection is recorded with the special score CP (described below), and the determinants are coded from whatever else the person used.

Exam trap

"This is a blood stain because it's red", with no reference to shape, is C, pure colour (2024 paper). It is not FC (a clear shape with colour added) or CF (colour first, shape vague but present), and it is not Cn, which merely names the colours ("red, green") without seeing anything. Remember too that the prime matters: C' is achromatic (black, white, grey), not chromatic. Colour seen on a black area is not a colour determinant at all but the special score CP.

Determinants III: shading, form dimension, pairs, reflections and blends

The ink of the blots is not flat. Within the black, the grey and the colours there are gradations, lighter and darker zones that can suggest a surface, a depth or a haze, and people use them in three different ways. The Comprehensive System gives each its own family, and each family has the same three steps as the colour codes: form first, shading first, or shading alone.

A texture response, coded with T, is one in which the shading is felt as a surface to touch: soft, furry, rough, silky, wet, cold. "A woollen shawl: here is the fringe, and the shading makes it look soft and fuzzy" is FT, the shape of the shawl leading. "Something soft, like cotton wool" is TF, the softness leading and the shape vague. "Velvet, from the shading", with no shape at all, is a pure T, which is rare. In most records the form-first FT is by far the commonest of the three.

A vista response, coded with V, is one in which the shading is seen as depth or distance. "A well seen from above; the darker middle is deeper down" is FV; "a deep valley, the shading going down and down" is VF; "depth, just depth" is a pure V. A diffuse shading response, coded with Y, uses the light and dark in a general way that is neither touch nor depth. "A shirt with dirty patches; the darker parts are the dirt" is FY; "smoke, lighter here, darker there" is YF; "darkness" or "fog" is a pure Y.

Here the older systems disagreed most. Klopfer used a small c for texture, K and k for diffuse shading (K for shading seen as a three-dimensional haze, k for shading seen as a flat projection such as an X-ray or a map) and FK for vista. Beck used T for texture, V for vista and Y for diffuse shading, and these are the letters Exner adopted. The traditional readings, all of them claims to be tested in Section 10.10, are that texture reflects a need for closeness and contact, vista a painful kind of self-examination, and diffuse shading a sense of helplessness under stress.

Depth can also come from shape alone. A form dimension response, coded FD, is one in which the person sees perspective or distance from the size and outline of the blot areas, with no use of shading: "a man lying on his back with his feet towards us; the feet look big because they are nearer". FD was a new category in the Comprehensive System. The difference from FV is the source of the depth: shading for FV, shape and size for FD.

Two more codes exploit the symmetry of the blots. A pair response is scored when the person reports two identical objects, one on each side of the midline, relying on the symmetry: "two crabs, one on each side". It is written (2) in the tables and simply 2 in the code string, after the form quality. A reflection response is scored when one side is seen as the mirror image or reflection of the other: "a tiger at the river's edge, and this half is its reflection in the water" is Fr, form first; "trees and clouds reflected in a lake, all hazy" is rF, the reflection leading and the shape vague. A reflection is never also coded as a pair, because the second object is not a second object, only an image of the first. Beck had scored reflections as vista and Klopfer as FK; the Comprehensive System gave them a category of their own, and Section 10.5 shows how pairs and reflections are combined in an index of self-focus.

Finally, many good responses use more than one feature of the blot. A response with two or more determinants is a blend, and the codes are joined by full stops. "Two women in red saris, dancing and holding hands" is Ma.FC: human movement, active, plus form-colour. "Two puppies curled up asleep; they look soft and furry here" is FMp.FT: animal movement, passive, plus form-texture. Each determinant in a blend is counted separately when the codes are tallied, and the number of blends in a record is itself read as a sign of psychological complexity (Section 10.5).

Table 10.13 The 24 determinant symbols of the Comprehensive System in their nine families, with invented examples.

Exam trap

Three depth-and-darkness pairs are favourite swaps. C' versus Y: C' when black, grey or white is named as a colour ("a black crow"), Y when the gradations of light and dark are used ("smoke, lighter here, darker there"). FV versus FD: vista takes its depth from shading, form dimension from size and outline. (2) versus Fr: a pair is two objects, a reflection is one object and its mirror image, and the two are never coded together. Texture (T) is shading felt as touch, not "tension"; tension in objects ("a coat hanging on a hook") is inanimate movement, m.

Form quality: how well does the percept fit the blot?

A response can be organised with great energy, use form, colour and movement together, and still be wrong. Chirag looks at an invented blot with two small hooks at the top and nothing else crab-like about it, and says, "A crab; these are the claws." The claws fit, more or less. The rest of the crab is simply not there. The form quality (FQ) code records how well the object a person reports matches the contours of the area they used. It is the one point in coding at which the coder judges the match between the inner impression and the outer stimulus, and for that reason it is the main Rorschach index of reality testing: the capacity to perceive the world as others perceive it, and to check one's impressions against what is actually there.

The Comprehensive System uses four form quality codes, attached to the end of the determinants, and a fifth possibility, none; Table 10.14 sets them beside their older equivalents.

  • Ordinary (o): a common answer, in which the general features of the object are easy to see in the area used. Whether an answer counts as ordinary is not left to the coder's taste; the system's form quality tables list, area by area, the objects reported often enough in large samples of records, and in the Comprehensive System good form is above all a matter of how often an answer occurs. "A moth" for a whole blot shaped like one is Wo Fo.
  • Ordinary-elaborated (+) (called superior-overelaborated in Exner's 1993 edition): an ordinary answer described with unusual precision, in a way that improves the fit rather than straining it. "A woman dancing: her arms raised here, the edge of her sari flaring out here, and her hair tied in a knot at the top." It is uncommon, and a coder should not give it simply because an answer is long.
  • Unusual (u): an infrequent answer whose contours nevertheless fit, so that an observer can see it quickly and easily once it is pointed out. "Two puppies curled up together" for a small rounded area might be u: few people say it, but it is not a distortion.
  • Minus (−): a distorted, arbitrary or unrealistic use of form, in which the person imposes contours that are not there or ignores the contours that are. Chirag's crab is F−.
  • None: a response with no form in it at all (pure C, C', T, V or Y, and Cn) receives no form quality code, and is counted as "none".
Table 10.14 Form quality codes in the Comprehensive System and their older equivalents.
CodeNameRuleOlder systems
+Ordinary-elaboratedOrdinary fit, described with unusual, fitting detailGood form, F+
oOrdinaryCommon answer; contours easily seenGood form, F+
uUnusualRare answer; contours fit and are easily seenOften F+; a good original
−MinusDistorted, arbitrary; form imposed or ignoredPoor form, F−
none(no code)No form used at allNot rated

Form quality is the oldest idea in Rorschach scoring. Rorschach marked each pure form response either F+ (good) or F− (poor), judging it against the answers of his normal subjects and his own sense of the fit, and the F+ percentage, the share of pure form responses that are good, was one of the first ratios Rorschach himself calculated. In one specimen record in his book he counted 12 good forms among 14 pure-form responses and wrote the result as 86 per cent. Beck tied the plus and minus judgements to frequency tables drawn from normal records; Klopfer replaced the simple plus and minus with a graded form level rating that also gave credit for elaboration; and the Comprehensive System extended the judgement from pure F responses to every response that uses form, and split "good" into ordinary, elaborated and unusual. Once each response has its form quality code, a few simple percentages summarise the whole record.

Remember

X+% = (number of responses with form quality + or o) ÷ R × 100

X−% = (number of responses with form quality −) ÷ R × 100

XA% = (number of responses with form quality +, o or u) ÷ R × 100

F+% = (pure F responses with form quality + or o) ÷ (all pure F responses) × 100

R is the total number of responses in the record. X+% is the share of conventional, good fits; X−% the share of distorted fits; XA% ("form appropriate") the share of all acceptable fits, conventional or unusual; F+% the old Rorschach ratio, restricted to pure form answers. In the Comprehensive System these are usually written as proportions (.50) rather than percentages (50%); the two mean the same thing.

Work through Chirag's record. He gave R = 20 responses. Vaishali's form quality codes came out as follows: 1 response coded +, 9 coded o, 5 coded u, 4 coded −, and 1 with no form (a pure C). Check that they add up: 1 + 9 + 5 + 4 + 1 = 20. Good.

For X+%, add the + and o responses: 1 + 9 = 10. Divide by R: 10 ÷ 20 = 0.50. Multiply by 100: X+% = 50%. For X−%, take the minus responses: 4 ÷ 20 = 0.20, so X−% = 20%. For XA%, add the +, o and u responses: 1 + 9 + 5 = 15, and 15 ÷ 20 = 0.75, so XA% = 75%. Notice that the pure C response counts in R, the denominator, but in none of the numerators, because it has no form quality at all. Finally, for F+%: 8 of Chirag's responses were pure F, and of those, 5 were coded o and none +, while 1 was u and 2 were minus. So F+% = 5 ÷ 8 = 0.625; rounded to two decimal places, .63, or 63%.

In plain words: half of Chirag's percepts are the conventional ones most people would see, another quarter are unusual but fit the blot, and one in five does not fit the blot at all. One minus answer in five is well above the average in Exner's own adult norms, though less striking against several later international samples, and it is the kind of figure that sends a careful clinician back to the record: are the four minus answers bunched on the coloured cards, as if strong feeling disturbed his perception, or scattered through the record with odd wording, which would suggest something more pervasive? But it is a hypothesis, not a finding. Section 10.5 shows how form quality enters the Structural Summary and the Perceptual-Thinking Index, and Section 10.10 explains why the answer to "is 20 per cent high?" depends on which normative sample you compare with.

Clinical link

Form quality is where the Rorschach comes closest to a perceptual test, and the reviews summarised in Section 10.10 found the perceptual accuracy and thought disorder variables among the best supported in the whole test. But the tables that define "ordinary" were built mostly from Western records, and samples from many countries produce lower form quality on them. A percept that is common and well fitting in an Indian setting (a kalash, a temple lamp, a Kathakali face) may not appear in the table at all; the coder must then extrapolate from similar objects that are listed, and an answer that resembles nothing on the list will usually be coded u at best. A good coder judges the fit, not the familiarity, and a careful interpreter remembers that a lower X+% in a person from a different culture may partly reflect the table rather than the person.

Exam trap

Form quality is the index of reality testing; colour is read for emotion, location for organisational approach. Minus means a poor fit, not unpleasant content or "minus marks". u is unusual but acceptable, not "unacceptable". A formless response (pure C) has no form quality; it is not coded minus. X+% counts + and o; X−% counts minus; both are divided by all responses, R. And the confabulatory DW almost always carries poor form quality, which is why it is read as impaired reality testing (IHBAS 2020).

Content, popular and original responses

Only now, after location, organisation, determinants and fit, does coding turn to the question a lay person would ask first: what did the person see? The content code names the category of the object. The later editions of the Comprehensive System use 27 content codes, set out in Table 10.15, the last of which, Idiographic (Id), takes anything the other 26 do not cover. When a response contains more than one kind of object, every content is coded, separated by commas, with the main one first: "two women in red saris" is H,Cg, human and clothing. Brackets mark the unreal: (H) is a whole human figure that is fictional or mythological, such as a ghost, a fairy, a demon or a cartoon character, and (A) and (Ad) do the same for animals.

Table 10.15 The 27 content codes of the Comprehensive System.
CodeContentCodeContent
HWhole human, real(H)Whole human, fictional or mythological
HdHuman detail, real (a face, a hand)(Hd)Human detail, fictional (a demon's mask)
HxHuman experience (an emotion, a sensation)AWhole animal, real
(A)Whole animal, fictional (a dragon)AdAnimal detail (a paw, a horn)
(Ad)Animal detail, fictionalAnAnatomy (organs, bones)
ArtArt (paintings, ornaments, emblems)AyAnthropology (historical or cultural objects)
BlBloodBtBotany (plants, leaves, flowers)
CgClothingClClouds
ExExplosion, including fireworksFdFood
FiFire or smokeGeGeography (a map)
HhHousehold objectsLsLandscape
NaNature (sky, sea, rainbow)ScScience and industry (machines, vehicles)
SxSex (sexual organs or activity)XyX-ray
IdIdiographic: anything not listed

Content lists have changed more than any other part of the coding. Rorschach's own specimen records used only a handful of headings (human, human detail, animal, animal detail, plant, object and the like), each later system drew up its own list of a different length, and even the letters differed: anatomy is An in Beck's system and the Comprehensive System but At in Klopfer's. Content is the part of the code most open to interpretation by symbol, and also the part on which the traditional readings are least secure. Human contents are read as a sign of interest in people, and their absence as a sign of poor interpersonal orientation, a reading Section 10.5 explains; anatomy and X-ray contents are read as signs of concern with the body.

A popular response, coded P, is a particular answer, to a particular area of a particular card, that is given so often that it marks the most conventional way of seeing that blot. In the Comprehensive System a response is popular if it occurs at least once in every three records in the normative samples, and the system lists 13 of them; P is written after the contents in the code string. The 13 are not reproduced in this book, card by card or in any other way, because a person who knew them in advance could game the test, which is part of the case for test security made in Book 2, Section 1.9.6. Populars are read as a measure of conventional perception: a record with very few suggests a person who does not see what most people see, and a record crowded with them a person clinging to the obvious. Because populars exist only for the real cards, the invented responses in this section never receive a P.

The older systems also coded the opposite pole. An original response, coded O, was an answer so rare that, by Rorschach's own rule in Psychodiagnostik, it turned up only about once in a hundred records. Originals were split by their fit: O+ for a rare answer that fits well, read as a sign of creativity, and O− for a rare answer that fits badly, read as a sign of bizarre or idiosyncratic thinking. Coders found originals very hard to agree on, because whether an answer is rare depends on how many records the coder has seen. The Comprehensive System has no O code at all. A rare answer that fits is coded u for form quality, and a rare answer that does not fit is coded minus, so the two halves of the old O live on inside form quality.

Exam trap

P is popular, O is original: P is frequent (at least one record in three in the Comprehensive System, which lists 13 populars), O is rare and was dropped by the Comprehensive System. H is a real whole human, Hd a part of one, (H) a fictional or mythological one; Hx is human experience, an emotion or sensation, not a human figure. And content is what was seen, never why it was seen: "a red butterfly" is an animal content with form-colour determinants.

Special scores and the Z score

The last slot of the code string records features that have nothing to do with which part of the blot was used, and everything to do with how the person thinks and speaks about it. These are the special scores. The most important group, six cognitive special scores, records cognitive slippage: lapses in the logic or language of a response, from the kind of slip anyone makes when tired to the strange fusions of the most disordered thinking. The six descend from the deviant verbalisations catalogued by David Rapaport (1911 to 1960) and his colleagues in Diagnostic Psychological Testing (1945 to 1946), who described fabulised combinations, contaminations and autistic logic as signs of disordered thinking. Four of the six (DV, INCOM, DR and FABCOM) are coded at Level 1, a mild slip, or Level 2, a severe or bizarre one; ALOG and CONTAM have a single level. Section 10.5 shows how the six are weighted and summed (WSum6), and how they enter the Perceptual-Thinking Index.

Further special scores record perseveration (PSV, the same answer repeated rigidly), confabulation (CONFAB, the old DW: one detail generalised to a larger area or the whole blot, usually with minus form quality) and features of content: abstract or symbolic content (AB), aggressive movement (AG), cooperative movement (COP), morbid content (MOR, damaged objects or a sad, gloomy quality), personalised answers that lean on the person's own experience (PER), and the colour projection (CP) met earlier. In the later editions every response with human content or human movement is also classified as a good or poor human representation (GHR or PHR), by a set of rules that uses the other codes of the response. Table 10.16 gathers them with invented examples.

Table 10.16 The special scores of the Comprehensive System, with invented examples.
CodeNameTriggered byInvented example
DVDeviant verbalisationA made-up word, an odd meaning or redundancy"A tiny little miniature boat"
INCOMIncongruous combinationImpossible features merged in one object"A parrot with four legs"
DRDeviant responseAn inappropriate phrase, or wandering off the task"A crab; my uncle sold crabs, and they cheated him, that is this town"
FABCOMFabulised combinationAn implausible relationship between separate objects"Two rabbits riding a scooter"
ALOGInappropriate (autistic) logicStrained reasoning offered without prompting"It must be a king, because it is the biggest"
CONTAMContaminationTwo percepts fused into one in the same area"A mountain-man: it is a mountain and it is a man"
PSVPerseverationThe same answer repeated rigidly"Again the same crab" on blot after blot
CONFABConfabulationOne detail generalised to a larger area or the whole blot"Claws, so the whole thing is a crab"
ABAbstract contentA symbolic meaning given"This is love; the red is passion"
AGAggressive movementMovement that is aggressive"Two dogs fighting"
COPCooperative movementPositive, collaborative movement"Two friends lifting a basket together"
MORMorbid contentA damaged object or a gloomy quality"A broken pot", "a sad old tree"
PERPersonalised answerPersonal knowledge used as justification"I have seen exactly this in my village"
CPColour projectionColour seen in an achromatic area"A purple flower" on a black blot
GHR, PHRGood or poor human representationAssigned to human responses by rule(follows from the other codes)

One more number sits in the code string between the populars and the special scores. The Z score, for organisational activity, was Beck's idea, and the Comprehensive System kept it. A Z value is given whenever a response organises the blot in a meaningful way: a whole response (unless it is vague), separate details brought into a relationship, whether they are next to each other or far apart, or white space integrated with the ink. The value is read from a table for each card, higher where organising the blot is harder. The values are summed, and the sum is compared with what would be expected for that number of organised responses; Section 10.5 explains the result, Zd, and what it is claimed to show about how efficiently a person scans a complex field.

Exam trap

INCOM versus FABCOM: an incongruous combination is impossible features inside one object ("a parrot with four legs"); a fabulised combination is an impossible relationship between two objects ("two rabbits riding a scooter", which is also coded M because rabbits do not ride). CONTAM, two percepts fused into one, is the most severe of the six. CP is colour seen where there is none; it is a special score, not a colour determinant. And COP and AG are coded only for movement responses.

Putting it together: coding six invented responses

Now push a few responses all the way through. Vaishali has Chirag's record in front of her, and she codes six of his responses to three invented blots. Blot A is the black and grey blot of Figure 10.8, with its white hole in the middle. Blot B is a black blot with two red areas at the sides and a red patch at the bottom. Blot C is a multicoloured blot of scattered parts, with two small hooks at the top and a small grey area at the bottom. Imagine that each invented blot comes with its own location and form quality tables, as the real cards do; the codes below use those imagined tables. Z values and populars are left out, because both are read from tables that exist only for the real cards, and so are the good and poor human representation codes (GHR, PHR), which a coder assigns last, by rule, from the other codes. Her colleague Sarika, another trainee, codes the same six responses on her own, without seeing Vaishali's codes, so that they can compare. Table 10.17 collects the six codes.

Response 1 (Blot A). "A big moth, with its wings spread out." Inquiry: "These are the wings, this is the body in the middle. It's just the shape." The whole blot is used, so the location is W. There is one object, and a moth has a definite shape, so the developmental quality is o. Only the shape is mentioned, so the determinant is F. The moth fits the whole blot easily and is listed as ordinary, so the form quality is o. The content is an animal. Code: Wo Fo A.

Response 2 (Blot A). "A table lamp, and this white part in the middle is its glass." Inquiry: "The lamp is this middle part, the base at the bottom, and the white is the glass where the light comes out." The central column is a common detail, and the white hole is part of the percept, so the location is DS. One object with form demand: o. The white is used as a part of the object, not described as a colour, so the determinant is still F, and the fit is ordinary. A lamp is a household object. Code: DSo Fo Hh.

Response 3 (Blot B). "Two women in red saris, dancing, holding hands." Inquiry: "Here are their heads, their arms joined in the middle, their legs; the red is the sari." The red side areas form a common detail: D. Two objects, both with form demand, in a relationship (dancing together, holding hands): +. Dancing is active human movement, Ma; the colour is used, but the women's shapes come first, so FC; the two are joined into a blend, Ma.FC, with ordinary form quality. They are identical figures on either side of the midline: 2. The contents are human and clothing, and the movement is cooperative. Code: D+ Ma.FCo 2 H,Cg COP. (This is the response taken apart in Figure 10.7.)

Response 4 (Blot B). "Blood." Inquiry: "Because it's red. Just blood." The red bottom patch is a common detail: D. One object with no form demand: v. Colour alone, no shape: C. A formless response gets no form quality. Code: Dv C Bl.

Response 5 (Blot C). "A crab. These are the claws, so the whole thing is a crab." Inquiry: "The claws here at the top; the rest of it is the crab." Chirag starts from one detail, the two hooks, and stretches the crab over the whole scattered blot, which does not look like a crab at all. Klopfer or Beck would have written the location as DW, a confabulated whole. In the later Comprehensive System it is W, one object with form demand (o), determined by form, but the fit is distorted: F−. The leap from one detail to the whole blot earns the special score CONFAB (confabulation). He does spell out his faulty reasoning ("so the whole thing is a crab"), but ALOG is not added as well, because the coding rules give CONFAB precedence and never code the two together. Code: Wo F− A CONFAB. This is the confabulatory response of the classical literature, and it is form quality that shows it up.

Response 6 (Blot C). "Two puppies, curled up asleep; they look soft and furry here." Inquiry: "Their heads are here, curled up together, and the shading makes it look like fur." The small grey area is rarely used: Dd. Two related objects with form demand: +. Sleeping is passive animal movement, FMp, and the fur comes from the shading felt as texture with the shape leading, FT: the blend is FMp.FT. Few people see puppies there, but they fit and are easy to see once pointed out: u. A pair, and the content is animal. Code: Dd+ FMp.FTu 2 A.

Table 10.17 Six invented responses and their Comprehensive System codes.
Response (invented blot)Code stringThe decisive clue
1. "A big moth, wings spread out" (A)Wo Fo AWhole blot; "just the shape"
2. "A table lamp; the white is its glass" (A)DSo Fo HhWhite space used as a part, not a colour
3. "Two women in red saris, dancing" (B)D+ Ma.FCo 2 H,Cg COPTwo related figures; shape first, red second
4. "Blood, because it's red" (B)Dv C BlNo shape at all; no form quality
5. "Claws, so the whole is a crab" (C)Wo F− A CONFABDetail stretched to the whole (old DW)
6. "Two puppies asleep, soft and furry" (C)Dd+ FMp.FTu 2 ARare area; passive movement plus texture

Even six codes start to show a pattern. Across them there are two W, two D, one Dd and one white space; three ordinary form qualities, one unusual, one minus and one with no form; and two blends. But six responses are not a record. The Comprehensive System will not interpret a record of fewer than 14 responses (Section 10.3), and every reading of these codes depends on the counts, percentages and ratios of a whole record, which is the subject of Section 10.5. When Vaishali and Sarika compare their codes, they go through them slot by slot, location first, then developmental quality, determinants, form quality, pairs, contents and special scores, and note every disagreement. Training manuals for research coders do exactly this, scoring practice records one segment at a time until each segment reaches a set level of agreement, for the reasons Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9 explains.

Where the older systems would have coded these responses differently, Table 10.18 shows how. The differences are not trivial. A response that Klopfer coded FM was, for Beck, simply F, so the same record could give two very different pictures of a person's "urges" depending on who coded it. That is the problem the Comprehensive System was built to solve, and Section 10.5 tells the story of how Exner solved it.

Table 10.18 How Klopfer, Beck and the Comprehensive System coded the same features.
FeatureKlopferBeckComprehensive System
Whole minus small partsCut-off whole allowedNot allowedNot allowed (D or Dd)
Detail areasD, d and four kinds of rare detailD and Dd, fixed by frequencyD and Dd, numbered in tables
Developmental qualityNone (form level rating)None (Z score)+, o, v/+, v
Animal movementFMNot scored as movementFM
Inanimate movementmNot scored as movementm
Texturec (Fc, cF, c)TT (FT, TF, T)
VistaFKVV (FV, VF, V)
Diffuse shadingK and kYY (FY, YF, Y)

Exam trap

The Comprehensive System codes location, developmental quality, determinants, form quality, pairs, content, populars, organisational activity (Z) and special scores. It gives no IQ score. Rorschach did think that some features of a record (many good wholes, a high F+ percentage, an orderly sequence) went with intelligence, and he commented on "intelligence" in his specimen records, but neither he nor Exner produced an IQ; intelligence is measured with the tests of Chapter 6. So in a list of "dimensions the Comprehensive System scores", the odd one out is the explicit IQ score.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. White space is never coded alone; a common detail used together with white space is written .
  2. A location that is neither the whole blot nor a common detail is coded .
  3. In developmental quality, two or more related objects, at least one with form demand, are coded ; a single object without form demand is coded .
  4. A response determined by the shape of the blot alone is coded .
  5. Human movement is coded M; an animal doing what its kind does is coded ; objects or forces in motion are coded .
  6. "Blood, because it is red", with no reference to shape, is coded ; simply naming the colours is coded .
  7. Black, white or grey used as a colour is coded with the symbol .
  8. A response with two or more determinants is called a , and its codes are joined by full stops.
  9. A single detail generalised to the whole blot, the confabulatory response, was coded in the older systems and is read as a sign of impaired .
  10. A record has R = 25: 2 responses with form quality +, 13 with o, 4 with u, 5 with minus and 1 with no form. Its X+% is and its X−% is .

Answers: (1) DS, (2) Dd (unusual detail), (3) + (synthesised); v (vague), (4) F (pure form), (5) FM; m, (6) C (pure colour); Cn (colour naming), (7) C' (C prime), (8) blend, (9) DW; reality testing, (10) X+% = (2 + 13) ÷ 25 = 15 ÷ 25 = 0.60, that is 60%; X−% = 5 ÷ 25 = 0.20, that is 20% (check: 2 + 13 + 4 + 5 + 1 = 25).

10.5 The Rorschach III: Reading the Record, from Klopfer and Beck to Exner and R-PASMedium yield · 7 Qs

Imagine you have just finished coding a Rorschach. In front of you are twenty short lines of letters and symbols, one for each thing the person saw, and every line is correct. You still know almost nothing about the person. "W+ Ma.FCo H" tells you that one response used the whole blot, saw people in action and used colour in a controlled way, but a single line is like a single word torn out of a letter. The meaning lies in how the lines add up: how many of the responses used the whole blot, how often movement appeared compared with colour, how many percepts fitted the blot and how many did not, whether people appeared at all. This is the step at which the Rorschach stops being a list of percepts and becomes a portrait, and it is also the step on which Hermann Rorschach's followers argued most fiercely. Who decides which numbers matter, and what they mean? The answer changed three times in a hundred years, and the story of those changes is the story of the scoring systems, from the rival American schools of Samuel Beck and Bruno Klopfer to John Exner's Comprehensive System and its successor, the Rorschach Performance Assessment System (R-PAS).

From codes to a profile: the sequence of scores and the Structural Summary

Start with what you already have. Section 10.4 taught you to turn each response into a code string, and this section uses only a handful of those codes. For location, W is the whole blot, D a common detail, Dd an unusual detail, and S, white space, is always added to one of the others (WS, DS). Developmental quality grades how the percept is put together: + for synthesised (separate parts related to each other), o for ordinary, v for vague, v/+ for vague parts brought together. The determinants are F (form alone); M, FM and m (human, animal and inanimate movement, each marked a for active or p for passive); the colour codes FC, CF, C and Cn (form-colour, colour-form, pure colour and colour naming); C' for achromatic colour; T, V and Y for texture, vista and diffuse shading; FD for form dimension; (2) for a pair and Fr or rF for a reflection; and a dot joins two determinants in a blend, as in M.FC. Form quality is + (unusually well fitted), o (ordinary), u (unusual but acceptable) or minus (a poor fit). Content codes name what was seen: H a whole human, (H) a fictional or mythical human, Hd a human part, A and Ad an animal and an animal part, An anatomy, Bt botany, Cg clothing, Na nature and so on. P marks a popular response. So an invented response, "two dancers lifting a lantern between them, and their skirts are red", might be coded W+ Ma.FCo (2) H, Cg.

The first thing a scorer in the Comprehensive System does with these strings is write them out in order as the sequence of scores: one line per response, card by card, giving the card number, the response number, the location and developmental quality, the determinants, the form quality, any pair, the contents, a P if it is popular, an organisational score and any special scores (the codes for odd verbalisations and faulty logic that Section 10.4 introduced). Why keep the order instead of going straight to totals? Because some of the most interesting information is sequential. A person whose form quality is good on the black and grey cards but collapses every time colour appears, or whose responses grow shorter and vaguer as the test goes on, shows something no total can show. The sequence is the raw record of how the person met each blot, one after another.

Then come the totals. The Structural Summary is the page on which the codes are counted and combined, and it has two halves. The upper section is simple bookkeeping: frequencies. How many W, D and Dd responses were there, and how many involved white space? How many were synthesised, ordinary or vague? How many times did each determinant appear (a blend such as M.FC counts once for M and once for FC, and the blends are also listed separately)? How many responses had each level of form quality, and each kind of content? How many populars, and how many special scores? The lower section turns those counts into ratios, percentages and derived scores, grouped into clusters that each address one area of functioning: a core section (with the number of responses, R, Lambda and the Experience Balance, all taught below), and clusters for ideation (thinking), affect (feelings), mediation (how accurately the person perceives), processing (how much effort the person puts into taking in information), interpersonal perception and self-perception. At the foot of the page sit the constellations, indices that add up several signs at once to flag a specific problem, such as the Depression Index; you will meet them in Section 10.5.6. Figure 10.10 shows the whole road from a spoken response to a written interpretation.

Why go to all this trouble instead of reading each response for what it seems to say? Because the founding idea of the scored Rorschach is that no single sign means anything on its own. One pure colour response may be a moment of playfulness; five of them, with poor form quality and few form-dominant colour responses, begin to say something about how the person handles feeling. Interpretation therefore works on the configuration, the pattern of many scores together, and each score is read against norms: the values found in samples of non-patients of similar age (what norms are and how they are built is the subject of Chapter 5; whether the Rorschach's norms were sound is argued in Section 10.10). Exner made this into a fixed routine. His interpretive "search strategy" checks a list of key variables in order, and the first one that is positive decides which cluster is read first; if the index of disordered perception and thinking is raised, for example, the interpreter begins with the clusters for thinking and perceptual accuracy. Every conclusion is then treated as a hypothesis to be checked against the history, the interview and the other tests, which is the posture this chapter recommends for every projective technique.

The very first number on the page is R, the total number of responses, and it is read first as a check on whether the record can be interpreted at all. Rorschach himself was unimpressed by it; in the English translation of Psychodiagnostik he concludes that "the study of the number of answers reveals nothing characteristic in the different groups of cases". Later systematisers took it more seriously, if only because so many other scores rise and fall with it: a person who gives 40 responses has more chances to produce a minus form or a texture response than a person who gives 12. A. K. Singh's Indian textbook gives the normal ranges the older authorities proposed: 15 to 30 for Rorschach, 20 to 45 for Klopfer and 17 to 27 for Exner in 1974. Exner later fixed a floor: a Comprehensive System record with fewer than 14 responses is treated as of doubtful validity, and the examiner asks the person to take the test again straight away, giving more answers this time (Section 10.3 describes the administration). Very short records are read as possible guardedness, depression, low ability or organic impairment; very long ones as an inability to stop, a striving to impress or, at the extreme, the flooding of disordered thinking. Notice what is missing from the Structural Summary: there is no IQ score anywhere on it. The Comprehensive System codes location, determinants, form quality, content, populars and special scores, and none of its ratios is a measure of intelligence (Section 10.4 makes the same point about the codes).

Location, form quality and content: what the main scores are claimed to mean

Each family of codes has a traditional reading, built up from Rorschach's own observations and the clinical experience of his successors, and in the Comprehensive System partly tested against research. As you read the claims below, keep two questions in mind: what is the logic linking the score to the trait, and how good is the evidence that the link is real? The first question is answered here. The second is answered in Section 10.10, and the short version is that the scores for perceptual accuracy and disordered thinking have the strongest support, while many of the others rest more on tradition than on data.

Location: how the person takes in a complex field. Every blot is a cluttered field of shapes, and the person must decide how much of it to use. A whole response (W) is the most demanding choice, because the person has to hold the entire blot in mind and make it into one thing. Rorschach linked a good supply of well-formed W responses to intelligence and to an abstract, synthesising turn of mind, and the link with intelligence turned out to be modest. In the Comprehensive System the reading is more careful. Many W responses, especially synthesised ones (W+, in which separate parts are related to one another, "two people leaning over a fire"), are taken to show organisational activity, the effort and drive to pull a complex field together into a meaningful whole. Exner measures this partly with Zf, the number of responses that earn an organisational score (a score for organising the blot, which Exner took from Samuel Beck), and partly with two ratios: the Economy Index, W : D : Dd, which shows how the person spreads attention between the whole, the obvious parts and the odd corners, and the Aspirational Index, W : M, which compares organising effort with the human movement responses that are taken to show resources for using it. Indian textbooks quote an expected adult W : D ratio of about 1 : 2 (A. K. Singh, citing Exner's 1974 volume): most people, most of the time, take the obvious parts. The quality of the W matters as much as the number. Many synthesised W responses suggest ambition and effort; many vague W responses ("some kind of map", "a cloud") suggest an easy, global, unanalysed way of taking things in, which in an adult is read as immature or careless processing.

The other locations are read against W. D responses, the common details, show a practical, economical person who deals with what is obvious; a record built almost entirely of D suggests someone who plays safe and avoids the effort of integration. Dd responses, the unusual details, are expected to be few (Singh gives about 5 to 8 per cent of responses in normal adults); many of them suggest a person who fixes on small or odd parts of a situation, read either as perfectionistic, obsessional care or as idiosyncratic perception. White space responses (S) have had the most argued reading of all. Rorschach, Beck and Rapaport took them as a sign of negativism or opposition, the person reversing figure and ground; Klopfer read a moderate number as healthy self-assertion; the Comprehensive System treats a large number as a sign of anger or resentment. Here the research is mixed, which is a good example of why each sign is a hypothesis.

Form quality: reality testing. If location tells you how the person approaches the field, form quality tells you whether what the person saw is actually there. A response whose shape fits the outline of the area used, the way most people would see it, has good form; a response that ignores or violates the outline ("a man's face" for an area with no face-like shape at all) has minus form. Form quality is therefore the Rorschach's main index of reality testing, the ability to perceive the world as it is and to tell one's own ideas from what is out there. The Comprehensive System puts it into three percentages, each worked out the same way: count the responses with a given level of form quality, divide by R, and (if you like) multiply by 100. X+%, conventional form, is the share of all responses with + or o form quality. XA%, form appropriate, is the share with +, o or u form quality, that is, every response that fits the blot whether or not it is common. X−%, distorted form, is the share with minus form quality. In Exner's 1993 adult reference tables the average X+% was .79 and the average X−% .07, so about four responses in five were conventional and fewer than one in ten distorted (in 2001 James Wood and colleagues, pooling independent samples of non-patients, found X+% much lower and X−% much higher than these figures, a story for Section 10.10). A high X−% is the classic sign of psychotic perception: the person sees what is driven by inner preoccupation rather than by the stimulus, and minus human movement responses (M−) are taken as especially serious. A very high X+% has its own, quieter meaning: a person who sees only what everybody sees, conventional to the point of rigidity.

The confabulated whole: one detail becomes everything. Rorschach noticed that some people saw a small part of the blot clearly and then, on the strength of that part alone, named the whole blot, paying little attention to the rest of it. In an invented example: "It's a bicycle, because of this little round wheel in the corner", where the wheel fits but the rest of the blot looks nothing like a bicycle. He scored such an answer not W but DW, meaning a whole arrived at from a detail, and he called it a confabulated whole response (A. K. Singh's table adds the rarer DdW, from an unusual detail, and the confabulated detail DdD; Section 10.4 gives the codes). The word is the one neurologists use for patients with Korsakoff's syndrome (Book 3 describes the amnestic disorders), who fill gaps in memory with confident inventions, and the parallel is apt. Why should a DW say anything about reality testing? Because the person has let one fragment of evidence override everything else in the stimulus, and has done so without noticing the misfit. That is exactly what impaired reality testing means: the conclusion is no longer checked against the data. Rorschach reported such answers in people of low intelligence, in organic, epileptic and schizophrenic patients, in whom the grasp of form is unclear, and in some normal subjects, and most later Rorschach workers treated a clear DW as pathological. A. K. Singh reports that Beck was the dissenter, arguing that some confabulatory answers came from bright normal adults; the general reading, and the one examiners want, is impairment in reality testing.

Content: what the person sees, and who. Content is the most intuitive family of scores and, in the Comprehensive System, the least weighted on its own. The scores that matter most are the human contents. Human responses (H), and above all whole, real human figures, are taken to show interpersonal orientation: an interest in other people and a capacity to understand them as whole, real beings. The total of human contents, SumH (H + (H) + Hd + (Hd)), shows how much the person thinks about people at all; the number of pure H responses shows how far they are seen as complete, real people rather than as parts (Hd) or as fictions and caricatures ((H), (Hd)). In Exner's 1993 adult tables non-patients gave on average a little over three pure H responses, and fewer than two pure H responses in a record of ordinary length counts as a warning sign. So when a record contains no human responses at all, the reading is poor interpersonal orientation: little interest in or understanding of people, a person whose inner world is not populated by others. The other contents have their own traditional readings: many anatomy and X-ray responses (An, Xy) suggest preoccupation with the body; a high share of animal content is read as stereotyped, unimaginative thinking; food responses (Fd) as dependency; blood (Bl) and explosions as poorly controlled affect or aggression. Finally, the popular responses (P), the dozen or so percepts given by a large share of people, show how far the person sees the world the conventional way; Exner's 1993 adults gave on average about six or seven. Few populars suggest a person out of step with ordinary perception, many a cautious conformist.

Exam trap

Know which score answers which question. Many good W responses (Comprehensive System) = organisational and integrative effort, the drive to synthesise. Reality testing is read from form quality (X−%, XA%), not from location; emotional reactivity from the colour responses and the Affective Ratio (Afr, responses to the three all-coloured last cards divided by responses to the first seven); impulse control from the balance of form-dominant to colour-dominant colour (FC : CF + C) and the stress scores taught in Section 10.5.4. So in IHBAS-style items: confabulatory (DW) responses = impaired reality testing, not "extreme lack of empathy" (empathy is read, if at all, from the human movement and human content scores), not "regressed ego" (a psychoanalytic notion of falling back to earlier, more primitive modes of functioning, which psychoanalytic Rorschach workers read from the overall pattern of a record rather than from this one sign) and not "cognitive immaturity" (vague, unintegrated responses, DQ v, are the sign of immature processing). Absence of human responses = poor interpersonal orientation, not apathy (which would be read from a short, constricted record with few movement or colour responses), not "lack of empathy" (too narrow: the score is about interest in and understanding of people in general), and therefore not "all of the above".

Movement, colour and style: the Experience Balance and Lambda

Of everything Rorschach left behind, the idea his successors kept most faithfully is a ratio between two kinds of response. Rorschach noticed that people who saw many human figures in movement (M) seemed to live much of their lives inside their own heads: in his list, more creative, with more "inner life", stable feelings and "more intensive than extensive rapport" with others, a few deep ties rather than many light ones. People who gave many colour responses seemed to live outward: more adaptable to reality, with labile feelings and "more extensive than intensive rapport", reaching out to many people and to the world. He called the balance between the two a person's Erlebnistypus, German for "experience type", the characteristic way a person experiences life. A person in whom M predominated was introversive, one in whom colour predominated was extratensive, and one with both present in about equal, large amounts he called ambiequal. If both were nearly absent, he called the type coarctated (from the Latin for "narrowed"; the English translation spells it coartated), and he found it in pedants, in depressed moods and in some long-standing psychoses; if both were present in abundance, the type was dilated.

Rorschach was careful about one word, and so should you be. He insisted that the M-type person "must not be said to be introverted, but described as capable of introversion, or introversive", because for him introversion was a mobile capacity, not a fixed trait, and a rigid, fixed introversion was pathological. His "introversive" is also not Carl Jung's introvert, and not the low scorer on Hans Eysenck's extraversion scale. An introversive person in the Rorschach sense may be warm and sociable; what defines the style is that problems are worked out in thought before they are acted on.

To compare M with colour, Rorschach needed a way of counting colour responses that recognised that they are not all alike. A form-colour response, in which shape leads and colour only adds to it ("a red butterfly"), shows feeling under the control of form; a pure colour response ("blood, because it is red") shows feeling with no control at all. He solved the problem with weights, and the Comprehensive System kept them exactly. In words: give each FC response half a point, each CF response one point and each pure C response one and a half points, and add them up. Colour naming (Cn), simply listing the colours, gets no weight at all, because it is not a response to the blot as something seen. The total is the weighted sum of colour, WSumC.

Remember

WSumC = (0.5 × FC) + (1.0 × CF) + (1.5 × C)

FC, CF and C are the numbers of form-colour, colour-form and pure colour responses; Cn is not counted.

EB = M : WSumC     EA = M + WSumC

EB is the Experience Balance, M the number of human movement responses; EA, Experience Actual, is their sum.

John Exner renamed Rorschach's Erlebnistypus the Experience Balance, EB, and wrote it as the ratio M : WSumC, with human movement on the left and weighted colour on the right. Only M goes on the left. Animal movement (FM) and inanimate movement (m) are not part of the EB; they go, with the achromatic and shading responses, into a separate ratio that Section 10.5.4 uses. Add the two sides of the EB and you get Experience Actual, EA, which Exner read as the person's available resources: the amount of organised thinking and feeling the person can bring to bear on life.

How large must the difference be before a person counts as having a style? A difference of half a point means nothing, so Exner set rules. When EA is between 4.0 and 10.0, one side of the EB must exceed the other by at least 2.0 points; when EA is greater than 10.0, by at least 2.5 points, because with more responses a bigger gap is needed to mean the same thing. If M is larger by that margin the person is introversive; if WSumC is larger, extratensive; if neither side is larger by the margin, the person is ambitent, Exner's name for Rorschach's ambiequal type. And when EA is below 4.0 there is simply too little movement and colour to judge, and no style is assigned; nor is one assigned when either side of the EB is zero. Exner's descriptions of the three styles are claims built on his research programme. The introversive style belongs to a person who prefers to delay action, think the alternatives through and rely on inner evaluation; the extratensive style to one who merges feeling with thinking, tests ideas by trying them out and learns from the results. Neither is healthier than the other; they are two different ways of solving problems. The ambitent, by contrast, has no consistent preference, and in one of Exner's problem-solving studies ambitents took longer, repeated more operations and made more errors than either of the styled groups, so Exner, unlike Rorschach, did not see the ambitent as the more flexible person.

Try the rules on two invented records. Esha, a 24-year-old, gives 22 responses with 2 M, 2 FC, 3 CF and 1 pure C. Her WSumC is (0.5 × 2) + (1.0 × 3) + (1.5 × 1) = 1.0 + 3.0 + 1.5 = 5.5. Her EB is 2 : 5.5 and her EA is 2 + 5.5 = 7.5, inside the 4.0 to 10.0 band, so she needs a gap of 2.0. Eight of her responses are pure form, so her Lambda is 8 ÷ (22 − 8) = 8 ÷ 14 = .57, well under 1.0, and nothing overrides the EB. The gap is 5.5 − 2 = 3.5, on the colour side, so her style is extratensive. A record with 4 M and a WSumC of 3.5, by contrast, also has an EA of 7.5, but the gap is only 0.5, so that person is ambitent.

There is one more number that can overrule the EB, and it is about the responses that have no movement or colour at all. Lambda (written L, after the Greek letter) compares the pure form responses with all the rest. In words: count the responses whose only determinant is F, then divide that number by the number of responses that have any other determinant. As a formula:

Remember

Lambda = F ÷ (R − F)

F is the number of pure form responses and R the total number of responses, so R − F is the number of responses with at least one other determinant. Do not confuse it with F%, which is F ÷ R.

Why compare pure form with the rest? A pure F response is the most economical answer possible: the person attends to shape and nothing else, ignoring colour, shading and the movement a figure might suggest. Everybody gives some, and Exner's 1993 adult tables put the average Lambda at about .58, so a typical person gives a little over one pure form response for every two richer ones. A person with a high Lambda, 1.0 or more (Exner's rule is "greater than .99"), gives at least as many pure form answers as all the others put together. Exner read this as an avoidant style: a habit of narrowing and simplifying the field, economising on effort, dealing with complexity by not looking at it. It can be efficient, and it can also miss a great deal. In the fourth edition of his first volume (2003) Exner treated high Lambda as a style in its own right that takes precedence over the EB, so a high-Lambda person with an introversive EB is described as avoidant-introversive. Very low Lambda suggests the opposite problem: a person who cannot leave any complexity alone and gets caught up in every shade and colour. A high Lambda also has an innocent explanation that the examiner must rule out first: a guarded person who wants to give nothing away will produce a short record full of pure form answers.

Take a third invented record. Nirmal, seen for a court-ordered assessment, gives only 15 responses, of which 11 are pure form; the other four are 1 M, 2 FC and 1 FM. His Lambda is 11 ÷ (15 − 11) = 11 ÷ 4 = 2.75, nearly three times the cut-off: a high-Lambda, avoidant record. His EB is 1 : 1.0 (two FC at half a point each), and his EA is 2.0, below 4.0, so no EB style can be assigned anyway. Figure 10.11 places Esha and Nirmal, and Brinda from the next subsection, on one map.

Exam trap

EB is M : WSumC, human movement against weighted colour; it is not M against FM, and FM and m belong elsewhere. The weights are 0.5 for FC, 1.0 for CF and 1.5 for C; Cn gets none. Lambda is F ÷ (R − F), not F ÷ R (that is F%). Rorschach's introversive is not Jung's or Eysenck's introvert: it is a problem-solving style, not shyness. "Ambiequal" is Rorschach's word and "ambitent" Exner's for the same middle type, and "coarctated" means both M and colour are nearly absent.

A worked Structural Summary: Brinda's record

Nothing makes the Structural Summary clear like doing one. Brinda is an invented client: a 27-year-old research scholar referred by a university counselling centre because of anxiety before her thesis defence and long, painful indecision over small choices. The referral question was what her usual way of coping looks like, and whether anything in her functioning suggests a more serious disturbance. She gave the 20 responses coded in Table 10.19. To keep the test secure, the table gives only the codes, not what she said, and it does not say which card each response came from; neither is needed for the arithmetic.

Table 10.19 Brinda's invented record: the sequence of scores, reduced to the codes this section uses (card numbers and wording omitted; a and p mark active and passive movement).
No.Location and DQDeterminantsForm qualityContent (P = popular)
1W+Ma.FCoH, Cg
2W+MaoH, P
3W+FMaoA, P
4W+Mp.FC+(H)
5WoFoA, P
6WoFoA, P

8WvCFuNa

11DoFoA, P

20DdoF−An

Step 1: is the record interpretable? Count the rows: R = 20. That is above Exner's floor of 14 and inside the ordinary range, so the record can be read.

Step 2: location and its balance. Count each location: responses 1 to 8 are W, so W = 8; responses 9 to 18 are D, so D = 10 (response 16 uses white space as well, so S = 1); responses 19 and 20 are Dd, so Dd = 2. Check: 8 + 10 + 2 = 20. The share of whole responses is, in words, the number of W divided by R, times 100:

W% = (W ÷ R) × 100 = (8 ÷ 20) × 100 = 0.40 × 100 = 40 per cent.

The Economy Index is W : D : Dd = 8 : 10 : 2, and the Aspirational Index W : M is 8 : 5 (there are five M, counted in Step 4). Four of the eight W are synthesised (W+), and only one is vague. The developmental quality totals are + = 6 (responses 1, 2, 3, 4, 9 and 10), v = 1 (response 8) and o = 13.

Step 3: Lambda. The pure F responses are numbers 5, 6, 11, 12, 15, 16, 19 and 20, so F = 8, and R − F = 20 − 8 = 12 responses have some other determinant. Then:

Lambda = F ÷ (R − F) = 8 ÷ 12 = 0.6667, which rounds to .67.

For comparison, F% = F ÷ R = 8 ÷ 20 = .40. (The two are tied together: Lambda = F% ÷ (1 − F%) = .40 ÷ .60 = .67.)

Step 4: the movement and colour counts. Read down the determinant column and count each determinant wherever it appears, including inside a blend. M appears in responses 1, 2, 4, 9 and 18, so M = 5. FC appears in responses 1, 4 and 13, so FC = 3 (two of the three are inside the blends M.FC). CF appears once, in response 8. There is no pure C and no Cn. So:

WSumC = (0.5 × FC) + (1.0 × CF) + (1.5 × C) = (0.5 × 3) + (1.0 × 1) + (1.5 × 0) = 1.5 + 1.0 + 0 = 2.5.

The form-colour ratio, FC : CF + C, is 3 : (1 + 0) = 3 : 1.

Step 5: the Experience Balance and the style. EB = M : WSumC = 5 : 2.5, and EA = 5 + 2.5 = 7.5. Now apply the rules in order. Lambda is .67, below 1.0, so there is no avoidant style to override the EB. EA is 7.5, between 4.0 and 10.0, so the gap needed is 2.0 points. The gap is 5 − 2.5 = 2.5 points, on the M side, which is more than 2.0. Brinda's style is introversive.

Step 6: demands against resources. The other movement and the shading go into Exner's second ratio, eb, whose two sides add up to experienced stimulation, es: in words, add the animal and inanimate movement to all the achromatic and shading responses. Brinda has FM = 2 (responses 3 and 10), m = 1 (response 14), C' = 1 (response 7), T = 1 (response 17) and no V or Y, so es = 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 0 + 0 = 5. Exner read es as the demands pressing on the person, from needs and from painful feelings, and set it against EA, the resources. The difference EA − es = 7.5 − 5 = 2.5 is converted with a table into the D score: differences from −2.5 to +2.5 give a D of 0, and each further step of 2.5 points moves it one point up or down. Brinda's D is 0: by this reading, her resources and the demands on her are roughly in balance, and she is not overloaded.

Step 7: form quality. Count the form quality column: + = 2 (responses 4 and 9), o = 13, u = 3 (responses 8, 14 and 16) and minus = 2 (responses 19 and 20). Check: 2 + 13 + 3 + 2 = 20. Then:

X+% = (+ and o) ÷ R = (2 + 13) ÷ 20 = 15 ÷ 20 = .75

XA% = (+, o and u) ÷ R = (2 + 13 + 3) ÷ 20 = 18 ÷ 20 = .90

X−% = minus ÷ R = 2 ÷ 20 = .10

Step 8: people and conventionality. The human contents are H in responses 1, 2 and 9, (H) in response 4 and Hd in response 18, so SumH = 3 + 1 + 1 = 5 and pure H = 3. There are 6 populars, and her full sequence of scores contains no special scores for odd verbalisation or faulty logic. Table 10.20 collects the results.

Table 10.20 Brinda's Structural Summary at a glance: the main scores of this section, how each is computed, her value and what each is claimed to show.
