PCount of populars6Conventionality

Now say it in plain words, the way a careful clinician would, as a set of hypotheses. Brinda's record is of ordinary length and gives the examiner plenty to work with, which suggests she was not guarded during the test. She puts real effort into making sense of things as a whole: two responses in five use the whole blot, more than the one in three that the usual W : D ratio of 1 : 2 implies, and half of those are well-organised syntheses, so she is an ambitious, effortful organiser, perhaps one who finds it hard to settle for a quick, partial answer. Her Lambda is close to the average, so she neither shuts out complexity nor drowns in it. Her introversive EB suggests that she prefers to think problems through before acting, which fits the long deliberation her counsellor described, and which is a style rather than a disorder. Her feelings are mostly expressed under the control of form (FC outnumbers CF + C by three to one), and her D score of 0 suggests that she is coping with her current load. Her perception is adequate: three responses in four are conventional, nine in ten fit the blot, and her two minus responses both came on small, unusual details, so her perception slips, if at all, when she fixes on odd corners of a situation. She is interested in people and sees them as whole and real (five human contents, three of them pure H, and five human movement responses), and she sees what most people see. Nothing in the record points to disordered thinking or a psychotic process. Every one of these sentences is a claim of the system, of varying strength (Section 10.10 weighs them), and each is worth writing down only if the interview, the history and the other tests point the same way. Figure 10.11 places her on the Experience Balance map beside Esha and Nirmal.

Clinical link

A Rorschach report never says "Brinda is introversive and has good reality testing" as if these were measured facts like her height. It says what the record suggests and how far the other evidence agrees: "Her Rorschach suggests a reflective style that prefers to think problems through before acting, consistent with her own account of long deliberation; there were no signs of perceptual distortion or disordered thinking." Writing findings as tested hypotheses protects the client from over-reading, and it protects the report from the vague, flattering statements that fit everybody (the Barnum effect, Book 2, Section 2.9.9). And no diagnosis is ever made from the Rorschach alone.

Exam trap

The arithmetic slips examiners like: count a blend's determinants separately (M.FC adds one to M and one to FC); leave Cn out of WSumC; divide F by R − F, not by R, for Lambda; check Lambda before EB, because a Lambda over .99 means the avoidant style comes first; and check EA before naming a style, because the gap needed is 2.0 when EA is 4.0 to 10.0, 2.5 when EA is over 10.0, and no style is called when EA is under 4.0. X+% counts + and o; XA% adds u; X−% counts minus alone.

After Rorschach: five American schools

Everything in the last four subsections presupposes that somebody has settled which codes exist, how each is defined and what each ratio means. For fifty years nobody had. Hermann Rorschach died in April 1922, months after Psychodiagnostik appeared, and he left a method still under construction: a handful of codes, a theory of experience type and a last paper on applying the test, published after his death by his colleague Emil Oberholzer. The method crossed the Atlantic with the American psychiatrist David M. Levy, usually credited with bringing it to the United States in the mid-1920s, and there it was taken up by several energetic people who never agreed on how to use it. By the 1950s there were five American systems, and they were different enough that a "Rorschach" given in Chicago and one given in Los Angeles were not quite the same test. Figure 10.12 lays out the century.

The first and the most rigorous of the five was Samuel Jacob Beck (1896 to 1980). Beck, who also studied the method in Zurich with Oberholzer, wrote the first American research paper on the test in 1930, took his doctorate at Columbia University in 1932, and set out his method in Introduction to the Rorschach Method (1937), followed from 1944 by the volumes of Rorschach's Test. His instinct was the psychometrician's. A detail area counted as a common D only if it was used often in real records; a form was good only if it matched the forms that normal people saw there, which meant building frequency tables; and organising activity was scored with numbers, the Z score that Exner later took over. Beck distrusted anything that depended on the scorer's intuition, and he did not hide it.

The intuition he distrusted belonged above all to Bruno Klopfer (1900 to 1971). Klopfer, who had a doctorate from Munich, left Germany in 1933 as a Jew, spent a year in Zurich, where he first met the test, and reached Columbia University, where he worked for a time with the anthropologist Franz Boas. He taught Rorschach seminars, and in 1936 he founded the Rorschach Research Exchange, the journal that became the Journal of Projective Techniques and, in 1971, the Journal of Personality Assessment. With the psychiatrist Douglas M. Kelley, the Nuremberg examiner of this chapter's opening, he wrote The Rorschach Technique (1942), a manual from which a generation of clinicians learned the test. Klopfer's approach was phenomenological and clinical: he wanted the scoring to capture the experience behind a response, so he multiplied the codes (separate symbols for animal movement and for the kinds of shading, and a separate scoring of achromatic colour), rated the level of form on a scale rather than as simply good or bad, added testing the limits to the administration (Section 10.3), and summarised a record in a bar chart of its determinants that he called the psychogram. To Beck, many of these innovations were unreliable additions with no normative base, and the two men criticised each other's methods in print from the late 1930s. The quarrel split American Rorschach work into camps that lasted for decades.

Between them stood Marguerite Rosenberg Hertz (1899 to 1992), who took her doctorate at Western Reserve University in Cleveland in 1932 and taught there from 1938 to 1970. Her system leaned, like Beck's, on carefully compiled frequency tables for judging form, but she was open to some of Klopfer's ideas, and she is often described as the person who tried to keep the two camps talking; she was a founding member, and in 1940 to 1941 president, of the society that is now the Society for Personality Assessment. Zygmunt A. Piotrowski (1904 to 1985), born in Poland, made his name in 1937 with a list of ten Rorschach signs of organic brain disease, the "organic signs", and later set out his own system in Perceptanalysis (1957). Several codes in today's systems are his: A. K. Singh credits him with the symbol m for inanimate movement and the symbol Cn for colour naming (both 1936), and he was among the first to try to computerise Rorschach interpretation in the 1950s and 1960s.

The fifth school came from psychoanalysis. David Rapaport (1911 to 1960), a Hungarian-born psychologist, worked at the Menninger Clinic in Topeka, Kansas, with Merton Gill and Roy Schafer (1922 to 2018), and their two-volume Diagnostic Psychological Testing (1945 to 1946) did two new things. It treated the Rorschach as one test in a battery, read alongside the Wechsler-Bellevue, word association and the TAT, and it read the results through psychoanalytic ego psychology, asking what each response showed about the ego's ways of thinking, defending and adapting. Its most lasting gift was a careful catalogue of deviant verbalisations, the odd, illogical or bizarre ways some patients talk about what they see (a "fabulised combination" that merges two percepts impossibly, a "contamination" that fuses two ideas into one), which is the ancestor of the Comprehensive System's special scores for disordered thinking. Rapaport's group also differed in method, preferring an inquiry after each card instead of after all ten. Schafer carried the approach further in Psychoanalytic Interpretation in Rorschach Testing (1954), a classic of thematic reading.

Exam trap

The recognised Rorschach scoring systems are those of Beck, Klopfer, Hertz, Piotrowski, Rapaport and Schafer, then Exner's Comprehensive System and R-PAS. There is no "Murray system": Henry Murray gave us the Thematic Apperception Test (Section 10.6), not a way of scoring inkblots. Pair the names with their signatures: Beck, the empiricist with frequency tables and the Z score; Klopfer, the clinician with the Rorschach Research Exchange, the psychogram and testing the limits; Piotrowski, the organic signs and Perceptanalysis; Rapaport, Gill and Schafer, the Menninger test battery and deviant verbalisations.

Exner's Comprehensive System

By the 1960s the Rorschach was one of the most widely used tests in American clinics and also, scientifically, a mess. Research findings from one school could not be checked by another, because the scores were not defined the same way; the same response could be coded differently in different systems, and many clinicians used no formal scoring at all. John E. Exner Jr. (1928 to 2006) set out to sort it out. Born in Syracuse, New York, he took his doctorate in clinical psychology at Cornell University in 1958, knew Beck, Klopfer and Hertz personally, and spent the rest of his career on the test, later directing his Rorschach Workshops in Asheville, North Carolina. His solution was not to found a sixth school but to build one system out of the best-supported parts of the existing five.

Key study: Exner compares the five systems (1969 to 1974)

The question. Were the five American systems five versions of one test, or five different tests? The method. In The Rorschach Systems (1969) Exner laid the systems side by side and traced how each had developed, comparing them with each other and with Rorschach's own scoring. In 1972, with D. E. Exner, he surveyed practising clinicians on how they actually used the test ("How clinicians use the Rorschach", Journal of Personality Assessment). The result. The systems overlapped far less than their shared name suggested, and in practice many clinicians scored loosely or not at all, often mixing elements of different systems with their own preferences. The conclusion. In The Rorschach: A Comprehensive System, volume 1 (1974), Exner kept an element from any of the older systems only if it could be scored reliably by different examiners (the .85 agreement hurdle of Chapter 3, Section 3.6.9) and had research evidence behind it, standardised the administration, and collected norms. The criticism. Critics later argued that the system's norms made healthy adults look disturbed and that many of its variables had weak validity; that argument belongs to Section 10.10.

The Comprehensive System (CS) soon became the dominant way of giving, scoring and reading the Rorschach, in America and far beyond. Its parts will now be familiar. The administration is fixed: the person and examiner sit side by side, the instruction is simply "What might this be?", every word is recorded, and the inquiry follows the response phase (Section 10.3 gives the details). The codes take their area definitions from Beck, borrow freely from the others (animal movement and achromatic colour from Klopfer, inanimate movement from Piotrowski, the special scores from Rapaport's deviant verbalisations) and are defined in a single manual with tables of common areas and of form quality. The codes are added into the sequence of scores and the Structural Summary, and the summary is read against normative tables. Later, as Exner's research accumulated, came the rule that a record must have at least 14 responses, the interpretive search strategy, and the constellations.

A constellation works like a checklist. Each one lists a set of conditions on the Structural Summary, and the index is positive when enough of them are met; the Suicide Constellation, S-CON, for example, has 12 conditions and is positive when 8 or more are present. The idea is that no single sign is trusted, but a cluster of signs that each, on their own, slightly raise a risk may together point to it. Table 10.21 lists them. The best known change came in 2000, when Exner replaced the Schizophrenia Index (SCZI) with the Perceptual-Thinking Index (PTI), designed to measure disordered perception and thinking more accurately, under a name that no longer suggests that a positive score is a diagnosis. The first volume reached its fourth edition in 2003. Exner died of leukaemia in 2006, and the system was then frozen: about two years later his heirs decided that it would not be developed further, which is part of the reason a successor was built.

Table 10.21 The constellations (special indices) of the Comprehensive System and what a positive index is claimed to flag.
IndexFull nameWhat a positive index is claimed to flag
PTIPerceptual-Thinking IndexInaccurate perception and disordered thinking, as in psychosis; replaced SCZI in 2000
SCZISchizophrenia IndexThe older index for the same problems; too many false positives
DEPIDepression IndexFeatures associated with depression or affective disturbance
CDICoping Deficit IndexLimited social and coping skills; vulnerability in relationships
S-CONSuicide ConstellationFeatures found in people who later killed themselves; positive at 8 or more of 12
HVIHypervigilance IndexA guarded, mistrustful, always-on-watch way of meeting the world
OBSObsessive Style IndexA perfectionistic, detail-bound style of processing
Exam trap

The Comprehensive System (1974) is John Exner's, not Beck's, Klopfer's or Piotrowski's, although it borrowed from all of them. It is a system for scoring the Rorschach: not the MMPI (an objective inventory with its own scales, Chapter 9), not the TAT (scored through need and press or through systems such as McClelland's, Section 10.6) and not the Wechsler scales (Chapter 6). It scores location, determinants, form quality, content, populars and special scores and builds a Structural Summary, but it gives no IQ score. The PTI is the renamed and revised SCZI, so an older paper may call it the "schizophrenia index"; S-CON is the suicide constellation, DEPI depression, CDI coping deficit, HVI hypervigilance and OBS obsessive style.

After Exner: R-PAS, and Holtzman's different answer

Even before Exner died, the people closest to his research had begun to see three problems that a frozen system could not fix. The first was evidence: a long series of critiques had argued that many Comprehensive System variables had little validity, and a large meta-analysis of the system's variables led by Joni Mihura (published in 2013 and discussed in Section 10.10) would later sort the well-supported variables from the poorly supported ones. The second was the norms, which in independent samples made ordinary adults look disturbed. The third was R itself. Because the person decides how many responses to give, records vary enormously in length, from the minimum of 14 to 40 or more, and many scores rise and fall with length; Gregory Meyer showed in 1993 that R was significantly related to 26 of the 29 count-based criteria of the constellations. A long record has more chances to produce every kind of sign, so the same person could look healthier or sicker depending on how talkative they were that day.

The answer was the Rorschach Performance Assessment System (R-PAS), whose manual appeared in 2011, written by Gregory J. Meyer, Donald J. Viglione, Joni L. Mihura, Robert E. Erard and Philip Erdberg; four of the five had sat on Exner's Rorschach Research Council, and the fifth, Erard, was a forensic psychologist. It uses the same ten cards and much of the same coding, but it changes three things. First, it controls R with R-Optimized administration. Before the first card the examiner asks the person to give "two, or maybe three" responses to each card. If the person gives only one, the examiner prompts for another, reminding them of the request (and accepts one if no more comes); if the person gives four, the examiner pulls the card, reminding them of the request and moving on to the next card. Studies comparing the two administrations found that R-PAS raises R slightly and, more importantly, makes it much less variable, with little effect on most other scores. Second, R-PAS keeps only variables with research support, and lays them out on two profile pages, Page 1 for the variables with the strongest support and Page 2 for those with less, each page grouped into the same five domains: administration behaviours and observations; engagement and cognitive processing; perception and thinking problems; stress and distress; and self and other representation. Lambda, whose statistical properties made it awkward to analyse, gives way to F%, pure form divided by R. Third, every score is reported against an international reference sample of adult records gathered in the United States, Europe, Israel, Argentina and Brazil, as a standard score with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15 and as a percentile (Chapter 5 explains both). Exner did not write R-PAS, which appeared five years after his death, although the R-PAS developers list him among the contributors of the American records in its reference data.

Long before either system, an American psychologist had asked a more radical question: if the Rorschach's statistical problems come from the test itself, why not build a better test? Wayne H. Holtzman (1923 to 2019), who worked at the University of Texas at Austin from 1949 to 1993, and his colleagues Joseph S. Thorpe, Jon D. Swartz and E. Wayne Herron published the Holtzman Inkblot Technique (HIT) in Inkblot Perception and Personality (1961). It has two parallel forms, A and B, of 45 inkblots each, with two practice blots common to both forms. Some blots are chromatic and some achromatic, and unlike Rorschach's they vary in how symmetrical they are. The crucial rule is that the person gives only one response to each blot, followed at once by a brief standardised inquiry. That single rule removes the problem R-PAS later attacked with prompts and pulls: every record has the same number of responses, so scores can be compared directly from person to person. Responses are scored on 22 variables, many of them borrowed from the Rorschach literature (location, form appropriateness, colour, shading, movement and so on), with norms. Because there are two equivalent forms, the HIT can do what the Rorschach never could, give a true alternate-form reliability coefficient (Chapter 3, Section 3.3 explains parallel forms), and it can even be given to groups. It was widely praised by psychometricians and much less widely used by clinicians, who stayed with the familiar ten cards. In Lindzey's scheme it is, like the Rorschach, an association technique (Section 10.2).

Table 10.22 gathers every system in this section, from Rorschach's own unfinished scheme to the HIT.

Table 10.22 The ways of scoring and reading inkblots.
SystemAuthor(s)Key year(s)What is distinctive
Rorschach's ownHermann Rorschach1921A few codes, experience type (M against colour), DW; left unfinished at his death in 1922
BeckSamuel J. Beck1937; volumes from 1944Strictly empirical: frequency tables for areas and form, the organisational Z score
KlopferBruno Klopfer, with Douglas M. Kelley1942Clinical and phenomenological: many shading codes, form level rating, testing the limits, psychogram
HertzMarguerite R. Hertz1930s onFrequency tables for form; a middle way between Beck and Klopfer
PiotrowskiZygmunt A. Piotrowski1937 (organic signs); 1957Perceptanalysis; the codes m and Cn; organic signs
Rapaport and SchaferDavid Rapaport, Merton Gill, Roy Schafer1945 to 1946; 1954Psychoanalytic ego psychology; the test battery; deviant verbalisations
Comprehensive SystemJohn E. Exner Jr.1974; 4th edition 2003Synthesis of the five; norms; Structural Summary; R of at least 14; constellations; no IQ

Exam trap

R-PAS (2011) is by Meyer, Viglione, Mihura, Erard and Erdberg, not by Exner, who died in 2006. Its R-Optimized administration asks for two, maybe three responses per card, prompts after one and pulls after four. The Holtzman (1961) is a different test, not a Rorschach scoring system: two parallel forms of 45 blots (not 10, and not one form of 90), one response per blot, which is what makes alternate-form reliability possible. Do not mix up the two fixes for the variable-R problem: Holtzman fixes R at one per card; R-PAS steers it with prompts and pulls.
