10.6 The Thematic Apperception TestHigh yield · 11 Qs

The Rorschach asks a person what a blot might be. The test in this section asks a different question: what is happening between these people, what led up to it, and how will it end? Show a dozen people the same picture of two figures at a garden gate and you will hear a dozen different stories. One teller has the younger figure leaving home in disgrace, another has her returning in triumph, a third has the older figure dying and the younger one too late to say goodbye. The picture is the same, so the differences must come from the tellers. If you listen to twenty stories from one person, the same kinds of wishes, fears, helpers and enemies keep coming back, and they begin to sketch the person who is telling them. That is the bet behind the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT): a set of pictures of people in ambiguous situations, about which the person tells stories that are then read for the needs, conflicts and expectations they reveal. It is the second most famous projective technique after the Rorschach, and in Lindzey's classification it is the model construction technique, because the person must build something, a story, rather than simply name a percept (Section 10.2). This section tells where the test came from, how it is given, how its stories are read, first clinically and then by counting, and how it differs from the inkblots.

From magazine pictures to a test: Morgan, Murray and the Harvard Psychological Clinic

Asking people to tell stories about pictures was not a new idea in the 1930s. In 1907 H. L. Brittain published "A study in imagination" in The Pedagogical Seminary. He showed nine pictures to boys and girls aged 13 to 20 and asked them to write the stories the pictures suggested. He found clear sex differences: the girls' stories were full of pity, sadness and the fear of being left alone, which Brittain blamed on a society that blocked girls' normal social and physical activity. A year later W. Libby reported that younger children told "objective" stories, describing what they saw, while older children told "subjective" ones, full of thoughts and feelings, with the change coming at about thirteen or fourteen. In the early 1930s L. A. Schwartz, a psychiatrist at the Clinic for Juvenile Research in Detroit, built a Social Situation Picture Test of eight pictures of situations common in the histories of delinquent boys, to get his interviews started when there was no time to build rapport. None of these caught on. The method had to wait for a theory that said what the stories should be read for, and that theory came from Harvard.

The theory was the personology of Henry Alexander Murray (1893 to 1988), the physician turned psychologist who ran the Harvard Psychological Clinic. Book 1, Section 10.6.1 teaches his ideas: a need is a force inside the person that organises perception and action towards a goal; a press is a force in the environment that helps or hinders a need; and a combination of the two that keeps recurring in a life is a thema. Murray also distinguished manifest needs, shown openly in behaviour, from latent needs, shown only in fantasy and dreams. If some needs show themselves mainly in fantasy, then a psychologist who wants to measure them needs a way of collecting fantasies on demand. In Explorations in Personality (1938) Murray and his colleagues reported an intensive study of a group of young men, each examined by the whole clinic staff with interviews, experiments and a battery of new techniques. The picture-story test was one of those techniques.

Its co-creator was Christiana Drummond Morgan (1897 to 1967), a gifted amateur artist who had gone to Zurich in 1926 to be analysed by Carl Jung (Jung later used her visions in his seminars of 1930 to 1934) and who then worked beside Murray at the clinic. The story usually told about the test's origin is that a Radcliffe student of abnormal psychology, Cecilia Roberts (Murray himself, in a 1950 lecture, credited her with "the germinal suggestion"), mentioned that her son, ill in bed, had spent a day making up stories about the pictures in magazines, and asked whether pictures could be used in the clinic. Murray and Morgan collected pictures from magazine illustrations, photographs and works of art, and Morgan drew or redrew several of them herself; the historian of the test, Wesley G. Morgan, has traced the sources of the pictures one by one, and at least one card of the final set is a drawing by the artist Samuel Thal, made from a photograph.

In 1935 Morgan and Murray published "A method for investigating fantasies: the Thematic Apperception Test" in the Archives of Neurology and Psychiatry, with Morgan as first author. Their reasoning will sound familiar from Section 10.1: a person asked to interpret an ambiguous social situation is likely to reveal his own personality while doing so, and because he thinks he is talking about the picture he is less defensive and less aware of being scrutinised. They also reported a lesson from their early trials. At first they had asked people simply to interpret the action in each picture and to guess what had happened before and what would happen next. They found that far more was revealed when the person was asked to make up a dramatic story. The test went through several sets of pictures, known as Series A, B and C, before the final Series D appeared in 1943 with a manual from Harvard University Press. That version is still the one in use.

The 1943 cards and manual were credited to "Henry A. Murray, M.D., and the Staff of the Harvard Psychological Clinic". Morgan's name had gone. Murray said many years later, in 1985, that Morgan herself had asked to have her name removed as senior author, because she disliked the academic correspondence that came with it; historians have since argued over how far her part was played down because she was a woman. Most textbooks, and every exam paper you will meet, now credit the TAT to Morgan and Murray, 1935, with the 1943 manual as Murray's.

Why "apperception"? The word was introduced by the philosopher Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646 to 1716) and made central to the psychology of learning by Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776 to 1841), for whom every new idea is taken in and coloured by the "apperceptive mass" of ideas already in the mind. Apperception is therefore perception plus interpretation: perceiving something in the light of one's past experience, needs and expectations. "Thematic" points to the themas the stories carry. The name says exactly what the test exploits: the person does not merely see the picture, he apperceives it, and the way he does so carries his own themes into the story. Bellak's later name for this process, apperceptive distortion, is explained in Section 10.1.

Exam trap

The TAT is by Henry A. Murray and Christiana D. Morgan, Harvard Psychological Clinic, published in 1935 (the 1930s), final set and manual 1943. The paper spells her name "Christina"; it is Christiana. The baits: Sigmund Freud in the 1920s devised no picture test (his idea of projection lies behind the whole family, Section 10.1); Carl Jung's test is the word association test, from 1904 to 1910, not anything in the 1940s (Section 10.8); B. F. Skinner in the 1950s was studying operant conditioning (oddly, he did build an auditory projective device, the verbal summator, in 1936, but never a picture test); Hermann Rorschach made the inkblots (1921); John Exner made the Comprehensive System for scoring the Rorschach (1974); Leopold Bellak made the Children's Apperception Test (1949) and a TAT scoring scheme, not the TAT itself.

The cards and how the test is given

The full TAT is a set of 31 cards: 30 black-and-white pictures and one blank card, card 16. Most of the pictures show one or more people in situations that could be read in many ways; a few show landscapes or scenes with no clear human figure. The cards are numbered 1 to 20, and some numbers come in more than one version, marked with letters for the kind of person the card was meant for: B for boys, G for girls, M for males over 14 and F for females over 14. A card with no letter is used for everybody.

Why 31 cards if a person is given only 20? Count them, as Figure 10.13 does. Eleven cards (1, 2, 4, 5, 10, 11, 14, 15, 16, 19 and 20) are the same for everyone. Seven cards (3, 6, 7, 8, 9, 17 and 18) come in two forms each, one marked BM (for boys and men) and one marked GF (for girls and women), which gives 7 × 2 = 14 cards. Card 12 comes in three forms (12M, 12F and 12BG) and card 13 in three more (13MF, 13B and 13G), which adds 3 + 3 = 6. The whole set is therefore 11 + 14 + 6 = 31 cards. Any one person receives one card for each number: the 11 universal cards, one form of each of the 7 two-form cards, one form of card 12 and one of card 13, so 11 + 7 + 1 + 1 = 20 cards. That is how the same box serves four groups, men, women, boys and girls, with a set of 20 each, and it is why the numbers 31, 30 and 20 all appear in exam options.

Murray's manual gave the 20 cards in two sessions of ten, with at least a day between them, each session lasting about an hour. The pictures of the second series were deliberately more unusual, dramatic and bizarre, and the instructions for the second session invited the person to give freer rein to imagination, "as in a myth, fairy story, or allegory". The seating was left to the person: Murray suggested that the person sit or lie with his back to the examiner, as on the analyst's couch, while David Rapaport preferred the two to face each other.

The instructions matter, because they define the task. Murray's Form A, for adolescents and adults of average intelligence and education, begins: "This is a test of imagination, one form of intelligence. I am going to show you some pictures, one at a time, and your task will be to make up as dramatic a story as you can for each. Tell what has led up to the event shown in the picture, describe what is happening at the moment, what the characters are feeling and thinking; and then give the outcome." Notice two things. First, the instruction asks for a complete story with a past, a present and a future, plus the characters' thoughts and feelings: a beginning, a middle and an end. Second, it describes the test as a test of imagination and intelligence, which it is not. That is the disguised purpose of Section 10.1 in its plainest form: the person concentrates on being imaginative and does not notice that his own wishes and fears are being collected. Murray budgeted about five minutes a story, so that ten fitted into the hour. A simpler Form B was written for children, for adults with little education and for psychotic patients: "This is a storytelling test ... Tell what has happened before and what is happening now. Say what the people are feeling and thinking and how it will come out."

The blank card has its own instruction: "See what you can see on this blank card. Imagine some picture there and describe it to me in detail." If the person cannot, the examiner says, "Close your eyes and picture something," and once the scene has been described, "Now tell me a story about it." Card 16 is the least structured stimulus in the whole projective family, the far end of the structure continuum of Section 10.1, because there is nothing on it at all. Some people find it impossible; others produce their most personal story of the series.

During the stories the examiner says very little. Murray advised praising the first story and reminding the person of the instructions, then staying quiet except to encourage, to keep time and to prompt briefly if a crucial part is missing ("And how does it end?"). The examiner does not answer questions about what the picture "really" shows, because the person's reading of it is the data. Every story is recorded verbatim, by hand or on audio, including hesitations, asides and comments about the picture ("This is a strange one"). After the last story the examiner may hold a short inquiry, asking, for instance, where a plot came from (a film, a friend's life, one's own), or following up a lead from the stories with careful, non-leading questions.

That is the classical procedure. Almost nobody gives it in full today. In ordinary clinical practice the psychologist selects a subset of cards, chosen for the referral question and for the person's age and sex, and gives them in a single session: most clinicians use somewhere between about 5 and 12 cards, and 8 to 12 is the figure most often quoted. A clinician who wants to know how a young man sees his father picks cards that pull for parent and child themes; one who is worried about suicidal thinking picks cards that pull for loss and despair. A selection saves time, but it has a cost you will meet again in Section 10.10: if every clinician uses a different set of cards, nobody's results can be compared with anybody else's norms.

Exam trap

The full TAT has 31 cards: 30 pictures plus the blank card 16. 20 is the number Murray gave one person, in two sessions of 10, at least a day apart. In a typical session today a clinician uses about 5 to 12 selected cards (RML 2025 keyed "5 to 12"; "8 to 12" is the common textbook figure and falls inside it). "1 to 3" is too few for a theme to recur; "20 to 31" describes the whole kit, never one sitting; "over 50" matches no version of the test. The blank card belongs to the TAT: the Rorschach has ten cards and no blank one.

Reading a story through needs and press: Murray's scheme

How do you turn a pile of stories into a description of a person? Murray's answer rests on one assumption: the teller identifies with one character in each story, and lends that character his own needs and his own way of seeing the world. That character is the hero, the figure the story is mainly about, usually the one whose thoughts and feelings are described and who most resembles the teller in age and sex. Once the hero is found, the rest of the analysis follows the vocabulary of Book 1, Section 10.6.1, now used as a coding scheme. Figure 10.14 shows it as a flow.

The hero's needs are what he wants and strives for in the story: to succeed at something difficult (n Ach), to be with and be liked by others (n Aff), to attack or get even (n Agg), to control others (n Dom), to look after someone weaker (n Nurturance), and so on through Murray's list in Book 1. The press is whatever the environment of the story does to the hero or offers him: a rejecting parent, a rival, an accident, a kind teacher, a lack of money. Murray wrote press with a "p", as in p Rejection, p Aggression, p Dominance or p Nurturance. His distinction between alpha press, the environment as it objectively is, and beta press, the environment as the person perceives it, is the heart of the method. A TAT story shows only beta press. If every parent in a man's stories is cold and every boss unfair, you have learned how he sees parents and bosses, not how his own parents and bosses actually behave; that has to come from the history and from people who know him.

The interaction of a need and a press, the unit of plot, is the thema: "the hero wants to be accepted (n Aff), is rejected by the group (p Rejection), and withdraws" is a thema. The outcome is how the story ends and, above all, how it comes to end that way. Does the hero succeed by his own effort, by luck, or because someone rescues him? Does he fail, give up, get punished, or die? Murray read outcomes as a window on the teller's expectations: a person whose heroes always fail whatever they do, or always need to be rescued, may expect the same of his own life.

Murray's manual made this a semi-quantitative procedure. The interpreter rated the strength of each need and each press in every story on a scale from 1 to 5, taking into account its frequency, intensity, duration and importance in the plot. The ratings for each variable were then added across the 20 stories, so that the interpreter could see which needs and which press stood out for this person. The most frequent and most intense themas, and the typical outcomes, completed the picture. The method was thorough and slow, and few clinicians outside research ever used the ratings in full; most kept Murray's vocabulary and dropped his arithmetic.

One warning runs through every good account of the TAT. A need that is strong in fantasy is not necessarily strong in behaviour. A quiet, polite clerk whose stories are full of murders may be a man who keeps his aggression in fantasy precisely because he never acts on it; another man's violent stories may describe exactly what he does on Saturday nights. The stories tell you what is on the person's mind, the latent needs; how those needs reach behaviour, or are held back from it, must be judged from other evidence. This is the central puzzle of TAT interpretation, and it is also a central question for the test's validity (Section 10.10).

Exam trap

The TAT rests on Murray's need-press theory of psychogenic needs and environmental press, and on the projective assumption that people project their needs and conflicts onto ambiguous pictures. Each bait belongs to someone else: personal constructs are George Kelly's theory (1955), measured with his Role Construct Repertory Test, not with pictures; operant conditioning and "behaviour shaped only by reinforcement contingencies" are B. F. Skinner's; cognitive distortions are Aaron Beck's; "intelligence is a single general factor" is Charles Spearman's g (Chapter 6); "traits are stable and biologically fixed" is the claim of biological trait theories (Chapter 9). Need-press theory is the basis of the TAT, not of the Rorschach (perception of blots), sentence completion (Section 10.8) or the word association test (Galton and Jung, Section 10.8). Alpha press is real, beta press is perceived; a thema is need plus press.

Bellak's clinical reading, and a worked example

Murray's scheme was built for research on normal young men. Clinicians needed something quicker and closer to the questions they were asked: what are this patient's central conflicts, how does he see the people around him, how does he defend himself, and how well does he cope? The most widely taught answer came from Leopold Bellak (1916 to 2000), a Vienna-born psychoanalyst who left for the United States after the Nazi annexation of Austria in 1938, took master's degrees at Boston University (1939) and Harvard (1942), and worked on the TAT from the 1940s. Bellak devised a scoring and analysis blank on which the clinician records, story by story, a fixed set of ten variables, and then summarises across stories; his book The TAT and CAT in Clinical Use (1954) went through many later editions. His approach is ego-psychological: it looks not only at drives and conflicts, as Murray's needs do, but at the ego's ways of managing them. Table 10.23 lists his ten variables.

Table 10.23 Bellak's ten variables for analysing each TAT story.
VariableThe question the interpreter asks
1. Main themeStated as briefly as possible, what is this story about?
2. Main heroWho is the hero (age, sex, occupation, abilities), and how adequate is he?
3. Main needs and drives of the heroWhat does the hero want, and what does he do to get it?
4. Conception of the environmentIs the world of the story friendly, hostile, dangerous, indifferent?
5. Figures seen asHow are parental figures, peers and younger figures seen, and how does the hero relate to them?
6. Significant conflictsWhich wishes or forces pull against each other?
7. Nature of anxietiesWhat does the hero fear: punishment, loss of love, being alone, injury?
8. Main defencesHow are conflict and fear handled: denial, withdrawal, projection, humour?
9. Adequacy of the superegoWhen there is a "crime", is the "punishment" fair, too severe, too lenient or missing?
10. Integration of the egoHow well does the hero solve the problem, and how coherent and realistic is the story itself?

Two kinds of information go into these ratings. The first is content: what happens in the story. The second is form: how the story is told. A clinician notes how long the stories are, whether they follow the instructions (past, present, feelings, outcome), whether the teller sees the picture accurately or leaves out a figure that is plainly there, whether the plot hangs together, and whether the teller can stay inside the story or keeps breaking off to comment on the card or on himself. Form is where serious disturbance often shows first. A person whose thinking is disorganised may tell a story that wanders from one idea to an unrelated one, misreads obvious details of the picture, or mixes the story with private, odd associations; a severely depressed person may give stories of a sentence or two, slow and flat, with every outcome bleak. The examiner's notes on reaction time, refusals and comments therefore matter as much as the plot.

Now push one story through the whole procedure. The card below is invented for this book (it is not a TAT card, and no real card is described here), and the story is an illustration, not a real record.

Worked example: one invented card, one story

The invented card. A young woman stands on a railway platform holding a closed envelope. Behind her an older woman sits on a bench, looking away along the tracks. A train is at the platform.

The story. Chetna, aged 26, referred for low mood, says after a pause of about 20 seconds: "This girl has got a letter from a college in another city, they have given her admission. She is supposed to get on this train. The mother is sitting there, not looking at her, because she has said many times that a girl should not go so far, who will look after the house. The girl is thinking she has worked very hard for this, but she is also thinking that maybe her mother is right and she is being selfish. The train is about to leave. In the end ... she does not get on. She puts the letter in her bag and they go home together. Later she will tell herself it was the right decision. I think she will be okay. She will be fine."

Read it one step at a time, as Bellak and Murray would.

Step 1, the hero. The hero is plainly the young woman: she is the teller's age and sex, and hers are the only thoughts described.

Step 2, the needs. The hero has worked hard for an admission, so there is a clear need for achievement (n Ach), and wanting to leave for another city adds a need for autonomy. Against these stands a wish to keep her mother's approval and closeness, a need for affiliation, together with what Murray called n Abasement, accepting blame ("maybe she is being selfish").

Step 3, the press. The environment presses in one direction: the mother disapproves and withdraws ("not looking at her"), a press of rejection and of dominance, and the departing train adds a press of time. This is beta press. We know only how Chetna's hero sees the mother, not what any real mother says.

Step 4, the thema and the outcome. The thema is "the hero's striving for achievement and independence meets a parent's disapproval; she gives up the goal to keep the relationship". The outcome is passive and self-sacrificing: she does not get on, and the story ends with a reassurance that sounds more like a hope ("I think she will be okay. She will be fine").

Step 5, Bellak's other variables. The environment is seen as withholding rather than violent; the parental figure controls by silence; the central conflict is autonomy and achievement against affiliation and duty; the anxiety is loss of love; the main defence is giving up the wish and then justifying the choice ("she will tell herself it was the right decision"), close to what analysts call rationalisation; the superego is harsh, since wanting something for herself is felt as selfishness; and the ego is reasonably well integrated: the story is coherent, follows every part of the instruction, and stays realistic.

Step 6, what a careful clinician concludes, and what not. One story is one sample of fantasy, pulled partly by the card: a picture of a young woman leaving and an older woman looking away would draw family and leaving themes from almost anybody. So the clinician writes a hypothesis, not a finding: "possible conflict between independence and family approval, resolved by self-sacrifice; consider whether this pattern relates to her low mood." The hypothesis becomes worth reporting only if the same thema recurs on cards that do not pull for it, and if the interview and history support it. The clinician does not conclude that Chetna "has" depression, that her mother is controlling, or that she will give up her career. Diagnosis belongs to the full assessment (Book 3), and Section 10.10 weighs the evidence on interpretations of this kind.

Clinical link

Used well, the TAT is a structured way of getting a person to talk about relationships without asking directly, which is why many clinicians treat it as an interview aid rather than a test that yields a score. Choose cards for the referral question, record every word, read for themes that recur across cards with different pulls, and attend to form as much as content. Then treat what you find as a set of hypotheses to check against the interview, the history and the other tests. A report that says "the stories suggest" and gives the recurring theme with examples is defensible; a report that diagnoses from one vivid story is not.

Counting motives: McClelland's achievement scoring and later systems

Bellak's reading depends on the skill of the reader. In the late 1940s a group at Wesleyan University and later Harvard, led by David Clarence McClelland (1917 to 1998) with John William Atkinson (1923 to 2003), asked whether picture stories could be scored as objectively as a questionnaire, one motive at a time. Book 1, Section 10.6.2 tells what they found about the achievement motive itself: the preference for moderate risk, the Kakinada training courses, The Achieving Society. This subsection teaches only the method, because it is the best-known quantitative way of scoring picture stories and it rests on an idea worth understanding.

Key study: building a scoring system by arousing the motive (1948 to 1953)

McClelland and Atkinson began with hunger. In studies published in 1948, men at a United States Navy submarine base who had gone without food for different lengths of time, up to 16 hours, wrote stories about pictures. The hungrier men did not write more about eating; they wrote more about being deprived of food and about the things characters did to get it. A motive, aroused, showed up in fantasy in a recognisable form. The group then aroused the achievement motive. Male college students did a series of tasks, such as anagrams, under different conditions: in a relaxed condition the tasks were presented casually, while in an achievement-oriented condition the students were told that the tasks measured intelligence and leadership ability. Afterwards everyone wrote stories about a set of pictures. Whatever kinds of imagery appeared more often after arousal than in the relaxed condition were taken as the signs of achievement motivation, and those categories became the scoring system, published in The Achievement Motive (1953) by McClelland, Atkinson, Russell Clark and Edgar Lowell. The elegance of the design is that the scoring categories were not chosen by anyone's clinical intuition: they were defined by what an experimentally aroused motive actually does to stories. Once defined, the categories were applied to stories written under neutral conditions, on the assumption that people who produce much achievement imagery without being aroused carry a strong motive with them.

The procedure for collecting stories was also standardised. Each picture was shown briefly, often projected on a screen, and the person then had about four minutes to write a story, guided by four printed questions: what is happening and who are the people; what has led up to the situation; what is being thought and wanted, and by whom; and what will happen. Modern researchers call this format the picture story exercise (PSE), because the pictures need not be TAT cards: they are chosen, and often newly drawn or photographed, for how strongly they pull for the motive being studied. Four to eight pictures, about five minutes each, is the usual set.

Scoring proceeds in two stages. First the scorer decides, for each story, what kind of imagery it contains. A story has achievement imagery (AI) when some character shows concern with doing well in relation to a standard of excellence, and the 1953 manual recognised three ways this can appear: competition with a standard of excellence (wanting to win, to do better than others, or to beat one's own best), unique accomplishment (an invention, a discovery, a work of art), and long-term involvement in an achievement goal (training for a career over years). A story about a task in which nobody cares how well it is done is scored as task imagery (TI; the scoring manual's full name for it is doubtful achievement imagery, and it also covers stories that mention achievement but meet none of the three criteria), and a story with no achievement content at all is unrelated imagery (UI). Only if achievement imagery is present does the scorer go on to the second stage, checking the story for ten subcategories, each scored once per story if present. Table 10.24 lists them with an invented example of each.

Table 10.24 McClelland's scoring of achievement imagery (1953), with invented examples.
CodeCategoryScored whenInvented example
AIAchievement imageryConcern with a standard of excellence (scores +1)"He wants to make the best model in the exhibition"
TITask imageryA task, but no concern with excellence (scores 0)"She is filling in a form at the bank"
UIUnrelated imageryNo achievement content (scores −1)"Two friends chat about a film"
NStated needSomeone wants to reach the goal"She longs to top the class"
IInstrumental activitySomething is done to reach it"He practises every evening"
Ga+Positive anticipationImagining success"She pictures her name on the list"
Ga−Negative anticipationWorrying about failure"He fears he will not finish in time"
BpPersonal blockAn obstacle inside the person"Her hands shake on stage"
BwWorld blockAn obstacle outside the person"The power fails the night before"
NupNurturant pressSomeone helps or encourages"His teacher stays late to help"
G+Positive affectPleasure at success"She is overjoyed when she wins"
G−Negative affectDistress at failure or blocking"He is crushed by the result"
Ach ThAchievement themaAchievement is the central plotThe whole story is about the contest
Remember

Story score = −1 for unrelated imagery (UI); 0 for task imagery (TI); 1 + k for achievement imagery (AI)

n Ach score = Σ story scores, summed over all the stories a person writes

Here k is the number of the ten subcategories (N, I, Ga+, Ga−, Bp, Bw, Nup, G+, G−, Ach Th) present in the story, each counted once, so k runs from 0 to 10 and one story can score anywhere from −1 to 11. Σ means "add up".

Work an example. In a motivation study at a college, Gautam and Hansa each write stories to the same four pictures. Two trained scorers code them independently, and these are the agreed codes.

Gautam. Story 1 is about a student building a robot for a national competition: achievement imagery, with a stated need (N), practice and testing (I), the hero imagining the trophy (Ga+), a rival team's better equipment (Bw), joy at winning (G+) and the contest as the whole plot (Ach Th). That is AI plus six subcategories, so 1 + 6 = 7. Story 2 is about a man repairing his scooter because it has broken down, with no concern about doing it well: task imagery, 0. Story 3 is two friends chatting at a tea stall: unrelated imagery, −1. Story 4 has achievement imagery with instrumental activity and a helpful coach (I and Nup): 1 + 2 = 3. Gautam's total is 7 + 0 + (−1) + 3 = 9.

Hansa. Story 1 has achievement imagery with N, I, a personal block (Bp, her stage fright), worry about failing (Ga−) and an achievement thema: 1 + 5 = 6. Story 2 has achievement imagery with instrumental activity only: 1 + 1 = 2. Story 3 has achievement imagery with N, I and distress at a poor result (G−): 1 + 3 = 4. Story 4 is task imagery: 0. Hansa's total is 6 + 2 + 4 + 0 = 12.

On raw totals Hansa (12) scores higher than Gautam (9). But Hansa wrote 800 words and Gautam 450, and a long story simply has more room for scorable imagery. For this reason later researchers correct for length, either statistically or, in David G. Winter's system for scoring motive imagery in running text, by expressing the count per 1,000 words. Apply that density idea here to see its effect. Gautam: 9 ÷ 450 × 1,000 = 0.02 × 1,000 = 20 images per 1,000 words. Hansa: 12 ÷ 800 × 1,000 = 0.015 × 1,000 = 15 images per 1,000 words. Both results are exact, so no rounding is needed. In plain words: Hansa wrote more achievement imagery in total, but Gautam packed more of it into each word he wrote, and once length is allowed for, his stories are the more achievement-laden. Two further cautions follow from the method itself. Scores are comparable only between people who wrote to the same pictures under the same conditions, because pictures differ in how strongly they pull for achievement; and a score is trustworthy only if the scorers are trained, which in this tradition means reaching about 85 percent agreement with expert-scored practice stories before scoring real ones (Chapter 3, Section 3.6 explains how agreement is measured).

The same arousal logic produced scoring systems for the need for affiliation and the need for power (Book 1, Section 10.6.2), and Winter later combined the three into one system that can be applied to any running text, even political speeches. Two other systems carried story scoring into the clinic. Drew Westen developed the Social Cognition and Object Relations Scale (SCORS), reviewed in a 1991 Psychological Bulletin article, which rates stories for how the teller represents people and relationships; its later global form, the SCORS-G, rates eight dimensions on 7-point scales, among them the complexity of representations of people, the emotional quality of those representations, emotional investment in relationships and in moral standards, understanding of why people act as they do, the management of aggressive impulses, self-esteem, and the coherence of the sense of self. It is the scoring system to reach for when the referral question is about close relationships. Phebe Cramer's Defense Mechanism Manual, set out in her 1991 book on the development of defence mechanisms, scores stories for three defences that she argued appear in a developmental order: denial (the earliest and least mature), projection, and identification (the most mature). Whether any of these scores are reliable and valid, including the long argument over whether TAT scores can have internal consistency at all and the evidence that story-based "implicit" motives predict different behaviour from questionnaire motives, is the business of Section 10.10.

Exam trap

McClelland's need for achievement is scored from stories written to pictures (TAT cards or picture story exercises), not from a questionnaire: the Edwards Personal Preference Schedule is the questionnaire built on Murray's needs (Book 1, Section 10.6.1). Murray rated needs and press 1 to 5; McClelland counted achievement imagery (+1, 0 or −1) plus up to ten subcategories. Bellak gave the clinical ten-variable analysis; Westen the SCORS for object relations; Cramer the Defense Mechanism Manual (denial, projection, identification). None of them wrote the TAT.

The TAT beside the Rorschach, and what the TAT is for

Students often learn the TAT and the Rorschach as twins, and examiners exploit the confusion. Both are projective; both present an ambiguous stimulus, leave the response open, disguise their purpose and are read globally (Section 10.1). But they differ at every step, as Table 10.25 shows.

Table 10.25 The TAT and the Rorschach compared.
FeatureThematic Apperception TestRorschach Inkblot Test
Authors and dateMorgan and Murray, 1935; manual 1943Hermann Rorschach, 1921
StimulusPictures of people in ambiguous social scenes; 31 cards, card 16 blankTen symmetrical inkblots, five achromatic and five with colour
Degree of structureMore structured: figures and settings are recognisableLess structured: no objects at all, only shapes and colours
TaskTell a story with a past, present, feelings and an outcomeSay what the blot might be
Lindzey familyConstructionAssociation
What is analysedContent: needs, press, themas, outcomes, relationships, defencesMainly structure: location, determinants, form quality, content codes
Typical useMotives, conflicts and how the person sees relationshipsPerception, thought processes, reality testing, affect

The deepest difference is in the last two rows. The Rorschach is read mainly for how a person perceives and organises: whether the percepts fit the blot, which features of the blot drive the response, whether thinking stays logical (Sections 10.4 and 10.5). That makes it, at its best, a test of the structure of thinking and of reality testing. The TAT gives the person recognisable people in recognisable situations and asks what they want, feel and do to one another. That makes it, at its best, a test of the content of motives and of relationships: what the person wishes for, what he expects from others, and how he thinks things turn out. A useful slogan, true as a tendency though not a rule: the Rorschach shows how a person thinks, the TAT what he thinks about.

That difference tells you when to choose the TAT. Suppose a clinician wants to understand a client's characteristic ways of thinking, feeling and behaving in close relationships, and wants to get at them by having the client tell stories about ambiguous pictures. Only one test matches the whole description. The Rorschach uses inkblots, not pictures of people, and asks for percepts, not stories. A sentence completion test (Section 10.8) asks the person to finish written stems such as "My mother ..." and so gets at relationships directly, but through completion, not storytelling. The MMPI is an objective inventory of true-or-false statements (Chapter 9), with no pictures and no stories. The TAT, with its cards of parents and children, couples, friends and rivals, and its demand for what the characters feel and think, is built for exactly this question, and with a relationship-focused scoring system such as Westen's SCORS it is the standard choice.

The same logic answers the other questions the papers ask. The purpose of analysing TAT stories is to reveal unconscious thoughts, conflicts and motives: the needs the person may not be able or willing to report. It does not measure intelligence (IQ), vocabulary or memory, even though Murray's instruction pretends it is "a test of imagination, one form of intelligence"; a long, fluent story is not scored as a high IQ, and the examiner does not test whether the person can remember the pictures. And the TAT is classified as a projective personality test: not an objective personality test or self-report inventory (questionnaires the person answers about himself, scored by a key, Chapter 9), not an objective achievement test (those measure learned knowledge, Chapter 8), not an intelligence test (Chapter 6), and not a structured neuropsychological battery (Chapter 11).

Indian context

Murray's cards show American scenes, clothes and faces of the 1930s and 1940s, and a storyteller who cannot recognise the world of a picture may describe it rather than enter it. In 1960 Uma Chowdhury, then with the Department of Anthropology of the Government of India, published "An Indian modification of the Thematic Apperception Test" in the Journal of Social Psychology. She adapted Murray's pictures to Indian figures, dress and settings, adding some scenes of her own, and tried them out on Hindu and Muslim groups chosen to give a cross-section of Indian society. The same year she published an Indian adaptation of the Children's Apperception Test (Section 10.7). Do not confuse her with Udai Pareek, who adapted the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study for India (Section 10.8).

Two pointers close the section. The evidence on how reliable and valid TAT interpretations are, from the low agreement of intuitive readings to the strong predictive record of achievement scores, belongs to Section 10.10 and is not argued here. And the TAT's descendants, the picture-story tests built for children, older adults and particular cultures, are the subject of the next section.

Exam trap

The key difference: the TAT uses ambiguous social scenes with characters; the Rorschach uses symmetrical inkblots. The baits: the TAT does not measure IQ (both are personality techniques); neither test asks for yes or no answers (the TAT wants stories, the Rorschach percepts; yes or no answers belong to inventories); and the two are not identical in method or purpose. For "how a client thinks, feels and behaves in close relationships, by telling stories about ambiguous pictures", the answer is the TAT, not the Rorschach, sentence completion or the MMPI. The TAT reveals unconscious thoughts, conflicts and motives, not IQ, vocabulary or memory. It is a projective personality test using ambiguous interpersonal scenes, not an objective, achievement, intelligence or neuropsychological test, and in Lindzey's scheme a construction technique (Section 10.2).

10.7 Apperception Tests for Children and Other Groups: The CAT and Its RelativesLower yield · 1 Q

Show a TAT card to a four-year-old and you will rarely get a story. You are more likely to hear a list ("a man, a lady, a window, a book"), a question ("Who is that?"), or a silence while the child looks at the door. The child is not being defensive. The TAT was made with adolescents and adults in mind: most of its pictures show grown-ups in grown-up predicaments, and the task of inventing a plot with a past and a future asks more of language and imagination than a small child can yet give. Murray had foreseen part of the problem, with versions of some cards for boys and girls and simpler instructions for children (Section 10.6.2), but even those cards assume a child old enough to tell a proper story. Over the next seventy years psychologists rebuilt the picture-story method for people the TAT did not fit, and each new test changed one of four things: the figures on the cards, the situations they show, the culture they come from, or the scoring. Figure 10.15 sets out the family; this section tells its story, beginning with the best known member.

Why young children need different pictures

Three things about young children make the TAT a poor fit. The first is development. Story-telling is a skill that grows through childhood. Libby noticed as early as 1908 that younger children's picture stories were "objective", describing what is visible, while older children's were "subjective", full of thoughts and feelings (Section 10.6.1). A five-year-old who names objects instead of telling a story is showing his developmental level, not a disturbance, and a test that asks every child for a dramatic plot will mistake youth for pathology. The second is content. The worries of a young child are not those of an adult. They are about being fed and cared for, about whether a parent loves a new baby more, about being left alone in the dark, about being punished for being angry or messy. Pictures of couples, careers and deaths do not pull for these themes; pictures of meals, bedtimes and brothers and sisters do. The third is identification. The whole method rests on the teller identifying with the hero (Section 10.6.3), and a child may find it hard to step into the shoes of a businessman or a bride.

Change the pictures, then. But change them to what? The first and still the most famous answer was surprising: change the people into animals.

The Children's Apperception Test

The Children's Apperception Test (CAT) was published in 1949 by Leopold Bellak, the TAT interpreter of Section 10.6.4, and Sonya Sorel Bellak. Bellak credited the central idea to a conversation with Ernst Kris (1900 to 1957), the Viennese art historian and psychoanalyst, who suggested that children would identify much more readily with animals than with people. There were good reasons to think so. Children's stories, fables, toys and cartoons are full of animals, and so are their fears and dreams. An animal is also a safer vehicle: a child who could never say that he hates his baby sister can let a young deer snatch its little sister's food. And animals are ambiguous in a way people are not. An animal figure has no obvious age, sex or culture, so a single set of cards can serve boys and girls of many backgrounds, where the TAT had needed separate versions for each (Figure 10.13).

The CAT consists of 10 cards showing animals in human situations, intended for children of about 3 to 10 years, and given in a fixed order. In keeping with test security (Book 2, Section 1.9.6) this book does not describe the cards one by one; what matters is what they were designed to pull for. Taken together, the set offers situations that draw stories about feeding and being given to, rivalry between brothers and sisters, the child's relationship with each parent and with the parents as a couple, aggression and the fear of it, toilet training and acceptance by the adult world, and fear of being alone, especially at night. Through these the clinician hopes to see the child's drives, fears, defences and picture of the people at home.

The CAT is given very differently from an adult TAT. The examiner first builds rapport, often through play, and presents the task as a game, in words such as: "We are going to play a game. I will show you some pictures, and you tell me a story about each one: what is happening, what happened before, and what will happen next." Young children need more help than adults, so the examiner may prompt more often, with neutral questions such as "What is he doing?", "How does she feel?" and "How does it end?", without suggesting an answer. The child may hold the cards, and the examiner may use fewer than ten if the child tires, so a session is usually much shorter than an adult's hour. Every word the child says is recorded exactly as said, together with what the child does: fidgeting, turning a card face down, asking to go to the toilet, or wanting to take a card home are all information. Interpretation uses Bellak's ten variables (Table 10.23) adapted for children, story by story and then across stories. There is no numerical score and no right or wrong story.

Two companions followed. The CAT-S (1952), the supplement, added ten further animal pictures of more specific situations that some children face, such as prolonged illness, physical disability, a mother's pregnancy and the separation of parents; a clinician uses the relevant cards when a child is going through one of these events. The CAT-H (1965) kept the situations of the original ten cards but drew them with human figures. The reason for the CAT-H is the most instructive part of the CAT's story. The Bellaks' assumption that children identify more readily with animals was tested in the 1950s by researchers who showed children animal and human versions of the same scenes and compared the stories (K. R. Biersdorf and F. L. Marcuse's 1953 comparison is one of several). The expected advantage for animals did not reliably appear. Bellak's own conclusion was that some children tell richer stories to animals and some to people, and that older children of about seven to ten, especially brighter ones, may find the animal pictures babyish. A clinician can now choose the version that suits the child. The general lesson, which Section 10.10 develops, is that a projective test's founding assumptions are hypotheses, and some of them fail when tested.

Indian context

In 1960, the same year as her Indian modification of the TAT (Section 10.6.6), Uma Chowdhury published an Indian adaptation of the Children's Apperception Test (Delhi: Manasayan), redrawing the scenes so that their settings, objects and family situations would be familiar to Indian children. Both Indian adaptations are hers. When an Indian exam asks who adapted the TAT or the CAT for India, the answer is Uma Chowdhury; Udai Pareek adapted the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Section 10.8).

Exam trap

The CAT differs from the TAT mainly in that it uses animal figures (and, in the CAT-H, human figures) in cards designed for children of about 3 to 10. It is not an objective self-report inventory (a young child cannot fill in a questionnaire about himself, and the CAT has no items to endorse); it does not measure only intelligence (it is a projective personality technique with no IQ score); and it uses pictures, not inkblots (inkblots are the Rorschach and the Holtzman). The CAT is by Leopold and Sonya Bellak, 1949, not by Murray. CAT-H means human figures, not "humanistic"; CAT-S means supplement, not "senior".

Worked example: reading a seven-year-old's story

Children's stories have to be read with two questions in mind at once: what does this story say about this child's concerns, and what does it say about seven-year-olds in general? The card below is invented for this book; it is not a CAT card, and no real card is described.

Worked example: an invented card and a child's story

The invented card. Two young elephants stand at the edge of a river. One holds a bunch of bananas in its trunk; the other watches it. On the bank above, a large elephant stands with its trunk raised.

The referral. Ojasvi, a boy of seven, is brought to a child guidance clinic because of tantrums and bedwetting that began a few months after the birth of his sister.

The story (the examiner's neutral prompts are in brackets). "This is the baby elephant and this is the big brother. The baby has all the bananas. The big brother also wants but the baby won't give. [What happened before?] The mummy elephant gave all the bananas to the baby because baby is small. [What is the mummy doing now?] She is shouting at the big brother because he pushed the baby in the water. Then the baby cried. [How does it end?] Then a crocodile comes and ... no, then the big brother saves the baby and mummy gives him the biggest banana and says good boy. The end."

Step 1, the hero. The hero is the big brother: he is the figure in Ojasvi's own position, and the story follows his wants and actions. Notice that Ojasvi assigned the roles himself; nothing on the card says which elephant is older.

Step 2, needs and figures. The hero wants what the baby has. In the language of the CAT, being given food stands for being given love, so the need is to be given to and cared for, and close behind it is aggression: he pushes the baby into the water. The mother is seen as giving everything to the baby and as punishing the hero; the baby is a rival who "won't give".

Step 3, conflict, anxiety and defence. The conflict is between the wish for mother's love, with anger at the rival who has taken it, and fear of the mother's anger. The anxiety is loss of love and punishment. Then comes the most interesting moment in the story: a crocodile begins to appear, a danger that would solve the rival problem far too completely, and Ojasvi stops himself ("no, then ...") and turns the danger into a rescue in which he is the hero. Analysts call this kind of reversal undoing: the aggressive idea is cancelled by its opposite.

Step 4, superego and outcome. The punishment for pushing (being shouted at) is in proportion, not catastrophic, and the ending is happy and earned by the hero's own action: he regains the mother's love ("good boy") and the biggest banana. Bellak would note an ego that can find a solution, even a magical one.

Step 5, what to conclude, and what not. The story supports a hypothesis that fits the referral and its timing: Ojasvi may be struggling with jealousy of his sister and a fear that he has lost his place with his mother, and his symptoms may be a bid to win it back. The clinician checks the hypothesis against the other cards (do rivalry and lost-love themes appear on cards that do not pull for them?), against the parents' account and against observation in play (Book 2, Section 10.2.2). The clinician does not conclude that Ojasvi is a danger to his sister, that he has a disorder, or that the crocodile is a sign of anything bizarre. Jealousy after a sibling's birth is one of the most ordinary experiences of childhood; magical endings, sudden crocodiles and good boys rewarded with the biggest banana are exactly what seven-year-olds' stories look like. A child's story reflects his stage of development, his language, and the cartoons he watched last week, as well as his conflicts, and only the last is what the clinician is looking for.

Clinical link

In a child guidance setting the CAT is one window among several. The parents' interview gives the history, the school report shows how the child behaves elsewhere, play shows what the child does when he is not asked to talk, and the stories show what he imagines. When the stories agree with the other windows, they help the clinician explain the child to his parents in the child's own images ("he told a story about a big brother elephant who felt left out"), which parents often understand better than any technical term.

Pictures for later life: the SAT and the GAT

At the other end of life the TAT fits no better. Its heroes are mostly young, and its situations, love affairs, careers and conflicts with parents, are not the central concerns of most people in their seventies, whose worries are more likely to be illness, loneliness, dependence on their children, loss of a partner and the approach of death. Two apperception tests were built for older adults. The Gerontological Apperception Test (GAT), by R. L. Wolk and R. B. Wolk (1971), uses 14 black-and-white cards of situations an older adult could identify with. The Bellaks' Senior Apperception Technique (SAT, 1973) uses 16 cards that show older people in the situations of later life, including some pleasant ones, and was designed to draw out the concerns of old age, such as depression, loneliness, illness and anger.

Both have been criticised on the same grounds. Many of the pictures show older people in sad, lonely or dependent situations, so that almost anybody would tell a sad story about them; reviewers have objected to the negative tone and the stereotyped picture of old age, and neither test has an accepted scoring system with norms (Bert Hayslip and colleagues later proposed a revised scoring for the GAT). The problem is the general one of card pull, met already with Chetna's railway platform. If Ila, aged 74, tells stories in which every old person is ignored by grown-up children, the clinician must ask whether that is Ila's theme or simply what pictures of lonely old people pull from everyone. The answer comes, as always, from comparing cards with different pulls and from knowing Ila's life.

Exam trap

Two tests for older adults, two pairs of authors: the Senior Apperception Technique is the Bellaks' (1973); the Gerontological Apperception Test is the Wolks' (1971). The S in SAT stands for senior; the S in CAT-S stands for supplement.

Norms and culture: the Roberts, the Thompson TAT and the TEMAS

The CAT shares the TAT's great weakness: there is no standard scoring and almost no normative data, so two clinicians can read the same stories differently and nobody can say how common a theme is among ordinary children. The Roberts Apperception Test for Children (RATC), published in 1982 by Dorothea S. McArthur and Glen E. Roberts through Western Psychological Services, was built to fix that. Its pictures show human children in the ordinary situations of childhood: arguments with parents, rivalry with brothers and sisters, trouble with friends, school, and moments of parental affection. Its main selling point is the scoring. Each story is scored on a set of defined scales, adaptive scales (for example, how well the child identifies a problem and finds a realistic resolution, and whether characters give and receive support) and clinical scales (for example anxiety, aggression, depression and rejection), and the totals are compared with norms from ordinary children, so that a clinician can say that a child's stories contain more unresolved problems than those of most children his age. The revised Roberts-2 (2005, by Roberts and Chris Gruber) extended the age range to 6 to 18 and gives each child 16 pictures, from three parallel sets showing White, Black and Hispanic children, with updated clothes and hairstyles but the same situations.

Those parallel sets answer a question the TAT raised almost at once: does a storyteller identify more easily with figures who look like him? In 1949 Charles E. Thompson published the Thompson modification of the TAT (Harvard University Press), which redrew Murray's cards with Black figures for use with African American clients, on the reasoning that a Black teller shown only white faces might describe the pictures rather than enter them. Whether the race of the figures by itself changes the stories became a long research question, and it led, forty years later, to the most thorough cultural redesign of the method. The TEMAS (from "Tell-Me-A-Story", and the Spanish word for themes), by Giuseppe Costantino, Robert G. Malgady and Lloyd H. Rogler (manual 1988), was built for Hispanic and Black children in American cities, with a parallel non-minority version showing White children. It differs from the TAT in three ways. Its 23 pictures are in colour. They show Hispanic and Black children and families (or, in the parallel version, White ones) in familiar urban settings. And they are deliberately less ambiguous: each picture sets up a recognisable conflict, for example between what a child wants and what a parent asks, so that the story has to show how the child resolves it. The stories are scored for personality functions such as interpersonal relations, aggression, anxiety and depression, achievement motivation and moral judgement, along with cognitive and affective functions, and the original standardisation used 642 New York schoolchildren aged 5 to 13 from Puerto Rican and other Hispanic, Black and White backgrounds.

The Indian adaptations of Section 10.6.6 and Section 10.7.2 belong to the same movement. Cultural adaptation is more than repainting faces. A picture-story test works only if the situations on the cards are situations the culture recognises and the outcomes it invites are ones the culture can imagine: a scene of a young person leaving home means one thing in a society where that is expected at eighteen and another in a joint family where it may be a rupture. A clinician using any picture-story test with a client from a different background from the norm group has to ask whether a "deviant" story reflects the person or the mismatch.

Exam trap

The Roberts Apperception Test is by Glen E. Roberts and Dorothea McArthur (1982), not by Julian Rotter (sentence completion, Section 10.8) or Saul Rosenzweig (picture-frustration, Section 10.8). It shows human children, not animals, and its distinctive feature is normed scales. The TEMAS (Costantino, Malgady and Rogler) is the colour, multicultural picture-story test for Hispanic and Black children; the Thompson TAT (1949) is Murray's set redrawn with Black figures.

Psychoanalytic and build-your-own variants: the Blacky Pictures, the MAPS and others

Two more relatives changed not the audience but the kind of stimulus. The Blacky Pictures were built by Gerald S. Blum, who described the method in a 1949 monograph on the psychoanalytic theory of psychosexual development; the published test followed in 1950. Where the CAT uses many animals, Blacky uses one cartoon family of dogs: Blacky, Mama, Papa and a sibling, Tippy. Blacky's sex is set to match the teller's. There are 12 cartoons, and each was designed around one stage or conflict of Sigmund Freud's theory of psychosexual development: oral pleasures and oral aggression, the anal stage, the Oedipal rivalry, castration anxiety or penis envy, sibling rivalry, guilt, identification with a parent, and so on. The person tells a spontaneous story about each cartoon, then answers a set of structured questions about it (several with multiple-choice answers), and finally says which cartoons he likes and dislikes; each psychosexual dimension is then rated. Blacky was perhaps the most explicitly Freudian test ever built, and that is both its interest and its weakness: it could only ever measure Freud's categories, critics such as Hans Eysenck attacked its validity, and it has largely fallen out of use. Do not confuse it with the CAT: both use animals, but Blacky is one dog family built to test Freud's stages, and the CAT is many animals in everyday situations, read through Bellak's ego psychology.

The Make-A-Picture-Story test (MAPS), introduced in 1947 by Edwin S. Shneidman (1918 to 2009), later famous as a founder of the scientific study of suicide, lets the person build the stimulus. The materials are 22 background pictures, scenes such as a living room, a street and a blank background, and 67 cut-out figures that stand upright on small bases: men, women and children, figures of several ethnic groups, animals, legendary and fictional characters, silhouettes and figures with blank faces. The person acts as a director. He chooses a background, chooses and places the figures he wants, and then tells a story about the scene he has made. The MAPS therefore adds two kinds of data to the TAT's: which figures a person chooses (or avoids), and where he puts them, close together or far apart, facing or turned away. Evelyn Hooker used the MAPS, with the Rorschach and the TAT, in her 1957 study of gay and heterosexual men (Book 3, Section 9.10.3). The MAPS is little used today.

Two others deserve a line each. The Michigan Picture Test (G. Andrew, S. W. Hartwell, M. L. Hutt and R. E. Walton, 1953) used pictures of the situations of school-age children to screen for emotional maladjustment, and was revised by Max Hutt in 1980. The Object Relations Technique of Herbert Phillipson (1955, published by Tavistock in London) mixes features of the TAT and the Rorschach, using pictures of human figures drawn with varying degrees of shading and clarity and interpreting the stories through psychoanalytic object relations theory. In Lindzey's terms every test in this section, like the TAT, is a construction technique (Section 10.2): the person makes a story. Table 10.26 gathers them.

Table 10.26 The apperception (picture-story) tests.

TATMorgan and Murray, 1935; manual 1943People in ambiguous scenesAdolescents and adults31 cards, card 16 blank; 20 per person

Look back over the family and a pattern appears. The tests that changed only the figures or the culture, such as the CAT, the Thompson TAT and the Blacky Pictures, inherited the TAT's problem of unstandardised, intuitive scoring, so their results still depend heavily on the skill of the reader. The tests that added defined scales and norms, the Roberts and the TEMAS, are the ones whose scores can most easily be checked against evidence. Section 10.10 shows that the same lesson holds across the whole projective field. First, though, Section 10.8 turns from pictures to words: the word association test, sentence completion and the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study.

Exam trap

Blacky is Blum's (a dog family, Freud's psychosexual stages), not Bellak's; the MAPS is Shneidman's (cut-out figures on backgrounds), not Murray's. All the picture-story tests are construction techniques in Lindzey's scheme, like their parent the TAT, and none of them is an objective inventory or an intelligence test.
