10.8 Completion and Verbal Techniques: Word Association, Sentence Completion and the Picture-Frustration StudyLower yield · 1 Q

An inkblot asks you to find a whole world in a smear of ink. The techniques in this section ask for much less. They hand you a fragment (a single spoken word, the first two words of a sentence, a cartoon in which one person has spoken and the other has not) and wait to see what you add. Because the fragment already carries some meaning, these techniques sit towards the structured end of the continuum you met in Section 10.1: more structured than an inkblot or a TAT card, less structured than a true-false inventory. That position is the key to everything about them. They are quicker to give and far easier to score than the Rorschach, several can be given to a whole group at once, and two of them (the Rotter blank and the Picture-Frustration Study) come with scoring rules tight enough to give a number. The price is that their purpose is easier to see through: a person who wants to look well adjusted can usually tell what a cheerful answer to "My father..." would be.

In Gardner Lindzey's scheme (Section 10.2) word association is an association technique, because the person answers a stimulus with the first thing it calls up, while sentence completion, story completion and the Picture-Frustration Study are completion techniques, because the person finishes something left unfinished. In the simpler three-way grouping, word association and sentence completion are the classic verbal techniques. This section takes them in historical order, which is also the order of increasing structure: first the word association test, the oldest projective method of all; then sentence completion; then Saul Rosenzweig's cartoons of everyday frustration; and finally the unfinished stories used with children.

Word association: from Galton's experiment on himself to Jung's complexes

The word association test has a simple form. The examiner reads out a list of words one at a time, and after each the person says, as quickly as possible, the first word that comes to mind. The examiner writes down the reply and, in most versions, the time it took. What could such a bare procedure reveal? The first person to ask that question seriously asked it of himself.

Key study: Galton's 75 words (1879)

Francis Galton (1822 to 1911), whose life and anthropometric laboratory are described in Book 1, Section 1.3.4, wanted to see what his own mind did in the first instant after meeting a word. He chose a list of 75 words, kept each one hidden until the moment of the trial, and sat at a table with a stopwatch. As he exposed a word he started the watch, waited until the word had called up two associated ideas, stopped the watch and wrote the ideas down. He went through the whole list on four occasions about a month apart, in England and abroad, so that his circumstances differed each time. He called it "a most repugnant and laborious work".

The 300 trials produced 505 ideas, but only 289 of them were different: the same ideas kept coming back. Of every hundred words, 23 gave exactly the same association on all four occasions. When Galton dated the associations he could place in his life, 39 per cent went back to boyhood and youth, 46 per cent to his later manhood and only 15 per cent to recent events, and the early associations were the most fixed: a quarter of them appeared on all four trials, while not one of the recent ones did. "The roadways of our minds," he concluded, "are worn into very deep ruts." He published the method in the journal Brain in 1879 as "Psychometric experiments", but not his actual replies, because they laid bare "the foundations of a man's thoughts with curious distinctness" and exposed his mental anatomy more vividly than he would care to show the world.

Galton's experiment contains, in miniature, the two assumptions on which every later projective technique rests. The first is that a quick, unreflecting response can show content a person would never volunteer, which is exactly why Galton refused to print his. The second is that the response is shaped by the person's history, and especially by early experience: his oldest associations were the deepest ruts. That is why his 1879 study is usually called the first projective technique, even though Galton himself was studying the machinery of thought, not diagnosing anyone.

For the next twenty years word association belonged to the laboratory. In Leipzig, Wilhelm Wundt (1832 to 1920) and his students made the association experiment a standard procedure in the 1880s, timing the interval between stimulus and reply with precise instruments and sorting replies into kinds (a word that means the same, a word that names a part of the whole, a word that merely sounds alike). Emil Kraepelin (1856 to 1926), who had worked in Wundt's laboratory before becoming the psychiatrist whose classification you met in Book 3, carried the experiment into psychiatry and used it to study how fatigue, alcohol and mental illness change the flow of associations. In this tradition an odd reply or a slow one was noise, a lapse of attention to be averaged away.

The decisive turn came in Zurich. At the Burghölzli, the university psychiatric hospital directed by Eugen Bleuler, the young Carl Gustav Jung (1875 to 1961) and his colleague Franz Riklin began in the early 1900s to give association experiments to healthy people and to patients, publishing their first joint study in 1904 and the results in a series later collected as Diagnostic Association Studies (1906 and 1909). Jung noticed that the "errors" the laboratory had thrown away were not random. A long pause, a stumble or a strange reply tended to cluster around particular words, and when he asked about those words he found that they touched a complex: a cluster of emotionally charged ideas and memories, usually about something painful or shameful, that interferes with the smooth working of the mind when a stimulus brushes against it. A sign in the response that a complex has been touched is a complex indicator.

Jung's procedure became the model for clinical word association. He used a list of 100 stimulus words, most of them ordinary (body parts, colours, actions, household objects) with a few chosen to touch common sources of emotion such as death, money, love and quarrels. The examiner reads each word aloud; the person replies with the first word that comes to mind; the examiner records the reply and the reaction time, which Jung measured with a stopwatch reading to one-fifth of a second. When the whole list is done, the examiner goes through it a second time and asks the person to repeat the reply given the first time. This second run is the reproduction test, and a reply that cannot be remembered, or is remembered wrongly, is itself an indicator, because replies to complex-laden words are forgotten more often than others. Table 10.27 lists the indicators Jung and Riklin described.

Table 10.27 Jung's complex indicators in the word association test, each with an invented example (stimulus word, then reply).
IndicatorWhat happensInvented example
Prolonged reaction timeThe reply comes clearly later than the person's own usual time"hospital" ... (7 seconds) ... "white"
Failure to reactNo reply at all, or "nothing comes""divorce" ... silence
Repeating the stimulus wordThe person echoes the word before answering, buying time"brother" ... "brother? ... tall"
Mishearing the wordThe stimulus is misheard or misunderstood"pray" heard as "pay" ... "salary"
Several words or a sentenceA phrase instead of a single word"kiss" ... "not in front of everyone"
Clang or superficial replyA rhyme or sound-alike instead of a meaningful link"knife" ... "wife"
PerseverationThe same reply, or the disturbance, carries over to the next words"debt" ... "worry"; "lamp" ... "worry"
Very personal or emotional replyA reply that names the person's own life or feelings"exam" ... "I failed"
Faulty reproductionOn the second run the first reply is forgotten or changed"angry" ... "shout" first time, "quiet" second time
Expressive and bodily signsLaughing, blushing, stammering, a slip of the tongue, a change in skin conductance"secret" ... a nervous laugh, then "box"

How long is "prolonged"? Jung did not compare a reply with a population norm. He compared it with the same person's own typical time, which he called the probable mean: the middle value when all the person's reaction times are put in order, the value that statistics calls the median (the Research Methodology book teaches it in full). He preferred it to the ordinary arithmetic mean for a good reason. Most replies come quickly, but a handful of complex-laden ones take five, eight or twelve seconds, and those few long times drag the arithmetic mean upwards, so the very replies you are looking for would hide the standard you are measuring them against. The median is not moved by a few extreme values. The worked example below shows the difference.

Jung also looked inside the body. Working with the American neurologist Frederick Peterson, he recorded changes in the electrical resistance of the skin (the psychogalvanic reflex, measured with a galvanometer) and in breathing (measured with a pneumograph) while people gave their associations. Their long paper in Brain (1907) showed that the bodily reactions tended to rise on exactly the words that produced complex indicators, so the emotional disturbance could be seen in two independent channels at once. This pairing of word association with skin conductance is one ancestor of the polygraph, and for a few years Jung and others hoped the method could detect a suspect's guilty knowledge in court, but the association method never became an accepted tool of police work. Jung presented the method to American psychologists in his lectures at Clark University in 1909, published the next year. How he then used the complexes it revealed in analysis, and how the test differs from Freud's free association, is told in Book 2, Section 3.8.2; here the interest is the test itself.

Remember

Probable mean (median) reaction time = the middle reaction time after all the person's times are put in order. With an even number of times, it is the average of the two middle values.

A reply is "prolonged" when its time is clearly above the person's own probable mean. The standard is the person, not a group.

A worked example. Nidhi, a 26-year-old bank officer, has come to a hospital psychology department in Lucknow with poor sleep and tension headaches that her physician can find no cause for. As one small part of a longer assessment the psychologist gives her a short word association list of twelve invented words and times each reply with a stopwatch reading to one-fifth of a second. Her replies and times, in the order the words were read, are: tree, "leaf", 1.4 seconds; mother, "love", 1.8; book, "read", 1.2; money, "save", 1.6; road, "car", 1.4; angry, "shout", 2.0; marriage, a long pause, then "pressure", 5.6; river, "water", 2.8; exam, "pass", 1.8; father, "father? ... far", 4.8; rain, "umbrella", 1.2; window, "glass", 1.6. On the reproduction run she remembers every first reply except one: for angry she now says "quiet".

Step 1: put the twelve times in order. 1.2, 1.2, 1.4, 1.4, 1.6, 1.6, 1.8, 1.8, 2.0, 2.8, 4.8, 5.6.

Step 2: find the probable mean. With twelve values the middle falls between the 6th and the 7th, which are 1.6 and 1.8. Their average is (1.6 + 1.8) ÷ 2 = 3.4 ÷ 2 = 1.7 seconds. That is Nidhi's own typical speed.

Step 3: compare the arithmetic mean. The twelve times add up to 1.2 + 1.2 + 1.4 + 1.4 + 1.6 + 1.6 + 1.8 + 1.8 + 2.0 + 2.8 + 4.8 + 5.6 = 27.2 seconds, and 27.2 ÷ 12 = 2.2667, about 2.27 seconds. The two long replies alone have pushed the average more than half a second above her typical speed. Judged against 2.27 seconds, the 2.0-second reply to angry would look perfectly normal; judged against her own 1.7 it is a little slow.

Step 4: mark the indicators. Marriage took 5.6 seconds, 5.6 ÷ 1.7 = 3.3 times her typical time, after a visible pause. Father took 4.8 seconds (about 2.8 times her typical time) and she repeated the stimulus word before answering. River, the word straight after marriage, took 2.8 seconds although it is a neutral word: the disturbance seems to have carried over, which is perseveration. Angry was slightly slow and its reply changed on the reproduction run. Figure 10.16 shows the record.

Step 5: interpret, carefully. The record suggests that the words marriage, father and, more faintly, angry touch something emotionally loaded for Nidhi. It does not say what. A careless reader might write "unresolved conflict with father over marriage", but that sentence is the reader's story, not the data. The proper use of the result is as a lead for the interview: the psychologist later asks, gently, how things are at home, and Nidhi describes her father's insistence that she agree to a match before her younger sister's wedding, an argument she has been swallowing rather than having. Without that conversation the indicators would have been only a map of where to look. Jung himself used them in exactly that way. Notice too how much the arithmetic mattered: had the psychologist used the arithmetic mean, the slow reply to angry would have passed as normal, and only its changed reproduction would have flagged the word.

Jung read each person against himself or herself. Five years after Jung and Riklin began, two American workers took the opposite road. Grace Helen Kent and Aaron J. Rosanoff, at the Kings Park State Hospital in New York, published "A study of association in insanity" in the American Journal of Insanity in 1910. They chose 100 familiar words (the list begins table, dark, music, sickness, man), picked to be emotionally neutral rather than to touch complexes, and gave them to 1,000 people without mental illness. From these records they built frequency tables showing how often each reply was given to each word. A reply that appears in the tables is a common reaction; a reply not found there is an individual reaction. Comparing patients with the norms, they found that people with psychoses gave far more individual reactions, but also that the two groups shaded into each other, and they concluded that no sharp line divides mental health from mental disease. The Kent-Rosanoff list needed no stopwatch: its score was the commonality of the replies, a measure of how ordinary a person's associations are. It was the first word association test with norms, and its frequency tables were used in research on language and memory for decades.

The third classic use came in the 1940s, when David Rapaport (1911 to 1960), working with Merton Gill and Roy Schafer at the Menninger Clinic, made word association one test in a diagnostic battery alongside the Rorschach and the TAT in Diagnostic Psychological Testing (two volumes, 1945 and 1946). Their list mixed neutral words with "traumatic" ones touching sex, aggression and family, and they read reaction times, popular versus distant replies and the contrast between the two kinds of word, treating very distant or bizarre replies as a sign of disordered thinking. Today the formal word association test is rarely part of a clinical battery, but its logic survives in research on how words are linked in memory and in the clinician's habit of noticing where a person hesitates.

Exam trap

The first projective technique is Francis Galton's word association (1879, 75 words, tried on himself). Carl Jung made it clinical (with Franz Riklin, from 1904; 100 stimulus words; reaction time; complex indicators; his Clark lectures of 1909 were published in 1910, which is why some books date "Jung's test" to 1910). Kent and Rosanoff (1910) gave it norms from 1,000 normal people, so a reply is scored as common or individual. Jung detects complexes; Kent-Rosanoff measures commonality: examiners ask which is which. The word association test is not Freud's free association, in which the patient talks on without stimulus words, and it is not built on Murray's need-press theory (that is the TAT, Section 10.6). In Lindzey's scheme it is an association technique. RML 2021 keyed word association as "not a projective test"; Section 10.2 explains why that key is wrong and how to answer the item.

Sentence completion: from Ebbinghaus to the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank

Word association catches the mind at its fastest and gives a single word to interpret. Sentence completion slows the person down a little and gets a whole clause in return. In a sentence completion test the person is given a series of sentence stems, the opening words of sentences such as "I regret..." or "When I was a child...", and asked to finish each one, usually in writing and usually with the instruction to give real feelings and to work quickly. The method has a surprising ancestor. In 1897 the school authorities of Breslau asked Hermann Ebbinghaus (1850 to 1909), the memory researcher whose forgetting curve you met in Book 1, whether long school mornings exhausted the pupils. Among the tasks he tried, the one that best separated the abler pupils from the weaker was a completion method: passages of prose with syllables and words left out, which the children filled in. Ebbinghaus's completion test was a measure of intellect, the ancestor of the modern cloze test of reading, not of personality.

The turn to personality came in America between the wars. Arthur F. Payne used a set of sentence completions in vocational guidance in 1928, and A. D. Tendler in 1930 built a short completion test of what he called emotional insight, with about 20 stems written to provoke feelings rather than reasoning. During the Second World War, when the armed forces needed a quick way to screen large numbers of soldiers, sentence blanks spread through army hospitals; the best-known test of the method grew directly out of one of those army forms.

Before meeting the tests, notice what a stem can and cannot do. A stem pulls the reply towards a topic: "My mother..." will usually produce something about the mother, so the clinician can sample exactly the areas of life that matter. Stems differ in how directly they speak to the person. A first-person stem ("I feel...") asks openly about oneself; a third-person stem ("When she was blamed, she...") lets the person speak through someone else, and may draw out what the person would not say in the first person. This dimension is called projective distance: how far the stimulus stands from the respondent's own life (Saul Rosenzweig, whose test you meet below, later studied it with his own instrument). And stems differ in ambiguity: "Sometimes..." leaves almost everything open, "At school my teachers..." very little. Because the person writes the reply, can see what he has written and can guess what a healthy answer sounds like, sentence completion is the least disguised of the projective techniques, and some authors call it semi-projective. Its replies are read by content analysis: what is said, about whom, with what feeling, and what themes recur across the stems.

The test that turned this into a number was the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (RISB), published in 1950 by Julian B. Rotter (1916 to 2014), better known for social learning theory and locus of control, and Janet E. Rafferty. It has 40 short stems, most only one or two words long, and three forms: for high-school students, college students and adults. It takes about 20 minutes and can be given to one person or a group. Rotter and Rafferty wanted a screening instrument for counselling centres and research that kept some of the advantages of a projective method but could be scored objectively. Each completion is compared with example completions in a scoring manual (separate examples for men and women, built from real records) and placed in one of seven categories, which carry weights from 0 to 6, as Table 10.28 shows.

Table 10.28 The seven Rotter categories and their weights, each with an invented completion of the invented stem "My family...".
CategoryWeightWhat the completion showsInvented completion
C3, strong conflict6Severe unhappiness, hostility, despair"...would be happier without me."
C2, conflict5Clear unhappiness or conflict"...fights every night."
C1, mild conflict4Minor worries, mild negativity"...worries too much about my marks."
N, neutral3No emotional tone; plain description or a stock phrase"...lives in Kanpur."
P1, mild positive2Mildly positive, hopeful"...is usually supportive."
P2, positive1Clearly positive, warm"...is close and we laugh a lot."
P3, strong positive0Very warm, secure, often with humour"...is the best team I know, noise included."

The weights are summed over the 40 stems. Because a strongly positive answer scores 0 and a strongly conflicted answer scores 6, the total can run from 0 (40 × 0) to 240 (40 × 6), and a higher total means more maladjustment: the RISB is scored in the direction of trouble. Stems left blank are not scored. When only a few are blank, the total is prorated, that is, scaled up to what it would be on 40 stems, so that leaving answers out does not make a person look better adjusted; a record with many blanks cannot be trusted at all.

Remember

RISB total = Σ (weight of each completion), where each weight runs from 0 (P3) to 6 (C3) and Σ means "add up over all the stems answered".

Prorated total = raw total × 40 ÷ number of stems answered.

Higher scores mean more conflict. In Rotter and Rafferty's 1950 college data a total of 135 separated adjusted from maladjusted students well; later norms differ, so a cut-off is a screening guide, never a diagnosis.

A worked example. Rachit, a 20-year-old commerce student in Pune, is sent to the college counsellor by a teacher who has noticed that he has stopped attending classes. The counsellor gives the college form. Here are six of his completions, rewritten with invented stems so that no real item is shown, each rated the way the manual teaches: by comparing the completion with the manual's examples and asking how much conflict or how much positive feeling it carries.

  1. "At home I... feel like a guest nobody invited." Rejection and loneliness stated with some force: C3, 6.
  2. "My teachers... are fine, I suppose." No real feeling either way; a stock, guarded answer: N, 3.
  3. "When I am alone... I watch cricket clips and forget everything." Mostly neutral, but "forget everything" hints at escape from something: C1, 4.
  4. "The future... looks bright if I clear my CA exams." Hopeful, though conditional: P1, 2.
  5. "I secretly... want to study music, not commerce." A plain statement of a wish that clashes with his present course: C2, 5.
  6. "Most people... are kind if you give them a chance." A warm, generous attitude: P2, 1.

These six completions add up to 6 + 3 + 4 + 2 + 5 + 1 = 21, an average of 21 ÷ 6 = 3.5 per stem, a little above the neutral weight of 3. On the whole blank Rachit answered 38 stems and left 2 blank. His 38 ratings were: C3 four times, C2 six times, C1 nine times, N ten times, P1 five times, P2 three times and P3 once (check: 4 + 6 + 9 + 10 + 5 + 3 + 1 = 38).

Step 1: multiply each count by its weight. C3: 4 × 6 = 24. C2: 6 × 5 = 30. C1: 9 × 4 = 36. N: 10 × 3 = 30. P1: 5 × 2 = 10. P2: 3 × 1 = 3. P3: 1 × 0 = 0.

Step 2: add them. Raw total = 24 + 30 + 36 + 30 + 10 + 3 + 0 = 133.

Step 3: prorate for the two blanks. Prorated total = 133 × 40 ÷ 38 = 5,320 ÷ 38 = 140.0.

Step 4: interpret. The raw total of 133 is below the 1950 cut-off of 135; the correctly prorated total of 140 is above it. Forgetting to prorate would have flipped the screening decision. Even so, 140 does not mean "Rachit is maladjusted". The 135 cut-off came from American college students of 1950; means of later college samples have varied widely (from about 120 to almost 150), the 1992 second edition by Rotter, Michael Lah and Rafferty brought new norms for which some sources quote a cut-off of 145, and Rotter himself warned that the cut-off is not an absolute index of psychopathology. A total of 140 is a reason to talk to Rachit, and his completions tell the counsellor what to talk about: a home where he feels unwanted and a course he did not choose. That is what a screening test is for.

The Rotter blank gives one number. The Sacks Sentence Completion Test (SSCT) of Joseph M. Sacks and Sidney Levy, published in 1950 in Lawrence Abt and Leopold Bellak's book Projective Psychology, gives a map instead. Its 60 stems, four for each of 15 attitude areas, cover four broad domains of adjustment: the family (attitudes to mother, to father and to the family unit), sex (attitudes to women and to heterosexual relationships), interpersonal relationships (friends and acquaintances, superiors, people supervised, colleagues at work or school) and the self-concept (fears, guilt feelings, own abilities, the past, the future and goals). The clinician reads the four completions in each area together and rates the area 2 (severely disturbed, seems to need therapeutic help), 1 (mildly disturbed), 0 (no significant disturbance) or X (not enough evidence), then writes a summary of the main conflicts and how the areas connect. There is no total. The SSCT is a structured interview on paper, useful at intake for deciding where to begin, and its value depends on the clinician who reads it.

A third test measures something quite different. Jane Loevinger (1918 to 2008) used sentence completion not to find conflict but to place a person on a ladder of ego development: the growing complexity with which a person understands himself, other people and moral choices, from impulsive and self-protective ways of thinking, through conformist ones, to conscientious, individualistic and autonomous ones. Her Washington University Sentence Completion Test (WUSCT), with its manual by Loevinger and Ruth Wessler (Measuring Ego Development, 1970), has 36 stems. Each completion is matched with graded examples in the manual and assigned the stage it expresses, and the item ratings are combined into a total stage for the person. Because its scoring is so fully specified and its construct has been studied in hundreds of research projects, the WUSCT is usually described as the best-validated sentence completion measure, though it is used far more in research than in clinics. Loevinger's stage theory belongs to the Personality and Developmental books; here, notice that the same method (finish the sentence) can serve a conflict model, an area map or a developmental scale, depending on what the scorer looks for.

Finally, Bertram R. Forer's Structured Sentence Completion Test, described in the Journal of Projective Techniques in 1950, has 100 stems, deliberately structured so that groups of them sample particular areas: attitudes to important people, wishes and needs, and the feelings and reactions a person shows in particular situations. Forer is better remembered for a classroom demonstration of how readily people accept vague personality descriptions as their own, the Forer or Barnum effect, which Section 10.10 and Book 2, Section 2.9.9 discuss. Table 10.29 brings the sentence completion tests together.

Table 10.29 The main sentence completion tests.
TestAuthors, yearStemsScored forKey fact
Completion methodEbbinghaus, 1897Gapped proseIntellectual abilityAncestor of the method; not personality
Rotter Incomplete Sentences BlankRotter and Rafferty, 1950; 2nd ed. with Lah, 199240One overall adjustment score, 0 to 6 per stemHigher = more conflict; cut-off 135 in 1950 data
Sacks Sentence Completion TestSacks and Levy, 19506015 areas rated 0, 1, 2 or XFour domains: family, sex, interpersonal, self-concept
Washington University SCTLoevinger and Wessler, 197036Stage of ego developmentBest-validated; mainly a research tool
Forer Structured SCTForer, 1950100Attitudes, needs, feelings and reactionsLongest; stems grouped by area
Clinical link

In Indian clinics sentence completion is often the projective technique most used at intake, because it is quick, needs only a printed sheet and can be translated. Translation is not trivial. A stem that is open in English may become pointed in Hindi or Kannada, and a stem about "my mother" or "my father" may draw only respectful formulas from a young person in a family where criticising parents aloud is not done; the neutral or stereotyped reply is then a cultural signal, not a clinical one. Adolescents who will not talk in a first interview will often write, and a single completion such as "I am afraid... that I will do something to myself" can change the whole course of a session. Every such completion is followed up face to face.

Exam trap

Rotter blank: 40 stems, one total, 0 to 6 per stem, higher score = more maladjustment. Sacks: 60 stems, 15 areas, 4 domains, no total. Swapping 40 and 60 is the favourite distractor; 36 is Loevinger's WUSCT and 100 is Forer's. The Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank is not Rotter's Internal-External Locus of Control Scale (1966), which is an objective forced-choice questionnaire of 29 items. Sentence completion is a completion technique in Lindzey's scheme, not an association technique (so it is the wrong answer when asked for an association technique such as the Rorschach), and it uses written stems, not pictures, so it is not the test of choice when the clinician wants stories about ambiguous pictures (that is the TAT). When an item asks which test rests on Murray's need-press theory, the answer is the TAT, not any sentence completion test.

The Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study

Everyone is frustrated many times a day: the bus leaves as you reach the stop, a friend forgets a promise, a shopkeeper accuses you of something you did not do. What people do in those moments, whom they blame and whether they try to fix things, says a good deal about them. Saul Rosenzweig (1907 to 2004) spent much of his career on that question. In the 1930s he was a member of Henry Murray's Harvard Psychological Clinic, the group that produced the TAT (Section 10.6), and there he ran some of the first laboratory experiments on repression, studying whether people forget their failures more readily than their successes. As early as 1934 he proposed a simple way of sorting reactions to frustration by the direction in which blame or hostility is turned: outward onto other people or things, inward onto oneself, or nowhere at all, the frustration being smoothed over. He named the three directions extrapunitive, intropunitive and impunitive. He wanted a test that would sample these reactions quickly, in many standard situations, and that could be scored objectively.

The result was the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (the P-F Study), described in 1945 in a paper in the Journal of Personality with the revealing title "The picture-association method and its application in a study of reactions to frustration". A revised scoring manual followed in 1947, a children's form in 1948 and revised adult norms in 1950; an adolescent form was added later. The adult form, like the others, has 24 cartoon-like frames. Each shows two people (occasionally more) in a mildly frustrating everyday situation. The person on the left says something that either causes the frustration or describes it, written in a speech balloon. The person on the right is the one frustrated, and that person's balloon is empty. The faces are drawn without features or expressions, so that the picture itself does not tell the respondent what emotion to feel. The respondent writes, for each frame, the first reply that comes to mind for the frustrated person, working quickly. Some examiners then go back through unclear replies and ask what was meant, before scoring.

An invented frame (described in words, not a P-F item)

Two people stand at a bus stop in the rain. The person on the left holds out a pair of broken spectacles and says: "I'm so sorry, I sat on your glasses." The person on the right, whose face is blank, has an empty speech balloon. What does that person say?

A second invented frame: a shopkeeper leans over the counter and says to a customer, "You slipped that packet into your bag, didn't you?" The customer's balloon is empty.

The two invented frames show the two kinds of situation the test contains. In an ego-blocking situation some obstacle, a person or an object, directly interrupts, disappoints or deprives the frustrated person: the broken spectacles, a car that splashes you, a shop that closes as you arrive. In a superego-blocking situation the frustrated person is accused, blamed or charged by someone else, so the blow falls on conscience and self-esteem rather than on a wish: the shopkeeper's accusation. Most frames are ego-blocking. Rosenzweig himself called the instrument semiprojective: the situations are clearly structured around frustration and the reply is short, so the ambiguity is limited, but the respondent still has to supply the reaction. He also warned, in a 1950 paper on "levels of behaviour", that a written reply may show what the person thinks one ought to say, what the person would actually do, or a less conscious tendency, and that the clinician must judge which level is speaking.

Scoring is what makes the P-F distinctive. Every reply is coded on two dimensions at once. The first is the direction of aggression, Rosenzweig's three directions: extrapunitive, coded E (blame or hostility turned outward); intropunitive, coded I (turned on oneself); impunitive, coded M (blame avoided, the situation glossed over as no one's fault). In his later writings Rosenzweig renamed them extraggressive, intraggressive and imaggressive, and both sets of names appear in books. The second dimension is the type of reaction, that is, what the reply mainly attends to:

  • In obstacle-dominance (O-D) the frustrating obstacle itself stands out in the reply, which dwells on how bad (or how harmless) the obstacle is.
  • In ego-defence (E-D) the self is defended; the reply is mainly about who is to blame, or about protecting one's own position.
  • In need-persistence (N-P) the reply goes after a solution to the problem, by someone else, by oneself or by time.

Crossing three directions with three types gives nine scoring factors, which Figure 10.17 lays out with an invented reply in each cell. The obstacle-dominant factors are written with a prime (E′, I′, M′), the ego-defensive factors with plain capitals (E, I, M) and the need-persistent factors with small letters (e, i, m). Two further variants apply to superego-blocking frames: an underscored E, when the accused person aggressively denies the charge ("I never touched your packet!"), and an underscored I, when the person admits the act but denies real fault by pointing to unavoidable circumstances ("Yes, it went in my bag, but your shelf was falling over"). That makes eleven scoring factors in all.

Look at Figure 10.17 cell by cell and the logic becomes clear. E′ insists on how bad the obstacle is ("We've missed the whole opening!"); I′ turns the obstacle into something harmless or even welcome, sometimes with embarrassment at having troubled the other person; M′ shrinks it almost to nothing. E blames the other person; I blames oneself, with guilt or remorse; M absolves everyone, treating the event as unavoidable. The need-persistent replies all look for a way out, but differ in who is expected to provide it: e demands that someone else solve it, i offers to solve it oneself, usually to make amends, and m expects that time or ordinary circumstances will solve it, a reply of patience and conformity.

Most replies get one factor. When a reply clearly contains two (for example "You idiot! Well, let's catch the next show", which is both E and m), each factor receives half a score. The scores are then totalled across the 24 frames and turned into percentages, which give the person's profile: how often each direction and each type appears. Three further indices complete the scoring. The Group Conformity Rating (GCR) takes the frames on which most people in the norm group give the same kind of reply and reports the percentage of those frames on which this person gives it too: a measure of how conventionally, how much "like everyone else", the person reacts to frustration. A trend is a clear shift between the first and the second half of the test, for instance from blaming others early on to blaming oneself later, which Rosenzweig read as the person's second thoughts about his first reactions. And patterns combine factors to answer particular questions, such as how a person reacts specifically when accused.

Remember

Category percentage = (sum of the scores for that category ÷ number of frames scored) × 100, where a double-scored reply gives 0.5 to each of its two factors.

GCR = (number of conformity frames on which the person gives the modal reply ÷ number of conformity frames) × 100.

A worked example. Yuvraj, a 24-year-old delivery supervisor in Gurugram, is referred by his employer after a shouting match with a customer. Among other measures, the psychologist gives him the adult form. He answers all 24 frames; 20 replies get a single factor and 4 are double-scored, so those 4 contribute half a score to each of two factors. When the scores are added by direction, E (all three extrapunitive factors together) totals 12.5, I totals 5.0 and M totals 6.5 (check: 12.5 + 5.0 + 6.5 = 24). By type, O-D totals 4.0, E-D 14.5 and N-P 5.5 (check: 4.0 + 14.5 + 5.5 = 24).

Step 1: direction percentages. E% = 12.5 ÷ 24 × 100 = 0.52083 × 100 = 52.1 per cent. I% = 5.0 ÷ 24 × 100 = 20.8 per cent. M% = 6.5 ÷ 24 × 100 = 27.1 per cent (rounded to one decimal place; the three add to 100.0).

Step 2: type percentages. O-D% = 4.0 ÷ 24 × 100 = 16.7 per cent. E-D% = 14.5 ÷ 24 × 100 = 60.4 per cent. N-P% = 5.5 ÷ 24 × 100 = 22.9 per cent.

Step 3: the GCR. Suppose, for this invented example, that the scoring sheet marks 12 frames as conformity frames and Yuvraj gives the modal reply on 7 of them. GCR = 7 ÷ 12 × 100 = 58.3 per cent. (The number of conformity frames is fixed by the manual for each form and was revised over the years; the 12 here is only for practice.)

Step 4: a trend. In the first 12 frames his E scores total 8.0, two-thirds of those frames (8.0 ÷ 12 = 66.7 per cent); in the last 12 they total 4.5 (4.5 ÷ 12 = 37.5 per cent). His outward blame falls sharply as the test goes on.

Step 5: interpret against the norms. The psychologist now compares each percentage with the manual's norms for Yuvraj's age and sex, because only the comparison shows whether 52 per cent extrapunitive is high. Suppose it is clearly above the norm and his N-P is below it. The profile then describes a man whose first reaction to frustration is to blame someone else and defend himself, and who rarely moves on to solving the problem; the downward trend suggests that, given a moment, he reins himself in; a GCR near the middle of the normal range says that his replies are not bizarre or unconventional. That fits the incident at work, and it gives a plan for treatment (practising the step from blame to problem-solving). What the profile cannot say is whether Yuvraj will be aggressive tomorrow: he wrote what a cartoon character might say, at a desk, with time to think. Rosenzweig's own warning about levels applies, and the evidence on how well P-F scores predict behaviour belongs to Section 10.10.

Indian context

The P-F Study was adapted for India by Udai Pareek (1925 to 2010). Working with Rosenzweig himself, he prepared Indian adaptations of the children's form (from the late 1950s) and of the adult form, each with an Indian manual, keeping the original situations wherever possible. His book on developmental patterns in children's reactions to frustration (1964) used it widely, and Indian researchers have since used the P-F in many studies of children and adults. Pareek later became one of India's best-known organisational psychologists at the Indian Institute of Management, Ahmedabad. Do not confuse him with Uma Chowdhury, who adapted the TAT and the CAT (Sections 10.6 and 10.7).

Exam trap

Incomplete cartoon frames of everyday frustration, with an empty speech balloon for the frustrated person, are the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Saul Rosenzweig, 1945; 24 frames; children's form 1948). It is not the TAT, whose cards are full pictures about which the person tells a whole story (Section 10.6); not the Rorschach, whose stimuli are inkblots with no people or words in them (Section 10.3); and not the sentence completion test, which uses written stems with no pictures at all. Rosenzweig is not Rorschach. The three directions are extrapunitive (E, outward), intropunitive (I, inward) and impunitive (M, avoided), also called extra-, intra- and imaggressive; the three types are obstacle-dominance, ego-defence and need-persistence; nine factors, eleven with the two underscored variants. GCR stands for Group Conformity Rating. It is a completion technique, scored for frustration reactions, not for needs and press. The Indian adaptation is Udai Pareek's. Indian textbooks give several dates for the test (1944, 1945, 1948, 1949): test catalogues date its first materials to 1944, 1945 is the date of the founding paper in the Journal of Personality, and 1948 the date of the children's form. If an option list offers only one of these dates, choose it.

Story completion: fables and unfinished stories

The last completion technique moves from a sentence to a whole story. In a story completion test the examiner tells the beginning of a short story, stops at a moment of tension and asks the person, usually a child, how it ends. The best known are the fables of the Swiss psychoanalyst Louisa Düss, published in Geneva in 1940 as a "method of fables" for psychoanalysis with children: ten very short animal or family stories, each built to touch one of the conflicts of childhood that psychoanalytic theory emphasised (dependence on parents, jealousy of a new baby, fear of a parent's death, guilt, castration anxiety). An invented example in the same spirit, not one of her fables: "A little monkey goes to the market with its mother every morning. One day a new baby monkey is born, and the mother stays at home with the baby. What does the little monkey do?" A child who replies "It goes alone and gets lost" and a child who replies "It brings the baby a banana" are telling the examiner different things, or so the method assumes. The Düss fables reached English-speaking clinicians through the American child psychiatrist Louise Despert, which is why they are also called the Despert fables, and a revised form was published by Reuben Fine in 1948. A similar set of unfinished stories, the Madeleine Thomas stories, was used in Europe in the same period.

Story completion is quick, needs no materials and suits children too young for a sentence blank, and that is about all that can be said for it as a measuring device: it has no standard scoring, its reliability has been questioned, and it is used mainly with young children. Its real use is as a conversation with a child, which is how most clinicians who use it describe it.

Exam trap

Lindzey's completion family is sentence completion (Rotter, Sacks, Loevinger, Forer), story completion (Düss or Despert fables, Madeleine Thomas stories) and the Rosenzweig P-F Study. The TAT is construction (the person makes a whole story from a picture), not completion; word association is association; drawings are expression. If an item asks which technique asks the person to finish an unfinished stimulus, the answer is one of the completion techniques, never the TAT or the Rorschach.

10.9 Expressive and Choice Techniques: Drawings, Play, the Szondi and the Hand TestLower yield · 2 Qs

Every technique so far has asked the person to say or write something about a stimulus the examiner provides. The techniques in this section ask the person to do something instead: draw a person, build a world in a tray of sand, smear paint with the fingers, pick the faces one likes best. In Lindzey's terms (Section 10.2) most of them belong to the expression family, in which the manner of carrying out a free task is studied as much as the product, and a few belong to the choice or ordering family, in which the person chooses among or arranges stimuli by some criterion. Expressive techniques have one large practical advantage: they need little or no language. A five-year-old who cannot yet explain what frightens him can draw it, a patient who speaks only Tulu can arrange miniatures, and a teenager who answers every question with "fine" may still press the pencil so hard that it tears the paper. That is why drawings, above all, have been among the most widely used projective methods in child and adolescent clinics for seventy years. Whether what they show is what their interpreters claim is a separate question, which Section 10.10 answers; this section explains how each technique is given and read.

From counting details to reading the self: Goodenough and Machover's Draw-a-Person

The story of projective drawing begins with a test of intelligence. In 1926 Florence Goodenough (1886 to 1959) published Measurement of Intelligence by Drawings, built on a simple observation: as children grow, their drawings of a man acquire details (a neck, fingers, the right number of fingers, a joining of arms at the shoulders, clothing, proportion) in a regular developmental order. Count the details correctly included and you have an index of the child's intellectual maturity. Her Draw-a-Man test, revised by Dale Harris in 1963 as the Goodenough-Harris Drawing Test, is a cognitive measure, and its scoring belongs to Chapters 6 and 7. What matters here is what clinicians noticed while scoring it. Two children could earn the same score with drawings that felt utterly different: one figure large, bold and smiling, filling the page; another tiny, faint and pushed into a corner, with the arms pressed tight against the body. Perhaps the drawing carried information about the child's feelings as well as his intellect.

The person who turned that hunch into a method was Karen Machover (1902 to 1996). Born in Minsk, then in the Russian Empire, and brought to New York as a child, she worked as a clinical psychologist first at Bellevue Hospital and then for many years at the Kings County Hospital in Brooklyn, and in 1949 she published Personality Projection in the Drawing of the Human Figure. Its central idea is the body-image hypothesis: when a person draws "a person", the only model available is himself, so the drawn figure stands for the drawer's own body image and self-concept, and the paper stands for the environment in which that self lives. Anxieties, conflicts and ways of coping should therefore show in how the figure is drawn. Her technique became known as the Draw-a-Person test (DAP), and it is the founding member of the family of projective drawings.

The administration is deliberately bare. The person is given a blank sheet of paper and a pencil with an eraser and told simply to draw a person. A stick figure is not accepted; the examiner asks for a whole person. When the first drawing is finished the examiner says, "Now draw a person of the opposite sex." Throughout, the examiner watches and records the process: which figure was drawn first, the order in which body parts were drawn, where the person hesitated or erased, what was said. Then comes an inquiry. Machover asked the person to make up a story about each figure as if it were a character in a play or a novel, and to answer questions about it: how old the person is, what he or she is doing, wishes for, worries about, likes and dislikes about his or her own body. The answers are part of the record.

Machover then proposed a large catalogue of interpretive signs, which can be grouped into a few kinds. Every one of the meanings that follow is a claim from her clinical experience, not an established finding.

  • Size. A very large figure filling the page was read as expansiveness or poor control, a very small one as feelings of inadequacy, inferiority or depression.
  • Placement. Where the figure sits on the page (high or low, left or right, central or crowded into a corner) was read as the person's position in his world.
  • Line quality. Heavy, pressed lines were read as tension or assertiveness; faint, broken or sketchy lines as timidity, insecurity or anxiety.
  • Erasures and shading. Repeated erasing of a part and heavy shading were read as anxiety, especially about the body part concerned.
  • Omissions. A missing part (no hands, no feet, no mouth) was read as conflict about what that part does; hidden or missing hands, for example, as guilt or a sense of helplessness.
  • Emphasis on particular parts. Large or heavily worked eyes and ears were read as suspiciousness, a prominent mouth as oral concerns, broad shoulders as concern with strength, and so on.
  • The sex of the first figure. Most people draw their own sex first; drawing the other sex first was read as a sign of conflict about sexual identity.

The power of the method was obvious: it was quick, cheap, enjoyable for children and needed no language. Its weakness took longer to see. When later researchers tested Machover's signs one at a time, most of them failed: large eyes did not mark suspicious people, missing hands did not mark guilty ones, and clinicians' confidence in the signs turned out to rest partly on an illusion of their own making. That evidence, including Loren and Jean Chapman's famous studies of illusory correlation with exactly these drawing signs, is taught in Section 10.10. What survived is more modest: global ratings of a whole drawing (how well integrated, how bizarre, how impoverished) carry some information about overall adjustment, and drawings remain a good way to start a conversation with a child.

Exam trap

In the Draw-a-Person used projectively, interpretation rests on the assumption that the drawing reflects the person's self-concept, body image and unconscious attitudes (Machover's body-image hypothesis, 1949). It is not a measure of fine motor coordination alone (a drawing needs motor skill, but Machover read it for personality), it does not predict academic achievement precisely (a drawing scored for details does relate to intellectual maturity, but that is Goodenough's cognitive use, not Machover's), and it cannot diagnose specific neurological lesions (copying and drawing tasks may hint at brain damage, which is a neuropsychological question for Chapter 11, but no drawing localises a lesion). Goodenough's Draw-a-Man (1926) = intelligence; Machover's Draw-a-Person (1949) = personality. In Lindzey's scheme the DAP is an expression technique, not an association, construction or completion technique.

The House-Tree-Person

A year before Machover's book, a Virginia psychologist had published a drawing test with three subjects instead of one. John N. Buck, a pioneer of clinical psychology in Virginia, first described the House-Tree-Person technique (H-T-P) in short papers in 1946 and 1947 and set it out in full in 1948 in a long monograph in the Journal of Clinical Psychology, "The H-T-P technique: a qualitative and quantitative scoring manual", which is why 1948 is the date books give. Like Machover's, his method grew out of Goodenough's: Buck's quantitative scoring of the drawings gave an estimate of intelligence, and his qualitative analysis read them for personality. The intelligence scoring is rarely used today; the personality reading made the test famous.

Why a house, a tree and a person? Buck chose them, he said, because they are familiar even to very young children, because people of all ages accept the request to draw them more willingly than many other subjects, and because they stimulate more frank talk than other objects do. Each was also thought to carry a different layer of meaning. These are claimed interpretations, not established findings:

  • The house was read as the drawer's picture of home life and family relationships: how open or closed it is (doors and windows), how welcoming, how solid.
  • The tree was read as the deepest and least conscious layer, the person's basic feelings about himself and his life energy. Because a tree is further from the self than a human figure, it was thought to let people show damaged or negative feelings they would not put into a person: a scarred trunk, broken branches, a knothole, a dead tree.
  • The person was read as the layer closest to awareness: the self-concept and the person's way of relating to others, much as in Machover's test.

The H-T-P is given in two phases. In the achromatic phase the person draws, in pencil and on separate sheets, first a house, then a tree, then a person, as well as he can. The examiner times each drawing and records the order of details, pauses, erasures and comments. Then comes the post-drawing interrogation (PDI), Buck's structured set of 60 questions about the drawings (questions of the kind "Who lives in this house? Is it a happy house? How old is this tree? Is it alive? What is this person doing? How does he feel?"), which turns the drawings into stories the person tells about them. In the chromatic phase the person draws a house, a tree and a person again, this time with crayons, and is questioned again. The colour phase was meant to pull more emotional material, on the same reasoning that made Rorschach clinicians watch for reactions to the coloured cards (Section 10.3). Some examiners now give only one phase, or ask for all three objects on one page.

The H-T-P owes much of its later fame to Emanuel F. Hammer, whose book The Clinical Application of Projective Drawings (1958) gathered and extended the interpretive lore of the H-T-P and the DAP, and who edited with Buck Advances in the House-Tree-Person Technique (1969). Buck issued a revised manual in 1966. Figure 10.18 shows, on an invented set of drawings, the kinds of features an examiner notes. Notice the caption: these are features to be recorded, not signs whose meaning is proven.

Exam trap

The House-Tree-Person test was developed by John N. Buck (1948). Not Aaron T. Beck, the founder of cognitive therapy and author of the Beck Depression Inventory; not David D. Burns, the cognitive therapist who wrote the self-help book Feeling Good; not Karen Machover, who gave us the Draw-a-Person (1949). The Kinetic Family Drawing's Robert C. Burns is yet another, unrelated Burns. Emanuel Hammer extended the H-T-P (1958) but did not invent it. The phases run achromatic first (pencil), then chromatic (crayon), each followed by questions (the post-drawing interrogation). Buck's own scoring also gave an IQ estimate.

The Kinetic Family Drawing

Ask a child to draw her family and you usually get a row of people standing still and facing you, like a school photograph. That tells you who is in the family and perhaps who is big and who is small, but very little about how they live together. Robert C. Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman fixed this with one word. In the Kinetic Family Drawing (KFD), published in 1970 in Kinetic Family Drawings (K-F-D): An Introduction to Understanding Children through Kinetic Drawings, the child is asked to draw everyone in the family, including herself, doing something. The usual instruction runs roughly: "Draw a picture of everyone in your family, including you, doing something. Try to draw whole people, not cartoons or stick people." "Kinetic" means moving: the family must be shown in action, and the actions, and who does them with whom, become the material.

Burns and Kaufman's second book, Actions, Styles and Symbols in Kinetic Family Drawings (1972), named the three things to read, and clinicians add a fourth, the characteristics of the figures themselves.

  • Actions: what each person is doing, and above all whether anyone does anything with anyone else. Playing together, helping, hitting or ignoring are all actions.
  • Styles: the ways the child organises the page. In compartmentalisation the child draws lines or boxes that separate family members from each other; in encapsulation a figure is enclosed inside an object (a car, a bed, a skipping rope, a television). Other styles they described include lining the whole bottom of the page, underlining individual figures, edging (pushing the figures to the edges of the paper) and lining the top. Burns and Kaufman read compartmentalisation and encapsulation as ways of isolating oneself or others from feelings that are too strong, and a line along the bottom as a need for a firm base in an unstable family.
  • Symbols: particular objects (a hot sun, a stove, a lamp, a broom, a ball being thrown) to which they attached meanings such as warmth, nurturance, anger or a need for love.
  • Figure characteristics: relative size, distance between figures, who faces whom, who is missing, who is drawn first and how carefully each is drawn.

As with Machover's signs, the meanings attached to styles and symbols come from clinical experience, and research has not confirmed most of them (Section 10.10). The KFD's lasting value is that it gets a child talking about her family in her own terms.

A worked example. Anvi, aged nine, a Class 4 pupil in Bengaluru, has been quiet and tearful at school for about four months, her teacher reports, and she has stopped joining games at break. Her parents mention that her baby brother was born five months ago. Saloni, a trainee clinical psychologist, asks her for a Kinetic Family Drawing. The drawing, described here in words, is invented for this book.

Anvi draws her mother first, the tallest figure on the page, sitting in the middle on a sofa and feeding the baby. Next she draws her father, smaller, at the top left, at a desk with a laptop, half turned away. Then her grandmother, at the top right, standing at the stove. Last she draws herself, the smallest figure, in the bottom right corner, lying on her bed reading a book. Before she finishes she rules lines across the page so that each adult sits in a box of its own, draws the outline of her bed around her own figure, and runs a heavy line along the bottom of her box. Nobody touches anyone except the mother and the baby, and nobody looks at Anvi. Asked about the picture, she says: "Mamma is feeding the baby. Papa has office calls. Dadi is making dosa. I'm reading because nobody can play."

Step 1: who is there. Everyone is included, the baby too. Leaving out a family member, often a new sibling, is one of the commonest features clinicians look for, and Anvi has not done it.

Step 2: order and size. Mother first and largest, Anvi last and smallest. In the clinical lore, the first and largest figure is the most important to the child, and drawing oneself last and tiny suggests feeling unimportant or pushed aside.

Step 3: actions. Each adult is busy with a separate task; the only interaction on the page is between mother and baby. Anvi's own action, reading alone, is solitary.

Step 4: styles. The ruled boxes are compartmentalisation; the bed drawn round her is encapsulation; the line along the bottom of her box is lining at the bottom. Burns and Kaufman would read the three together as distance and isolation, and a wish for something firm to stand on.

Step 5: her own words. "Nobody can play" is the most informative thing in the whole record, and it came from the conversation, not from a sign.

Step 6: what Saloni may and may not conclude. She may write a hypothesis: that Anvi feels displaced and alone since her brother's birth, with everyone in the family occupied elsewhere. She then checks it in the ways that carry real evidence: when exactly the change began, what Anvi's parents and teacher observe, how Anvi behaves with her mother and the baby, and how she herself describes her feelings when asked directly. She may not conclude that Anvi is depressed, that she is neglected or that the family is "dysfunctional". A nine-year-old's drawing reflects her age (children of this age often draw figures separately), her drawing skill, what the family actually does (in many Indian homes a grandmother cooks and a father works from home every evening, and drawing that is accurate reporting, not projection), and her mood that afternoon. A single drawing on a single day is a weak basis for anything; Section 10.10 shows how weak.

Clinical link

Drawings are often requested in child protection work, and it is there that over-reading them does most harm. No drawing feature, whether genitals drawn on a figure, a missing mouth or heavy shading, has been shown to identify a child who has been abused with anything like the accuracy a court or a family deserves. A drawing can open a conversation with a frightened child; it cannot be evidence. Ethical practice treats drawing signs as hypotheses only, a point Section 10.10 develops.

Scoring drawings for adjustment: Koppitz, the DAP:SPED and the Bender read projectively

If single signs fail, can drawings be scored more carefully? Two attempts are worth knowing. The first came from Elizabeth M. Koppitz, a school psychologist already known for her developmental scoring of children's copies of the Bender designs. In Psychological Evaluation of Children's Human Figure Drawings (1968) she separated two kinds of feature in drawings by children of about 5 to 12. The first kind, developmental items, are features whose presence depends on age and maturity, the kind Goodenough counted. The second kind, emotional indicators, are features that, she argued, are not tied to age, are unusual in drawings by ordinary children and occur more often in drawings by children with emotional or behavioural problems. She listed about 30, among them poor integration of the parts of the figure, shading of the face or body, gross asymmetry of the limbs, a figure slanting markedly, a tiny figure, a very big figure, transparencies (body parts showing through clothing), crossed eyes, teeth, arms pressed tight to the body, hands cut off, a monster or grotesque figure, and the omission of the eyes, nose, mouth, body, arms, legs, feet or neck. One indicator alone meant little; two or more, Koppitz advised, suggested emotional problems worth a closer look. Her approach is more careful than Machover's because it counts signs against norms and makes no claim about what each sign means, but studies since have been mixed: children with problems do tend to include more indicators as a group, while few single indicators separate individual children well.

The second is the Draw a Person: Screening Procedure for Emotional Disturbance (DAP:SPED), published in 1991 by Jack A. Naglieri, Timothy McNeish and Achilles Bardos. It asks children and adolescents aged 6 to 17 for three drawings, a man, a woman and oneself, which can be done in a group in about 20 minutes. Each drawing is scored on a list of objectively defined items, and the total is converted to a T score, using norms from 2,260 young people, which places the child in one of three bands: further evaluation not indicated, indicated, or strongly indicated. The DAP:SPED does what Machover never did, giving standard scoring and national norms, and it claims only to screen, not to diagnose. Even so its validity evidence is limited and mixed, and it is used, like every drawing measure, as one input among many.

A third tradition read projectively a test built for something else. The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test asks the person to copy nine simple designs and is above all a test of visual-motor integration and maturation (Chapter 11). Max Hutt argued that the way a person copies (where on the page, at what size, with what pressure, whether the designs are crowded, expanded, rotated or overlapped) also reveals personality, and his adaptation of the test (first set out with Gerald Briskin in 1960, later published as the Hutt Adaptation of the Bender-Gestalt Test) included scales for reading the copies that way. Like the other drawing signs, these projective readings are now viewed sceptically. Table 10.30 gathers the drawing techniques.

Table 10.30 The drawing techniques: who built them, what the person is asked to do and what is read.
TestAuthor, yearInstructionWhat is read
Draw-a-Man (cognitive)Florence Goodenough, 1926; Harris revision 1963Draw a man, as well as you canDetails counted for intellectual maturity (Chapters 6 and 7)
Draw-a-Person (projective)Karen Machover, 1949Draw a person; then one of the opposite sex; then a story and questionsBody image and self-concept: size, placement, line, erasures, omissions
House-Tree-PersonJohn N. Buck, 1948; Hammer, 1958Draw a house, a tree and a person in pencil, then in crayon; questions after eachHome, deeper self, self and relationships (claimed); Buck's IQ estimate
Kinetic Family DrawingRobert C. Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman, 1970Draw everyone in your family, including you, doing somethingActions, styles, symbols, figure size and distance
Emotional indicatorsElizabeth M. Koppitz, 1968Draw a whole person (children about 5 to 12)About 30 indicators; two or more prompt a closer look
DAP:SPEDNaglieri, McNeish and Bardos, 1991Draw a man, a woman and yourself (ages 6 to 17)Objective items; T score for screening

Exam trap

Match the person to the drawing: Goodenough (1926, Draw-a-Man) and Harris (1963) = intelligence; Machover (1949, Draw-a-Person) = personality, body image; Buck (1948, House-Tree-Person) = personality plus an IQ estimate; Burns and Kaufman (1970, Kinetic Family Drawing) = the family doing something; Koppitz (1968) = about 30 emotional indicators in children's drawings, and also the developmental Bender scoring for children; Hutt = the Bender read projectively. All the projective drawings are Lindzey expression techniques. "Kinetic" means the figures must be doing something; compartmentalisation and encapsulation are KFD styles.

Play, sand, mosaics, finger paint and handwriting

Drawing is only one way of expressing oneself through a free task. For children the most natural is play, and from the 1920s child analysts treated a child's play as the equivalent of an adult's free associations; how play became a therapy, from Hermine Hug-Hellmuth and Melanie Klein to Virginia Axline, is told in Book 2, Section 10.2. As an assessment technique, play means giving the child standard materials, most often a set of family dolls and a doll's house, and observing what is done with them: which dolls are chosen, what happens between them, who is punished, put to bed or thrown out of the house, how the child handles aggression and how the story ends. Doll play of this kind was used widely in research on children's aggression and family attitudes in the 1940s and 1950s, and its therapeutic cousin, David Levy's release therapy, in which toys recreate a frightening event so that the child can play it out, is described in Book 2, Section 10.2.3. Like drawings, doll play needs no language, and like drawings it has no agreed scoring: what is read depends heavily on the observer.

The most elaborate play material was devised by Margaret Lowenfeld (1890 to 1973), a London paediatrician and child psychotherapist. In 1929, at the clinic that became her Institute of Child Psychology, she gave children a tray of sand and a cabinet of miniatures (people, animals, houses, trees, fences, vehicles) and invited them to build whatever they liked. The World Technique, named after the "worlds" the children built, is told in full in Book 2, Section 10.2.7, together with Dora Kalff's sandplay therapy that grew from it. Used for assessment, a world is read for what is put in and what is left out, how the tray is organised (ordered, chaotic, empty, fenced off), whether people appear at all, and what violent or protective themes run through it. Lowenfeld also devised the Lowenfeld Mosaic Test, published as a book in 1954 after many years of use: the person is given a box of flat pieces in several geometric shapes (squares, diamonds and several kinds of triangle) and several colours, and asked to make anything at all on a tray. The examiner records the process and classifies the product, for example as a picture of an object, an abstract pattern, a scattered or incomplete design, and reads its organisation and use of colour. Some books place Lowenfeld's techniques in Lindzey's construction family, because the person builds a product; most place them under expression, because the manner of building is what is studied.

As a medium for children's self-expression, finger painting was introduced by the American teacher Ruth Faison Shaw (1889 to 1969), whose book Finger Painting: A Perfect Medium for Self-Expression appeared in 1934, and turned into a diagnostic technique by Peter J. Napoli, whose monograph Finger Painting and Personality Diagnosis (1946) proposed reading the colours chosen, the movements, the pressure and the way the child approaches the messy material (eagerly, fastidiously, with disgust). Lawrence Frank had cited finger painting as an example of what he called constitutive methods, in which a person imposes structure on formless material (Section 10.2). Painting and modelling as therapies belong to art therapy (Book 2, Section 10.10.1), and acting out roles in front of an audience, which Lindzey also counted as expressive, belongs to psychodrama (Book 2, Section 7.11).

Last in this family comes the oldest and the least defensible technique, graphology: the analysis of handwriting to infer personality. It long predates psychology and is still used by some employers in Europe. Frank named handwriting as his example of refractive methods, in which personality is "refracted" through a conventional medium (Section 10.2), and in Lindzey's scheme it is an expressive technique. The evidence is plain. In a meta-analysis of seventeen studies of graphology in personnel selection, Efrat Neter and Gershon Ben-Shakhar (1989) found that when the handwriting samples were neutral in content (copied text, so that what was written could not give anything away) graphologists' predictions were close to zero in validity, and that psychologists with no training in graphology did as well as or better than graphologists. The authors concluded that whatever small validity graphology shows probably comes from what people write, not from how they write it. Graphology has no place in clinical assessment.

Exam trap

The World Technique (sand tray, 1929) and the Mosaic Test (coloured pieces, book 1954) are both Margaret Lowenfeld's; sandplay therapy is Dora Kalff's, and Victor Lowenfeld, who described stages of children's art, is a different person. Finger painting: Ruth Faison Shaw introduced it, Peter Napoli used it diagnostically, and Frank called it constitutive. Graphology is Frank's example of a refractive method and has no validity once the content of the writing is controlled. Play, drawing, finger painting, role playing and handwriting are all Lindzey expression techniques.

Choice and ordering techniques: the Szondi, picture arrangement and colours; and the Hand Test

The choice and ordering family asks the least of the person: no story, no drawing, not even a word, only a choice among stimuli or an arrangement of them. Because the responses can be counted directly, some textbooks describe choice techniques as a step towards making projective methods objective. The classic example is also the strangest test in this chapter.

Leopold Szondi (1893 to 1986) was a Hungarian psychiatrist, born in Nyitra (now Nitra in Slovakia) and raised in Budapest, where he worked on heredity and psychopathology. In June 1944, as Hungary's Jews were being deported, he and his family were among the passengers of the so-called Kasztner train, which took them to the Bergen-Belsen camp; in December 1944 they were released to Switzerland, and he spent the rest of his life in Zurich. Szondi believed that hidden, recessive genes shape not only illness but a person's choices of friends, lovers, occupation and even manner of death, through an attraction he called genotropism: people are drawn to others who carry latent genes like their own. These shared hereditary tendencies made up what he called the familial unconscious, a layer between Freud's personal unconscious and Jung's collective one, and his "fate analysis" (Schicksalsanalyse, first volume 1944) set out to map it.

The Szondi Test, developed in the 1930s and set out in Experimentelle Triebdiagnostik (Experimental Diagnostics of Drives, 1947), follows directly from that theory. It uses 48 photographs of the faces of psychiatric patients, arranged in six sets of eight. Each set contains one face from each of eight diagnostic groups of the time, which stand for eight drives: the homosexual (h), the sadist (s), the epileptic (e), the hysteric (hy), the catatonic schizophrenic (k), the paranoid schizophrenic (p), the depressive (d) and the manic (m), grouped in pairs into four "vectors" (sexual, paroxysmal, ego and contact). Homosexuality was still classed as a mental disorder when the test was built; Book 3, Section 9.10 tells how that changed. From each set the person picks the two faces liked most and the two disliked most. If a person keeps choosing the face of, say, the depressive, the theory says that he shares something of the depressive's hidden drive. The choices across the six sets give a profile of likes and dislikes for each of the eight drives.

The Szondi is the textbook example of Lindzey's choice family, and that is almost all it is good for. Studies in the 1950s found that people's choices among photographs of the same diagnostic group were inconsistent and that the predictions of the theory did not hold; later work suggested that choices reflect ordinary facial cues and stereotypes, such as how attractive or friendly a face looks. Genotropism has no support in genetics. In a 2006 survey of experts on discredited methods, the test was rated "probably discredited". It survives in a few European centres devoted to Szondi's ideas and in examination papers.

Ordering is the other half of the family. The Tomkins-Horn Picture Arrangement Test, developed in the 1940s by Silvan Tomkins (1911 to 1991), later known for his theory of affect, and Daniel Horn, and published with a manual by Tomkins and John Miner in 1957, consists of 25 plates. Each plate shows three line sketches of related situations involving the same central figure, and the person arranges the three into the order that makes the best sense and writes a sentence for each, so that the three become a little story. The arrangements are interpreted by comparing them with how often each arrangement is chosen in a large normative sample, which made the test one of the most statistical projective methods of its day; its reliability has nevertheless been disputed. It should not be confused with the Picture Arrangement subtest of the older Wechsler scales, which uses ordering of cartoon panels to test social reasoning and belongs to Chapter 6.

A choice technique with a large popular following and no scientific one is the Lüscher Colour Test, first published in 1947 by the Swiss psychotherapist Max Lüscher (1923 to 2017) of Basel and translated into English in 1969. The person ranks eight coloured cards from most to least preferred, and the order is read as a description of personality and current emotional state. Independent tests have not supported it: in one study, people who preferred red and yellow were no more extraverted than those who preferred blue and green, as the theory predicts they should be, and its comparisons with standard inventories show little agreement. Its interpretations read like the vague, flattering descriptions of the Barnum effect (Section 10.10).

Finally, a small test that sits a little apart. The Hand Test of the American psychologist Edwin E. Wagner (born 1930), first published in 1962 in a book Wagner wrote with Bruce Bricklin and Zygmunt Piotrowski (the Rorschach systematiser of Section 10.5) and given a revised manual by Wagner in 1983, uses 10 cards: nine simple line drawings of a hand in an ambiguous position, and a tenth card that is blank, on which the person imagines a hand. For each card the question is "What might this hand be doing?" Wagner's reasoning was that hands are the body's main instrument for acting on the world, so the actions a person attributes to pictured hands reveal the action tendencies closest to the surface, the behaviour most likely to be expressed. Responses are sorted into categories grouped as interpersonal (such as affection, dependence, communication, exhibition, direction and aggression), environmental (acquisition, active and passive actions), maladjustive (tension, crippled, fear) and withdrawal (description, bizarre responses, failure to respond). Its best-known summary score, the Acting Out Ratio, sets the friendly, cooperative responses against the directive and aggressive ones, and the test has been used mostly to estimate the risk of aggressive acting out. Because the person says what an ambiguous picture suggests, the Hand Test is sorted with the association techniques, close to the Rorschach (Section 10.2), although it appeared after Lindzey's 1959 paper, so Lindzey himself never placed it, and it is usually taught with the minor techniques. It is quick (about ten minutes) and is scored from a manual with normative tables, but its standing is weak: in a 2015 poll of 139 experts in child and adolescent mental health led by Gerald Koocher, the Hand Test and the Szondi were both among the five assessment methods rated most discredited for assessing personality in young people. Table 10.31 gathers the play, art and choice techniques.

Table 10.31 Play, art, choice and ordering techniques at a glance.

Wagner, 1962; not "Edward") asks what a hand might be doing and is used to estimate acting out.

Scan Your Brain
