10.10 How Good Are Projective Techniques? Reliability, Validity and Their Place TodayLower yield · not asked yet

For nine sections one small word has done a great deal of work. Colour responses are claimed to reflect emotional control; a tiny figure in a drawing is claimed to signal low self-esteem; the hero of a TAT story is claimed to carry the storyteller's own needs. This section cashes those claims. It asks of projective techniques the questions Chapter 3 asked of every test and Chapter 4 asks again: if two psychologists scored the same record, or the same person were tested twice, would the result be the same? Does the score tell us something true about the person? And does it tell us anything we could not have learned more cheaply from an interview and a questionnaire?

Few questions in psychology have produced a longer or more bitter quarrel. On one side stand researchers such as Scott O. Lilienfeld (1960 to 2020), James M. Wood and Howard N. Garb, who have argued that most projective scores fail the ordinary tests of a psychological measure. On the other stand researchers such as Gregory J. Meyer and Joni L. Mihura, who have argued that a carefully chosen set of scores passes them well, and who went on to build R-PAS (Section 10.5) on that set. Read closely, the two camps agree on more than their tone suggests, and by the end of this section you should be able to say exactly where. One idea makes the whole debate easier to follow. A projective technique is not one test but a family of scores and of ways of reading them. "Is the Rorschach valid?" has no single answer. "Is the form quality of Rorschach responses a valid index of distorted perception?" has one, and so does "Is the absence of hands in a child's drawing a valid sign of guilt?". The answers are different.

The yardstick: standardisation and norms

Every test in this book is judged by the same standards: a fixed way of giving it, an objective rule for scoring it, norms from a relevant comparison group, reliable scores, and evidence that the scores mean what they are said to mean for each use (Chapter 1 sets out the standards; Chapter 4 teaches validity in full). Projective techniques were born in clinics rather than in test-construction laboratories, and they meet these standards unevenly. Table 10.32 is the scorecard that the rest of this section fills in.

Table 10.32 The psychometric scorecard for projective techniques.
StandardThe question it asksThe usual finding
StandardisationIs everyone given the same test in the same way?Often not: rival scoring systems, free choice of cards, examiner effects
NormsIs there a relevant comparison group?Missing or old for many techniques; the Comprehensive System norms made ordinary people look disturbed
Scorer reliabilityDo two trained scorers agree?Good for structured systems (Exner, R-PAS, Rotter ISB); unknown for intuitive reading
Retest and internal consistencyIs the score stable and coherent?Modest; low alpha for picture stories, which defenders call the wrong yardstick
ValidityDoes the score mean what it claims?Supported for a small number of scores; most single signs unsupported
Incremental validityDoes it add to an interview and an inventory?Rarely demonstrated
UtilityDoes using it improve decisions or treatment?Little direct evidence

Start with standardisation, the requirement that every person meets the same test. Before 1974 American clinicians scored the Rorschach by five rival systems (Section 10.5), so a "Rorschach" in one hospital and a "Rorschach" in the next were in effect different tests, and research done with one could not be pooled with research done with another. Exner's Comprehensive System fixed the instructions, the seating and the coding, and R-PAS later controlled the number of responses as well (Section 10.5). The TAT never had that moment. Murray left a manual but no single scoring system, clinicians choose their own selection of cards (Section 10.6), and when Garb, Wood, Lilienfeld and M. Teresa Nezworski reviewed the surveys of practice in 2002 they concluded that almost all clinicians who use the TAT interpret it subjectively, without any formal scoring system. Machover's drawing signs (Section 10.9) were never given scoring rules or norms at all.

Why does this matter so much? Because administration changes scores, not just style. On the Rorschach, a person who is encouraged gives more responses, and every extra response is another chance to produce an unusual detail, a poor form or a special score; a long record and a short record from the same person can look like two different personalities. An examiner's tone, sex, warmth and even seating position can change what a person says to an ambiguous stimulus, which is why Exner seated the examiner beside the person rather than opposite. A technique without a fixed procedure has no fixed meaning.

The second standard is norms: a score means something only when it is compared with the scores of a relevant reference group (Chapter 5). Here the most important finding in the modern Rorschach literature came from an unglamorous study. In 1999 Thomas W. Shaffer, Philip Erdberg and John Haroian gave the Rorschach, following every Comprehensive System rule, to 123 nonpatient adults in California, people chosen because they were ordinary members of the community rather than patients. Measured against Exner's published norms, these healthy people looked disturbed. Their mean X−% (the proportion of responses whose form fits the blot badly) was 0.21, against 0.07 in the norms, more than two standard deviations higher; their mean X+% (the proportion of conventional, good-fitting forms) was 0.51, against 0.79 in the norms, more than three standard deviations lower. A year later Mel Hamel, Shaffer and Erdberg reported the same pattern in 100 nonpatient children aged 6 to 12 whose behaviour, rated by their parents, was better than average: read against the norms, their records suggested serious disturbance, and the Schizophrenia Index in particular labelled far too many of these ordinary children as disturbed.

Worse followed. In 2001 Exner reported that 221 of the 700 protocols in his 1993 adult normative sample were duplicates, the same records counted twice, so the sample held only 479 distinct people; he revised it. Wood, Nezworski, Garb and Lilienfeld then pooled the nonpatient comparison groups of 32 studies and examined 14 variables central to interpretation. On all 14, ordinary adults looked pathological against the norms: self-focused, perceptually inaccurate, depressed, anxious and emotionally constricted. The median difference was a d of .73, meaning that the typical nonpatient group sat almost three quarters of a standard deviation away from the published norms, always in the direction of illness. Critics summed the problem up in one word: the norms overpathologise normal people. Meyer replied with nine international studies of 2,125 nonclinical people and found them only about four tenths of a standard deviation more impaired than the norms across 69 scores; when Wood and colleagues reanalysed his data for their 14 critical variables, the gap was about eight tenths. The constructive answer came in 2007, when Meyer, Erdberg and Shaffer combined 21 adult samples from 17 countries into international reference values. The adult samples from around the world turned out to be quite similar to one another, but the child and adolescent samples were so unstable that the authors advised clinicians not to use many Comprehensive System scores to draw normative conclusions about psychopathology in children at all. R-PAS built its norms on international data of this kind (Section 10.5).

To see why a norm can matter more than the person, push one score through. The z score of Chapter 5 tells you how many standard deviations a score lies from the mean of the reference group:

z = (X − M) / SD

Here X is the person's score, M is the mean of the norm group and SD is its standard deviation. Take an ordinary adult whose X+% is 0.51, the average of the Shaffer sample, and compare it with a norm mean of 0.79. To keep the arithmetic simple, suppose, purely for illustration, that the norm SD is 0.09. Then X − M = 0.51 − 0.79 = −0.28, and z = −0.28 / 0.09 = −3.11. On the normal curve, a z of −3.11 is lower than about 999 people in 1,000. In plain words: an entirely average healthy adult, judged against those norms, would be described as having poorer perceptual accuracy than all but one person in a thousand. Nothing about the person produced that verdict. The reference group did.

Exam trap

A projective score is only as good as its norms and its standard administration. Shaffer, Erdberg and Haroian (1999) showed that healthy adults look disturbed against the Comprehensive System norms (X−% 0.21 against 0.07; X+% 0.51 against 0.79), and the 1993 normative sample contained 221 duplicate records. The remedy was international norms (Meyer, Erdberg and Shaffer, 2007), later used by R-PAS. Do not confuse norm problems (wrong comparison group) with reliability problems (inconsistent scores): a perfectly reliable score can still be compared with the wrong norms.

Reliability: scorers, occasions and cards

Reliability is the subject of Chapter 3, and its most important lesson for this chapter is already there. Section 3.6.9 tells how Exner admitted no Comprehensive System code that failed to reach .85 agreement between scorers, how Wood, Nezworski and Stejskal argued in 1996 that this agreement had never been properly demonstrated, and how Meyer and colleagues answered in 2002 with intraclass correlations whose medians ran from .82 to .97. Read that section rather than a summary of it here; its conclusion is the one that matters. When a projective technique has explicit coding rules and trained coders, two scorers can agree about as well as two scorers of any clinical rating. When the "scoring" is a clinician's intuitive reading of the whole record, nobody knows how well two clinicians agree, because the question has rarely been asked.

The completion techniques show the same contrast from the other side. The Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (Section 10.8) rates each of its 40 completions on a seven-point scale with printed examples, and its 1950 manual reported interscorer correlations of about .91 and .96 in samples of men and women, and corrected split-half reliabilities of .84 for the men and .83 for the women. The manual itself noted that the odd-even split was unsuitable, because the stems are not equivalent, so the halves were matched by content, the same problem Section 3.4.9 describes for the Rorschach cards. A projective format, in other words, is not doomed to unreliability. Explicit rules make the difference. For drawings the point is easy to see: whether a figure is less than five centimetres tall, or has no hands, can be scored by anyone with a ruler and a checklist; whether its lines look "aggressive" depends on the rater unless the manual defines the term.

Stability over time, the retest reliability of Section 3.2, is a harder question, because a person who sees the same inkblots or pictures again may remember earlier answers, try deliberately to be original, or be in a different mood. For the Rorschach, Cato Grønnerød pooled 36 retest samples in a 2003 meta-analysis (a meta-analysis combines the results of many studies statistically, so that the conclusion rests on all of them rather than on the most quoted one). He judged temporal stability high, found the Comprehensive System more stable than the older systems, and found, as you would expect, that stability fell as the interval between the two testings grew. Picture-story scores are less stable. Retest correlations for the achievement motive scored from TAT-type stories (Section 10.6) have generally been in the .30 range over intervals of several weeks. Part of the reason may be the instructions: a person who was asked for "creative" stories the first time may try not to repeat himself the second. When Allan Lundy retested 47 high-school students after a year in 1985, with instructions designed to break that set, the retest correlations were .48 for the need for affiliation and .56 for the need for intimacy, about the level of many inventory scales; he also found that alpha considerably underestimated this retest reliability, the opposite of what classical theory expects. Oliver Schultheiss and Joyce Pang, reviewing the evidence in 2007, found, as you would expect, that stability falls as the interval between the testings grows.

The fiercest argument concerns internal consistency. In 1972 the sociologist Doris R. Entwisle reviewed the "fantasy-based" measures of achievement motivation in Psychological Bulletin under the pointed title "To dispel fantasies about fantasy-based measures of achievement motivation", and concluded that their internal consistency (coefficient alpha) rarely exceeded .30 to .40. Little later evidence has challenged that figure. By the standards of Section 3.5, a score of which perhaps two thirds of the variance is error should not be used to make a decision about anyone.

The formula of Section 3.5.8 shows why a handful of cards can give such low figures. Standardised alpha depends only on the number of items and on how strongly they correlate on average:

Remember

αstandardised = k r̄ / [1 + (k − 1) r̄]

k is the number of items (here, cards) and r̄ ("r bar") is the mean correlation between the scores of any two cards.

Suppose a clinician scores achievement imagery on six cards, and the scores on any two cards correlate, on average, 0.10, a plausible figure for single stories given the alphas just quoted. Then k r̄ = 6 × 0.10 = 0.60; the denominator is 1 + (5 × 0.10) = 1 + 0.50 = 1.50; and alpha = 0.60 / 1.50 = 0.40. Doubling to twelve cards gives 12 × 0.10 = 1.20 over 1 + 1.10 = 2.10, so alpha = 1.20 / 2.10 = 0.571, about 0.57. How many cards would reach 0.80? The Spearman-Brown formula solved for length (Section 3.4.6) gives the factor by which a test must be lengthened: n = rtarget(1 − rnow) / [rnow(1 − rtarget)]. Treat one card as a one-item test with reliability 0.10: n = 0.80 × (1 − 0.10) / [0.10 × (1 − 0.80)] = 0.80 × 0.90 / (0.10 × 0.20) = 0.72 / 0.02 = 36 cards. That is more cards than the whole TAT contains, and no clinician would sit a client through 36 stories. By classical rules, then, a picture-story score is a short, noisy measure; the only question is whether that is the end of the matter.

The motivation researcher John W. Atkinson argued that it is not. In the theory he built with David Birch, The Dynamics of Action (1970), expressing a motive partly satisfies it for a while. A person with a strong need for achievement who writes a story full of striving has, for the moment, discharged some of that tendency, so the next story is more likely to be about something else; then the tendency builds again. The result is a zigzag, the saw-tooth effect, as it came to be called, in the achievement content of successive stories: high, low, high, low (Figure 10.19). Such a person is consistently high in motive over the whole set. But when many storytellers zigzag, each to their own rhythm, the scores on any two cards correlate weakly across people, so alpha comes out low or even negative. In computer simulations reported in 1977, Atkinson and his colleagues showed that a measure built on these assumptions could predict behaviour well while having very low internal consistency. Defenders add a second argument from the design of the test: the cards are meant to differ, each pulling for a different theme (a family scene, a solitary figure, a sexual tension), so they are not parallel items and should not be expected to correlate like the items of an inventory.

Is the argument right? It is ingenious, and it explains real features of the data, but it has not settled the matter. In 2002 Francis Tuerlinckx, Paul De Boeck and Willy Lens turned three accounts of how people answer TAT cards into item response models and fitted them to need-for-achievement stories. The account with a dynamic, motive-satisfying component did not fit best. The best fit came from a "drop-out" model in which some responses depend on the person's motive and on how strongly the particular card pulls for achievement, while other responses carry no information about the motive at all. The fair summary is this. Scorer agreement for formally scored picture stories is good; internal consistency is low, and there are reasons to think alpha understates the usefulness of the total; retest stability is moderate over short intervals and falls over years. None of this resembles the reliability of an objective inventory scale, whose retest and alpha coefficients usually lie in the .70s and .80s (Chapter 9).

Exam trap

"Superior retest reliability" is not an advantage of projective tests (RML 2025, Section 10.1): their retest and internal consistency figures are modest, and scorer agreement is good only with a structured scoring system (Exner, R-PAS, Rotter ISB). Entwisle (1972) found that the internal consistency of story-based achievement scores rarely exceeded .30 to .40; Atkinson's saw-tooth (dynamics of action) argument says alpha is the wrong yardstick for stories. The odd-even split is unsuitable for the Rorschach and for sentence blanks because cards and stems are not equivalent items (Section 3.4.9).

Validity and the clinician's eye: illusory correlation and the Barnum effect

Reliability is necessary but not enough; the real question is validity, whether a score means what it is said to mean (Chapter 4). Here projective techniques met a puzzle that has taught psychology more about clinicians than about tests. From the 1950s onwards, study after study failed to find the relationships that the manuals described: the large head that was supposed to mean intellectual concern, the elaborated eyes that were supposed to mean suspiciousness, the Rorschach contents that were supposed to mark particular conflicts. Yet experienced clinicians went on reporting that they saw these relationships in their own patients, every week. Were the researchers measuring the wrong things, or were the clinicians seeing things that were not there? Loren J. Chapman and Jean P. Chapman designed a pair of experiments to find out.

Key study: Illusory correlation (Chapman and Chapman, 1967 and 1969)

Question. Why do clinicians agree so strongly on diagnostic signs that research does not support? Method (1967). The Chapmans first asked practising clinicians which features of Draw-a-Person drawings go with which symptoms. Agreement was striking: 91 percent said that atypical eyes indicate suspiciousness, and 82 percent that a large or emphasised head goes with worry about one's intelligence. Then they showed undergraduates, who knew nothing about the test, a series of drawings, each labelled with two symptom statements of the kind "The man who drew this is (a) suspicious of other people, (b) worried about how manly he is", taken from the clinicians' list. The statements were assigned to the drawings at random, so that every statement appeared with every kind of drawing equally often: in the materials, the true correlation between any sign and any symptom was zero (Figure 10.20). Result. Asked afterwards what they had noticed, the students "discovered" exactly the links the clinicians reported: atypical eyes with suspiciousness, broad shoulders with worry about manliness. The 1969 study. The Chapmans repeated the design with Rorschach signs of male homosexuality, then still a diagnosis in DSM-II (Book 3, Section 9.10 tells how and why it was removed). Of 32 experienced clinicians surveyed, most named signs such as anal content, feminine clothing or figures of uncertain sex, which research had shown to be invalid; students shown records in which those signs had no relationship to the statements "found" the same signs. Almost all the clinicians missed the two signs that research had found to be valid. Conclusion. The signs had survived not because the data supported them but because they match what people expect. Criticism. The tasks were brief and artificial and used students, but the clinicians' own survey answers showed the same pattern, and the finding was later repeated with the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (Starr and Katkin, 1969) and with the MMPI scales (Dowling and Graham, 1976).

Loren Chapman had named the phenomenon in the same year, in experiments with pairs of words. An illusory correlation, in his definition, is the report by observers of a correlation between two classes of events which in reality are not correlated, or are correlated less than reported, or are correlated in the opposite direction. The source of the error is association by meaning. Suspicion "goes with" eyes and manliness with shoulders in ordinary language and imagery, and when a judge watches a stream of cases, those meaningful pairings stand out and are remembered, while the many cases that do not fit fade. Notice what this does to the defence "I have seen it in my own patients a hundred times". Clinical experience feels like evidence, but experience without a careful tally of the cases that did not fit is exactly the condition under which illusory correlations grow. The only protection is data collected so that the scorer does not know the answer in advance. That is why the blind designs you have met elsewhere carry such weight: Evelyn Hooker's expert judges, who could not tell homosexual from heterosexual men from their Rorschach, TAT and MAPS records once the labels were removed (Book 3, Section 9.10.3), and the Rorschach experts of this chapter's opening story.

A second bias works on the reader of the report rather than on the scorer. Book 2, Section 2.9.9 tells the story of the Barnum effect: Bertram Forer's 1949 classroom demonstration, in which students rated one horoscope-like paragraph as an accurate description of themselves, and Paul Meehl's 1956 name for the tendency to accept vague, generally true statements as personal ones. Projective reports are especially exposed to it, because global interpretation invites general sentences. Suppose Himani, a 27-year-old teacher, is assessed with a Rorschach and a TAT, and the report says: "Beneath a capable and sociable surface she has some doubts about her worth, and she can be self-critical when things go wrong; she wants close relationships but is wary of being let down." Himani reads it and says it describes her perfectly. Her agreement proves nothing, because the same paragraph would fit most of the people who have ever been assessed. Apply one test to every sentence of a projective report: would it be equally true of the next three clients? A sentence such as "12 of her 22 responses fitted the blot poorly (X−% = 12 / 22 = 0.55), far above the rates found in nonpatient samples, including the international reference samples" passes: whether or not it is good news, it says something about Himani that would not fit most people, and it can be checked. A sentence that fails is decoration.

Illusory correlation and the Barnum effect together explain a great deal of the history of this chapter. The first lets a clinician believe in an invalid sign; the second makes the resulting report feel accurate to its reader. Both are biases of human judgement, not defects of inkblots or pictures, and both are cured by the same thing: scores whose meaning has been checked against criteria by people who did not know the answer in advance.

Exam trap

Illusory correlation (Loren and Jean Chapman, 1967 for Draw-a-Person signs, 1969 for Rorschach signs) is the perception of a relationship that is not in the data; it explains why invalid signs survive in clinical lore. The Barnum effect (named by Meehl, 1956; demonstrated by Forer, 1949) is the acceptance of vague, generally true statements as personal descriptions; it explains why vague reports feel accurate. Do not swap them, and do not attribute illusory correlation to Meehl or the Barnum effect to the Chapmans.

What the big reviews found

By the 1990s the argument had moved from single studies to reviews of whole literatures. The most influential critical review is Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb's monograph "The scientific status of projective techniques", published in 2000 in Psychological Science in the Public Interest, a journal of authoritative reviews on questions of public importance. They examined the norms, reliability, validity, incremental validity and treatment utility of the three most used techniques, the Rorschach, the TAT and human figure drawings. Their conclusions were blunt. A small number of Rorschach and TAT indexes have empirical support; the substantial majority do not; the evidence for figure drawings is even more limited; and, with a few exceptions, projective indexes have not consistently added validity to other psychometric information. A new meta-analysis in the same monograph asked whether the three instruments can detect child sexual abuse. Some did better than chance, but there were virtually no findings replicated across independent research teams, and the published studies reported markedly larger effects than the unpublished ones. That last result is the file drawer effect: studies that find nothing tend to stay unpublished in the researcher's drawer, so the published literature overstates how well a test works. Three years later Wood, Nezworski, Lilienfeld and Garb put the case against the Comprehensive System before general readers in What's Wrong with the Rorschach? Science Confronts the Controversial Inkblot Test (2003).

The defence was not silent. In 1999 Jordan B. Hiller, Robert Rosenthal, Robert F. Bornstein and their colleagues compared the validity of the Rorschach and of the MMPI, the most researched objective inventory (Chapter 9), across the published literature. The unweighted mean validity coefficients were almost identical, .29 for the Rorschach and .30 for the MMPI, and not reliably different. The two tests differed in where they did best: the MMPI had larger coefficients when the criterion was a psychiatric diagnosis or a self-report, the Rorschach when the criteria were objective ones. In 2003 Stephen Hibbard published a point-by-point critique of the Lilienfeld monograph, arguing that it had omitted positive evidence. The two sides were partly arguing about different things: the defenders about the best-researched scores, the critics about the way the tests are usually used.

Which scores, then, survive? In 2002 Garb, Wood, Lilienfeld and Nezworski turned the evidence into practical guidelines for clinicians, using three criteria for recommending any index: it must show a consistent relationship with a particular disorder, trait or symptom; the supporting studies must be methodologically adequate; and the findings must be replicated by independent investigators. By those criteria the Rorschach scores of poor form quality and deviant verbalisations can help to detect thought disorder, and so help with the diagnosis of schizophrenia and of other conditions in which disordered thinking appears, such as bipolar disorder and schizotypal personality disorder; and the Rorschach Oral Dependency scale of Joseph Masling and colleagues (1967) is reasonably well supported as a measure of dependent personality traits. Most other claims failed. No distinctive Rorschach pattern has been found for most specific disorders, and the Depression Index (DEPI) is the clearest example: independent investigators have usually found no significant relationship between the original or the revised DEPI and diagnoses of depression. For the TAT, subjective interpretation has little support; Drew Westen's Social Cognition and Object Relations Scale (SCORS) has provisional support for identifying borderline personality disorder, and McClelland's achievement scoring has been used to help predict occupational success, though its correlations with real-world achievement are relatively low.

Figure drawings fared worst. Researchers distinguish two ways of reading them. The sign approach draws an inference from one isolated feature (a tiny figure means depression; large eyes mean suspiciousness). The global approach bases a judgement on the drawing as a whole, usually with a rating scale. Reviewers from Clifford H. Swensen in 1957 and 1968 onwards found little support for Machover's single signs, and later reviewers concluded that the sign approach yields inferences of negligible or zero validity; even the belief that a small figure indicates depression was not borne out when it was tested directly in 1996. Global, normed scales do better but not well. The Draw-a-Person: Screening Procedure for Emotional Disturbance (DAP:SPED, Section 10.9) was normed on 2,260 young people aged 6 to 17. In a 1992 study by Naglieri and Steven Pfeiffer it was used to separate 54 ordinary pupils from 54 pupils with conduct and oppositional defiant disorders; the clinical group scored significantly higher, with a large effect (d = .76), yet the test classified 77.8 percent of the ordinary pupils and 48.2 percent of the clinical pupils correctly. Turn those percentages into children: 0.778 × 54 ≈ 42 ordinary pupils were correctly passed, and 0.482 × 54 ≈ 26 disturbed pupils were correctly flagged, so 54 − 26 = 28 of the 54 disturbed pupils, more than half, were missed. Better than chance, as the researchers said, but far from good enough to decide anything about a single child.

The surprise of the reviews was a sentence completion test. The Washington University Sentence Completion Test of Jane Loevinger (Section 10.8), 36 stems scored by a detailed manual for the stage of ego development, has been validated so extensively that the critics themselves called it arguably the best-validated projective technique of all, while noting that practitioners rarely use it.

Key study: Mihura and colleagues' meta-analyses of the Comprehensive System (2013)

Question. Which of the main Comprehensive System variables are valid, judged only by peer-reviewed evidence? Method. Mihura, Meyer, Dumitrascu and Bombel systematically gathered the published validity studies for the 65 main variables and ran a separate meta-analysis for each variable that had been studied, published in Psychological Bulletin. They kept apart two kinds of criterion: externally assessed ones (observer ratings, psychiatric diagnoses) and introspectively assessed ones (the person's own self-report). Result. Against external criteria, the mean validity across 53 meta-analyses was r = .27; against self-report criteria, across 42 meta-analyses, it was only r = .08. Judged against external criteria with effect-size benchmarks, 13 variables had excellent support, 17 good, 10 modest, 13 little or none, and 12 had no construct-relevant validity studies at all (Figure 10.21). The best-supported variables mostly assess cognitive and perceptual processes, such as the Perceptual-Thinking Index (PTI) and the Synthesized Response, the synthesised (DQ+) responses of Section 10.4 that join separate parts of the blot into one meaningful relationship; the least supported were very rare scores, such as Colour Projection, or newer scales, such as the Egocentricity Index and the Isolation Index. Conclusion. The authors called their findings less positive, more nuanced and more inclusive than those in the Comprehensive System's own manual. Consequence. The R-PAS authors kept the well-supported variables and dropped or demoted the rest (Section 10.5), and a 2015 reply by Wood, Garb, Nezworski, Lilienfeld and Duke agreed that four groups of cognitive scores, which they called the "Rorschach cognitive quartet", are related to cognitive impairment and thought disorder and can now be endorsed for some uses, while arguing that by leaving out unpublished studies the meta-analyses had overestimated the validity of many noncognitive scores.

One more claim from Section 10.1 needs checking against the evidence: that projective tests are hard to fake. The logic is sound as far as it goes, since a person who cannot tell what a "good" answer is cannot easily give one. But in 1980 Samuel Albert, Hayward M. Fox and Marvin W. Kahn asked people to fake paranoid schizophrenia on the Rorschach, some after being told about the disorder and some without, and gave the faked records, mixed with records from real patients, to Rorschach experts who did not know which was which. The experts could not detect the fakes, and the informed fakers were labelled psychotic more often than the genuine patients were. The fair statement is narrower than the textbook claim: a projective technique disguises its purpose, which makes naive self-presentation harder, but it does not make the record immune to a motivated, informed person, and it has nothing as well established as the validity scales by which the MMPI-2 checks for faking (Chapter 9).

The same verdict applies to the attractive single signs that fill older manuals: colour shock and shading shock on the Rorschach (Section 10.3), the omitted hands or heavy shading of a drawing, the hero who always fails. Each rests mainly on clinical tradition and on the kind of experience the Chapmans showed to be unreliable. Treat each as a question to ask, never as an answer. Table 10.33 gathers the verdicts.

Table 10.33 What the evidence supports, technique by technique.
TechniqueScores with research supportLittle or no supportBottom line
Rorschach (CS, R-PAS)Form quality and deviant verbalisations (thought disorder, PTI); Synthesized Response; Oral Dependency scaleDEPI for depression; Egocentricity Index; colour shock and similar single signsGood for perception and thinking; use supported scores and current norms
TAT and picture storiesFormally scored implicit motives (achievement and others); SCORS, provisionallyIntuitive, unscored reading; diagnosisValid for motives when formally scored
Figure drawingsGlobal normed ratings such as DAP:SPED, as a rough screenSingle signs (eyes, head size, tiny figure, omitted parts)Weakest evidence; never decisive
Sentence completionWUSCT (ego development); Rotter ISB scored by its manualUnscored impressionistic readingIncludes the best-validated projective test
Any techniqueNone replicatedDetecting child physical or sexual abuseDo not use for this purpose
Exam trap

Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb (2000), in Psychological Science in the Public Interest: a small number of Rorschach and TAT indexes are supported, most are not, drawings least of all. Mihura and colleagues (2013), in Psychological Bulletin: of 65 Comprehensive System variables, 30 had good or excellent support, the best being cognitive and perceptual variables (thought disorder, reality testing); this shaped R-PAS. Hiller and colleagues (1999): Rorschach and MMPI validity about equal (.29 and .30). The best-validated sentence completion test is Loevinger's WUSCT, not the Rotter ISB.

Implicit motives: the strongest case for picture stories

The best evidence for picture-story methods began as an apparent failure. If TAT stories measure the need for achievement, they ought to agree with questionnaires that ask people directly how much they care about achievement. They hardly agree at all: when William D. Spangler later pooled 36 such correlations, the average was about .09, statistically different from zero but tiny. For decades the weak agreement looked like proof that one of the two measures was invalid, and most psychologists assumed that the invalid one was the TAT.

In 1989 David McClelland, Richard Koestner and Joel Weinberger turned the finding on its head in a Psychological Review paper titled "How do self-attributed and implicit motives differ?". Their answer was that the two measures tap two different motivational systems, and that psychologists should stop giving one name to two measures that do not correlate. An implicit motive, the kind coded from stories, is a learned, emotionally based readiness to enjoy a particular kind of activity, such as doing something well for its own sake; it responds to incentives built into the activity itself and predicts what a person does spontaneously and repeatedly over long periods. A self-attributed motive, the kind a questionnaire measures, is part of a person's conscious picture of himself and his values; it responds to social incentives, such as being asked, watched or evaluated, and predicts the choices a person makes when a situation explicitly poses the question. McClelland's group called the first kind of behaviour operant (initiated by the person) and the second respondent (given in answer to a structured situation); the words echo Skinner's but mean something different here. On this view, a near-zero correlation between the two measures is exactly what the theory predicts.

The prediction can be tested, and Spangler tested it. His 1992 Psychological Bulletin paper reported two meta-analyses covering 105 studies of achievement motive scores and behavioural outcomes. Picture-story measures correlated on average about .22 with operant outcomes such as occupational success and income and about .19 with respondent outcomes such as school performance; questionnaire measures correlated about .13 and .15. Both picture-story figures were slightly but significantly the larger. As the theory predicted, the picture-story scores predicted best when the incentives lay in the task itself; the matching prediction for questionnaires, that they would do best when the incentives were social, was not confirmed. This is the strongest case any projective method has made: when a picture-story technique is scored with a formal, research-based system for a specific motive, it measures something real that questionnaires miss. Two cautions keep the case honest. The correlations with real-world achievement are modest, as the critics stress; and the case applies to formally scored motive imagery, not to the intuitive reading of TAT stories that most clinicians practise.

The same pattern runs through Mihura's Rorschach data, where scores related to observers' ratings and diagnoses (r = .27) far better than to the person's own self-report (r = .08). Defenders draw a general moral: these techniques record what a person does with an ambiguous task (how she organises a blot, what she makes characters want) rather than what she says about herself, so they should be validated against behaviour, not against questionnaires. For this reason Gregory Meyer and John Kurtz proposed in 2006 that the labels "objective" and "projective" be retired, and that inkblot and story methods be called performance-based tests, a term R-PAS has adopted. The critics' reply is fair too: a low correlation with self-report is also what an invalid measure would show, so the argument succeeds only where the score is shown to predict behaviour that matters.

Exam trap

A TAT score that does not correlate with a questionnaire on the "same" motive is not automatically invalid: McClelland, Koestner and Weinberger (1989) distinguished implicit motives (stories; spontaneous, long-term behaviour) from self-attributed motives (questionnaires; choices in structured situations). Spangler (1992) found picture-story achievement scores predicted outcomes, on average better than questionnaires. This supports formally scored motive imagery, not intuitive TAT interpretation.

Incremental validity: does the test add anything?

A score can be valid and still be useless. If a psychologist already has a careful interview, the history and an inventory, the practical question is not whether a Rorschach index correlates with a criterion but whether adding it makes the final judgement more accurate. That gain is incremental validity (Chapter 4 teaches it as part of validity). It matters doubly for projective techniques, because they are expensive: a Rorschach takes far longer to give, code and interpret than a questionnaire takes to score. Ever since Paul Meehl's Clinical versus Statistical Prediction (1954), psychologists have known that more information does not automatically mean better judgement, and incremental validity is how the question is put to the test.

Remember

Incremental validity (hit-rate form) = hit rate with A and B − hit rate with A alone

Incremental validity (variance form) = ΔR2 = R2A + B − R2A

A is the information already in hand (interview, inventory), B is the added test, and a hit rate is the percentage of correct decisions. R2 is the squared multiple correlation, read as the proportion of the differences in the criterion that the predictors account for (multiple regression is taught in the Research Methodology book); Δ ("delta") means "the change in".

Garb and his colleagues describe a study that shows the hit-rate form at work. Psychologists and advanced graduate students made three kinds of two-way diagnostic decision (back-pain patients against psychiatric inpatients with schizophrenia or bipolar disorder; depressed against non-depressed back-pain patients; bipolar disorder against schizophrenia), using the MMPI alone, the Comprehensive System Rorschach alone, or both together. Pooled across tasks, the hit rates were 76 percent with the MMPI alone, 58 percent with the Rorschach alone and 74 percent with both. Work through it. Each decision had two possible answers, so guessing would score 50 percent: the Rorschach alone beat chance by 58 − 50 = 8 points, the MMPI by 76 − 50 = 26 points. The incremental validity of the Rorschach over the MMPI is 74 − 76 = −2 percentage points: adding it made judges slightly less accurate. A clinic making 500 such decisions a year would, at that rate, make about 0.02 × 500 = 10 more errors by adding the test. One study does not settle a question, and the difference was small, but Garb reported that in judgement research psychologists have frequently become less accurate when projective information was added to other information, even if the decreases were not always statistically significant.

Now the variance form, with invented numbers. Suppose that in a clinic study an interview rating and an inventory together predict therapists' later ratings of a client's interpersonal difficulties with a multiple correlation R = 0.40, so R2 = 0.40 × 0.40 = 0.16: 16 percent of the differences between clients are accounted for. Adding a formally scored TAT object-relations score raises R to 0.43, so R2 = 0.43 × 0.43 = 0.1849. Then ΔR2 = 0.1849 − 0.16 = 0.0249, about 0.025. In plain words, the TAT adds about two and a half percent to what was already explained. Whether that is worth an extra hour of a client's and a clinician's time depends on the stakes of the decision; what the arithmetic forbids is the common assumption that because a score is valid on its own, it must add something to a battery.

The reviews give a consistent picture. Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb found that, with a few exceptions, projective indexes had not consistently shown incremental validity over other psychometric data, and Garb's 2003 review of incremental validity in adult assessment concluded that the results tend to favour interviews, personality inventories such as the MMPI-2 and brief self-rated measures, and are generally less encouraging for projective techniques. Even the well-supported Rorschach Oral Dependency scale has not been shown to add validity to interview and history information. Defenders of the "battery" reply that a projective result is never used alone, only to confirm or question what other sources suggest. Garb's answer is the most important practical point in this section. If an invalid score happens to agree with the interview, it makes the clinician more confident without making him more accurate; if it disagrees, it may persuade him to abandon a correct hypothesis. An unsupported score is not a harmless extra.

Exam trap

Validity asks whether a score relates to a criterion; incremental validity asks whether it improves prediction beyond information already available (interview, history, inventory). A test can be valid and add nothing. In the study Garb cites, hit rates were 76 percent (MMPI), 58 percent (Rorschach) and 74 percent (both): an incremental validity of −2 points. "It is only used in a battery" does not make an invalid score safe.

Courts, ethics and culture

Three further questions decide how projective techniques may be used. The first is ethical and is covered in Book 2, Section 1.9.6: test security. A technique whose stimuli and common answers circulate freely can no longer do its job, and the protest when the ten Rorschach plates and their most common responses were posted on Wikipedia in 2009 was a protest about exactly that. It is why every blot, card and frame in this chapter is invented, and why the colour composition of the cards and the blankness of TAT card 16 are as much as a textbook should say about the real stimuli.

The second question is legal, because the stakes are highest in court: custody of a child, criminal responsibility, compensation, an allegation of abuse. In the United States the Supreme Court's decision in Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals (1993) made trial judges the gatekeepers of scientific testimony, asking among other things whether a method has been tested, whether it has been peer reviewed, what its known error rate is and whether it is generally accepted. In 1999 William M. Grove and R. Christopher Barden argued that Comprehensive System testimony could not meet such standards and should not be admitted; Rorschach researchers disputed this, and the argument has shaped forensic practice ever since. Garb, Wood, Lilienfeld and Nezworski (2002) drew the practical lines. Standards should be strictest in court, stricter in formal evaluation than in therapy; an expert witness should expect to be challenged who relies on the old Comprehensive System norms, who interprets scores that are not valid for the purpose, or who uses the Rorschach, the TAT or figure drawings to help decide whether a child has been abused; and, flatly, those techniques should not be used to detect child physical or sexual abuse, because, apart from one promising but unnormed TAT scale, no indicator has been shown consistently to separate abused children from other children receiving mental health care. In India, a psychologist's evidence enters court as expert opinion under Section 39 of the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam, 2023 (formerly Section 45 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872), which the court weighs but is not bound to accept; the scientific question of what a score can bear is the same.

Clinical link

A family court asks a psychologist whether a six-year-old girl has been sexually abused. Her drawing of a person includes a carefully drawn mouth and heavily shaded lower body, and her CAT stories are full of a frightening "uncle" animal. It is tempting to treat these as evidence. They are not. Drawing features of this kind have not been shown to separate abused children from children who are anxious, disturbed or simply imaginative for other reasons, and stories can echo something a child has seen on television or overheard. A false "yes" can destroy a family and a false "no" can return a child to danger. The defensible report describes what the child drew and said, states plainly that no projective indicator can establish abuse, and recommends a forensic interview by a trained specialist and a review of the other evidence.

The third question is cultural. Popular responses, the content of stories and even what counts as an ordinary percept depend on what people in a culture commonly see, read and talk about. The international reference samples of 2007 found adult Rorschach records broadly similar across countries, which is reassuring, but child records varied so much that the authors discouraged normative interpretation for children. Picture-story tests face the problem more sharply, because their pictures show people, clothes and settings; that is why Uma Chowdhury produced an Indian modification of the TAT in 1960 and an Indian CAT (Sections 10.6 and 10.7), and why Udai Pareek adapted the Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Section 10.8). Adapted pictures are a new test, not a translation, and need their own reliability and validity evidence. Before using any normative comparison with an Indian client, ask where the norms came from: many manuals report norms gathered in the United States or Europe. Garb and colleagues suggest one partial remedy, local norms collected in one's own clinic, while noting its limit: a clinic's norms describe its patients, not the healthy people in the community.

Their place today: a balanced verdict

Given all this, you might expect projective techniques to have vanished from practice. They have not. In 1995 C. Edward Watkins and his colleagues surveyed 412 American clinical psychologists. Projective techniques took five of the top ten places among the procedures they used, 82 percent reported giving the Rorschach at least occasionally, and assessment as practised looked very much as it had 30 or more years earlier. Barlow and Durand's abnormal psychology textbook still reports that a majority of clinicians give projective tests at least occasionally and that most doctoral programmes train students in them. Training, however, is shrinking where research is strongest: a 2014 survey of American clinical psychology programmes by Rebecca E. Ready and Heather Barnett Veague found the Rorschach taught most often in practitioner-scholar programmes, less often in scientist-practitioner programmes and least often in clinical-science programmes. Practice changes slowly; the research-led end of the profession has moved fastest.

Indian context

In India the techniques remain part of professional training. The Rehabilitation Council of India's M.Phil Clinical Psychology syllabus (effective from 2017 to 2018) lists Rorschach psychodiagnostics among the "tests for diagnostic clarification"; sentence completion, the picture-frustration test, the Draw-a-Person test and the TAT ("Murray's and Uma Chowdhary's") among the tests of adjustment and personality; and Raven's Controlled Projection Test, the Draw-a-Person test and the Children's Apperception Test for children. Its core tests, in which trainees must be certified competent before the Part I examinations, include the sentence completion test, the TAT, the CAT and the Rorschach, and its essential references include Exner's The Rorschach: A Comprehensive System (fourth edition, which the syllabus dates 2002; it is usually cited as 2003) and Chowdhury's An Indian Modification of the Thematic Apperception Test (1960; the syllabus spells her name Choudhary). The RCI's two-year M.A. (Clinical Psychology), which replaces the M.Phil from the 2025 to 2026 session, keeps the same projective tests, both in its syllabus and in its list of core tests. Interviewers for these programmes therefore expect you to know both how the techniques work and what the evidence says about them.

What, then, is the verdict? It is not "projective tests are worthless", and it is not "projective tests reveal the hidden person". It is closer to this. A handful of carefully scored indices measure real things: the perceptual accuracy and thought organisation captured by Rorschach form quality and the thought-disorder scores, dependency on the Oral Dependency scale, implicit motives in formally scored picture stories, and ego development on the Washington University test. These are most useful exactly where self-report fails, as when a guarded patient denies unusual experiences but cannot stop his perception from drifting away from the blot. Most of the rest, the single signs and the global intuitive readings, are best treated as material for conversation and hypotheses to check. Garb and his colleagues put the practical conclusion simply: psychologists are on safer ground using projective techniques as an aid to exploration in psychotherapy than as an assessment device. The defenders would add that a good clinician uses them precisely as a standardised behaviour sample, a structured interview in which the person cannot easily steer the conversation.

Remember: four rules for using a projective technique

(1) Use only scores with replicated research support for the specific purpose, scored by a formal system, and compare them with appropriate, current norms. (2) Treat every other observation as a hypothesis to be checked against the history, the interview, observation and objective tests. (3) Never base a diagnosis, a legal conclusion or a decision about abuse on a projective result alone. (4) Report what the person actually did and said, specifically enough that the sentence would not fit the next client (no Barnum statements).

Exam trap

The keyed main advantage of projective tests (RML 2025) is that they avoid self-report biases such as social desirability and faking, because the purpose is disguised. The tempting wrong options are not advantages: "superior retest reliability" and "numerical quantitative data" are strengths of objective inventories, and "they let people use the defence of projection" twists the projective hypothesis (Section 10.1). Know the evidence behind the keyed answer too: the disguise makes naive faking harder, but informed people can fake disturbance (Albert, Fox and Kahn, 1980).

Every test in one table

You have now met some thirty techniques, from Galton's word list to the Hand Test, and the exam questions on them almost always turn on the same few facts: who made it and when, what the person is shown, which of Lindzey's families it belongs to (Section 10.2), what is scored and the one detail that separates it from its neighbours. Table 10.34 lays them side by side in the order of Lindzey's families. Read across a row to revise a test; read down a column to see how the families differ, which is the fastest way to answer "which of these is a completion technique?" in an examination hall.

Remember

Lindzey's families in one line each, with an anchor test: association, say the first thing the stimulus calls up (Rorschach); construction, make up a story (TAT); completion, finish what is unfinished (Rotter ISB, Rosenzweig P-F); choice or ordering, pick or arrange (Szondi, Tomkins-Horn); expression, make something freely, where the manner matters (Draw-a-Person, House-Tree-Person). And the evidence in one line: structured scoring makes projective scores reliable, but only a few scores are valid, and fewer still add anything to an interview and an inventory.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The tendency of clinicians and students to "see" a relationship between a drawing sign and a symptom that is absent from the data was named by Loren and Jean Chapman, who showed it first with Draw-a-Person signs in 1967 and then with Rorschach signs in 1969.
  2. In the Chapmans' 1967 survey, 91 percent of clinicians linked atypical eyes in a drawing with .
  3. Shaffer, Erdberg and Haroian (1999) found that healthy adults looked disturbed when their Rorschach scores were compared with the norms, a problem critics described by saying the norms overpathologise normal people.
  4. Six TAT cards are scored for achievement imagery, and the scores on any two cards correlate 0.20 on average. Their standardised alpha is .
  5. Atkinson argued that alpha understates the TAT because writing an achievement story briefly satisfies the motive, so successive stories rise and fall in the effect.
  6. In Mihura and colleagues' 2013 meta-analyses, the best-supported Comprehensive System variables were those assessing and perceptual processes, such as the Perceptual-Thinking Index; validity was far higher against external criteria (r = .27) than against (r = .08).
  7. McClelland, Koestner and Weinberger (1989) argued that stories measure motives and questionnaires measure self-attributed motives, so the two need not correlate.
  8. Diagnoses made with an interview alone are 70 percent correct; with the interview plus a projective drawing they are 71 percent correct. The incremental validity of the drawing is .
  9. The sentence completion test that reviewers called arguably the best-validated projective technique is the , which measures ego development.
  10. The tendency to accept vague, generally true statements in a report as an accurate personal description is the effect, named by Paul Meehl in 1956.

Answers: (1) illusory correlation; (2) suspiciousness; (3) Comprehensive System (Exner's); (4) 0.60 [6 × 0.20 = 1.20; 1 + (5 × 0.20) = 2.00; 1.20 / 2.00 = 0.60]; (5) saw-tooth; (6) cognitive; introspectively assessed (self-report) criteria; (7) implicit; (8) 1 percentage point [71 − 70 = 1], a negligible gain; (9) Washington University Sentence Completion Test (Loevinger); (10) Barnum.

The case of the Nuremberg Rorschachs, revisited

Go back to the cells at Nuremberg with what this chapter has taught. Kelley and Gilbert handed the defendants the same ten cards with the same question, "What might this be?", and the projective hypothesis of Section 10.1 explains their hope: faced with an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus, a person must supply the structure himself, so the structure in the response is taken to come from him. But the hypothesis makes three claims, and only the first two are about the person who answers. The third, that an examiner can read the process backwards from the response to the person, is a claim about the reader, and Nuremberg put it to the test. In 1945 there was no single way of coding and reading a record. Kelley had written the clinical contributions to Klopfer's manual, and the rival American schools of Section 10.5.5 defined their codes differently, so two examiners could reach opposite conclusions from the same kind of record, as Section 10.1.5 warned. The codes of Section 10.4 and the Structural Summary of Section 10.5, which exist to make a record mean the same thing whoever reads it, came decades later. And Gilbert's Wechsler-Bellevue scores, all above 100, with Schacht at 143 and Göring at 138, measured ability, the maximum performance of a test with right answers; a Rorschach yields no IQ, and no Rorschach score was ever shown to detect a "Nazi personality".

Miale and Selzer read records that they knew to be those of the Nazi leaders, and found deeply disturbed men. Section 10.10.3 names the danger in that situation: an illusory correlation is a relationship that the observer sees but the data do not contain, and it survives because it matches what people expect, which is why the only protection is data scored by people who do not know the answer in advance. Molly Harrower's study of 1976 was exactly that protection: she removed every name, mixed eight Nazi records with eight records from other people, among them clergymen and patients, matched for overall mental health, and sent them to ten experts, who did not pick out the Nazis. Zillmer's reanalysis of 1989, with Exner's system, found no single mental disorder that fitted the Nazi leaders. That is the balanced verdict of Sections 10.10.4 and 10.10.8 in a single case. A formally scored record, read blind and against proper norms, can say something real about how a person perceives and thinks, since form quality and the thought-disorder scores are the best-supported Rorschach variables; it could not find a Nazi personality, and no diagnosis or legal conclusion should rest on it alone. A response may reveal the person who gave it; at Nuremberg it also revealed the expectations of the people who read it.

Chapter summary

10.1 What Makes a Technique Projective: The Projective Hypothesis. Self-report inventories, objective because a fixed key scores them, are open to social desirability, faking good and faking bad, defensiveness and lack of insight. Projective techniques, named by Lawrence K. Frank in 1939, take the other road. The projective hypothesis holds that a person facing an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus must supply its structure, so the response reveals needs, conflicts, defences and perceptual style; it rests on Freud's broader sense of projection (Totem and Taboo, 1913), which Leopold Bellak renamed apperceptive distortion, not on the defence. Every technique has an ambiguous stimulus, an open response, a disguised purpose and global interpretation. The techniques claim to reveal unconscious processes, not intellectual attainment, parenting style or career aptitude; the Rorschach and the TAT are projective, the 16PF, MMPI-2, NEO-PI-R, EPQ, MBTI and MCMI-III objective. Their main claimed advantage is avoiding self-report biases; their price is paid in scoring, norms and retest reliability.

10.2 Families of Projective Techniques: Lindzey's Classification and Others. Gardner Lindzey (1959) classified projective techniques by the response they ask for, not the stimulus. Association asks for the first word, image or percept (the Rorschach, the Holtzman, word association); construction for an elaborate product, usually a story (the TAT and its relatives); completion for finishing a stem, story or cartoon (sentence completion, the Rosenzweig P-F Study); choice or ordering for picking or arranging alternatives (the Szondi, the Tomkins-Horn); and expression for a free production whose manner is studied (Draw-a-Person, House-Tree-Person, Kinetic Family Drawing, play, handwriting). Construction and expression are told apart by what the examiner reads. Frank's earlier scheme (constitutive, constructive, interpretive, cathartic, refractive) was defined by unobservable processes and was never widely adopted; Indian texts add pictorial, verbal and expressive groups. Word association is a founding member of the association family, so it is projective and the RML 2021 key is wrong.

10.3 The Rorschach I: Hermann Rorschach, the Ten Cards and How the Test Is Given. Inkblots were old (Kerner's klecksography, Binet and Henri in 1895, Hens in 1917); Hermann Rorschach (1884 to 1922), a Swiss psychiatrist trained under Eugen Bleuler, asked how people see rather than what. His form interpretation experiment appeared as Psychodiagnostik in 1921, and he died the next year at 37. The test assesses perception and reality testing, thought processes and thought disorder, emotional functioning, relationships and coping style, aids diagnosis and, in the projective tradition, reveals unconscious intentions; it yields no IQ. Its ten symmetrical cards are five achromatic (I, IV, V, VI, VII), two black and red (II, III) and three multicoloured (VIII to X). The response phase asks "What might this be?" and records every word; the inquiry clarifies location and determinants without leading questions; testing the limits is optional and comes last. Colour shock and card rejections are observations, not scores.

10.4 The Rorschach II: Scoring Location, Determinants, Form Quality and Content. Every response is coded before anything is interpreted, here in the Comprehensive System of John E. Exner Jr. Location is W (whole), D (common detail), Dd (unusual detail) or S (white space, always attached to another code); the older confabulated whole, DW, stretched one detail over the whole blot. Developmental quality (synthesised, ordinary, vague-synthesised, vague) grades organisation, using the idea of form demand. Determinants record what shaped the percept: F (form alone); M, FM and m (human, animal and inanimate movement); FC, CF, C and Cn (form-colour, colour-form, pure colour and colour naming); C' for achromatic colour; texture, vista and diffuse shading; form dimension, pairs, reflections and blends. Form quality (+, o, u, minus) is the main index of reality testing, summarised in X+%, XA% and X−%. Content codes, popular responses, special scores for cognitive slippage and Beck's Z score complete the line. It gives no IQ.

10.5 The Rorschach III: Reading the Record, from Klopfer and Beck to Exner and R-PAS. Codes go into the sequence of scores and the Structural Summary, read as a configuration against norms. Many synthesised W responses show organisational and integrative effort; form quality shows reality testing, so confabulatory responses mean impaired reality testing; absent human content means poor interpersonal orientation. Rorschach's Erlebnistypus became Exner's Experience Balance, M : WSumC (FC, CF and C weighted 0.5, 1.0 and 1.5), giving introversive, extratensive or ambitent styles unless Lambda, F ÷ (R − F), is high. The five American schools were Samuel J. Beck's, Bruno Klopfer's, Marguerite Hertz's, Zygmunt Piotrowski's and David Rapaport and Roy Schafer's; there is no Murray system. Exner's Comprehensive System (1974) united them and added constellations such as the PTI. R-PAS (Gregory Meyer and colleagues, 2011) steers R and uses international norms; the Holtzman (1961) has two forms of 45 blots, one response each.

10.6 The Thematic Apperception Test. Christiana D. Morgan and Henry A. Murray of the Harvard Psychological Clinic published the TAT in 1935; the final set and Murray's manual followed in 1943. It rests on Murray's need-press theory and on the assumption that people project their needs and conflicts onto ambiguous pictures of social scenes. The set has 31 cards (30 pictures and blank card 16); Murray gave 20 in two sessions of ten, while clinicians now choose about 5 to 12. Stories are read through the hero, his needs, the press (alpha real, beta perceived), the thema and the outcome; Leopold Bellak's ten variables give an ego-psychological reading; David McClelland and John Atkinson scored achievement imagery. The TAT uses social scenes and asks for stories, the Rorschach inkblots and percepts; it reveals unconscious motives, not IQ, and suits questions about close relationships. Uma Chowdhury adapted it for India in 1960.

10.7 Apperception Tests for Children and Other Groups: The CAT and Its Relatives. Young children cannot easily tell stories about adult predicaments, so Leopold and Sonya Bellak published the Children's Apperception Test in 1949: 10 cards of animals in human situations for children of about 3 to 10, on Ernst Kris's suggestion that children identify more readily with animals. The CAT-S (1952) added supplementary cards, and the CAT-H (1965) redrew the scenes with human figures after research failed to confirm the animal advantage reliably; Uma Chowdhury published an Indian CAT in 1960. Older adults have the Wolks' Gerontological Apperception Test (1971) and the Bellaks' Senior Apperception Technique (1973). The Roberts Apperception Test for Children (1982) added normed scales, while the Thompson TAT and the TEMAS adapted the method to culture. Gerald Blum's Blacky Pictures used a dog family to test Freud's psychosexual stages, and Edwin Shneidman's MAPS (1947) let the person build the scene. All are construction techniques.

10.8 Completion and Verbal Techniques: Word Association, Sentence Completion and the Picture-Frustration Study. Francis Galton's experiment on 75 words (1879) is usually called the first projective technique. Carl Jung, with Franz Riklin from 1904, made word association clinical: 100 words, reaction times judged against the person's own probable mean, and complex indicators. Grace Kent and Aaron Rosanoff (1910) gave 100 neutral words norms from 1,000 people. Sentence completion descends from Hermann Ebbinghaus's completion method; the Rotter Incomplete Sentences Blank (Julian Rotter and Janet Rafferty, 1950) weights 40 completions from 0 to 6, a higher total meaning more maladjustment, the Sacks test maps 60 stems in 15 areas, and Jane Loevinger's 36-stem test measures ego development. Saul Rosenzweig's Picture-Frustration Study (1945) scores 24 cartoon frames for direction (extrapunitive, intropunitive, impunitive) and type (obstacle-dominance, ego-defence, need-persistence), with a Group Conformity Rating; Udai Pareek adapted it for India.

10.9 Expressive and Choice Techniques: Drawings, Play, the Szondi and the Hand Test. Florence Goodenough's Draw-a-Man (1926) measured intelligence; Karen Machover's Draw-a-Person (1949) read personality through the body-image hypothesis, with signs that later research did not support. John N. Buck's House-Tree-Person (1948) has an achromatic then a chromatic phase, each followed by the post-drawing interrogation. Robert Burns and S. Harvard Kaufman's Kinetic Family Drawing (1970) shows the family doing something and is read for actions, styles and symbols. Elizabeth Koppitz listed about 30 emotional indicators (1968), and the DAP:SPED (1991) added norms. Play, Margaret Lowenfeld's World Technique and Mosaic Test, finger painting and graphology complete the expressive family. In the choice family, Leopold Szondi's test (1947) asks for liked and disliked faces among 48 photographs of psychiatric patients, and the Tomkins-Horn asks for an order. Edwin E. Wagner's Hand Test (1962) asks what a pictured hand might be doing.

10.10 How Good Are Projective Techniques? Reliability, Validity and Their Place Today. Projective techniques meet testing standards unevenly: the TAT and drawings lack standard scoring, and the Comprehensive System norms made healthy adults look disturbed until international norms arrived (2007). Formal coding gives good scorer agreement, but story-based motive scores have low internal consistency, though Atkinson's saw-tooth argument holds that alpha is the wrong yardstick for stories. Loren and Jean Chapman (1967, 1969) showed how illusory correlation keeps invalid signs alive, and the Barnum effect makes vague reports feel accurate. Lilienfeld, Wood and Garb (2000) found a few Rorschach and TAT indexes supported and most not, drawings least; Mihura and colleagues (2013) found the cognitive and perceptual variables best supported. Formally scored implicit motives predict long-term behaviour. Incremental validity is usually small, the techniques must not be used to detect abuse, and they remain in Indian training. The verdict: a few formally scored indices are valid; the rest are hypotheses to check.
