From the past papers

  1. Which of these projective tests uses the perception of inkblots to assess respondent's personality types? (RML 2020)

    (a) Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (b) Thematic Apperception Test (c) Myers-Briggs Type Indicator Test (d) Rorschach Test

    Answer: (d) Rorschach Test. Hermann Rorschach's ten cards (1921) are the only inkblots among the options (Section 10.3.3). The MMPI is a true-false self-report inventory and the MBTI a forced-choice questionnaire that sorts people into Jungian "types", so neither uses a picture of any kind; the word "types" in the stem is bait for the MBTI. The TAT uses pictures of people in ambiguous scenes, not inkblots.

  2. Projective tests claim to reveal information about: (RML 2020)

    (a) intellectual attainment (b) unconscious processes (c) parenting style (d) career aptitude

    Answer: (b) unconscious processes. The central claim, which follows from the projective hypothesis, is that the response to an ambiguous stimulus reveals needs, wishes, fears and conflicts of which the person is unaware (Section 10.1.6). Intellectual attainment is measured by achievement tests, parenting style by questionnaires and by observing families, and career aptitude by aptitude batteries and interest inventories.

  3. What is Rorschach's projective test designed to measure? (RML 2021)

    (a) Unconscious intentions. (b) Dreams (c) Conscious desires. (d) Brain size

    Answer: (a) Unconscious intentions. In the projective tradition the Rorschach gives a person room to reveal unconscious needs, conflicts and intentions that he would not or could not report directly (Section 10.3.3). It is not a way of analysing dreams; conscious desires are what an inventory asks about; and it is not a test of brain structure.

  4. Which is not a projective test (RML 2021)

    (a) Word association test (b) Rorschach's ink lot test (c) Thematic apperception test (d) Sentence completion test

    Answer: keyed (a) Word association test, but the key is wrong: by the standard classification all four are projective. Word association is a founding member of Lindzey's association family, named beside the Rorschach, and Galton's word association of 1879 is usually called the first projective technique; Jung made it clinical with complex indicators (Section 10.2.5). The Rorschach is an association technique, the TAT a construction technique and sentence completion a completion technique. Learn word association as projective; mark (a) only if this exact item reappears, because that is the paper's known key. If an option names a genuine objective inventory (MMPI-2, 16PF, NEO-PI-R) or the Controlled Oral Word Association Test, a fluency test, that option is the answer.

  5. How many Rorschach cards are coloured? (RML 2022)

    (a) 10 (b) 5 (c) 3 (d) 4

    Answer: (b) 5. Two cards (II and III) are black and grey with red, and three (VIII, IX and X) are fully multicoloured, so five carry colour; the other five (I, IV, V, VI and VII) are achromatic (Section 10.3.4). "3" counts only the multicoloured cards, and "10" and "4" are simply wrong.

  6. Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) is based on which technique of psychological testing? (RML 2022)

    (a) Expressive technique (b) Construction technique (c) Association technique (d) Completion technique

    Answer: (b) Construction technique. In Gardner Lindzey's classification (1959; the families are his, not Frank's) the TAT is the model construction technique: the person builds a new and elaborate product, a story with a past, a present and an outcome (Section 10.2.2). Association asks for the first word or percept (the Rorschach), completion for finishing a supplied stem or frame (sentence completion, the P-F Study), and expression for a free production whose manner is studied (drawings, play).

  7. Who developed the House-tree-person test? (RML 2022)

    (a) John Buck (b) Aaron T.Beck (c) David D. Burns (d) Karen Machover

    Answer: (a) John Buck. John N. Buck set out the House-Tree-Person in 1948: an achromatic (pencil) phase and then a chromatic (crayon) phase, each followed by the post-drawing interrogation (Section 10.9.2). Aaron T. Beck founded cognitive therapy and wrote the Beck Depression Inventory, David D. Burns wrote the self-help book Feeling Good, and Karen Machover devised the Draw-a-Person (1949).

  8. The following statements about the Rorschach inkblot test are correct EXCEPT: (RML 2022)

    (a) It was introduced in 1921 by Swiss psychiatrist Hermann Rorschach. (b) It is a projective method of psychological testing. (c) Its function is to assess cognition and personality of the person and to diagnosis certain psychological conditions. (d) It employs 12 inkblots in which a person is asked to describe what he or she sees in them.

    Answer: (d). The test has ten cards, not 12 (Section 10.3.4). The other three statements are true: Psychodiagnostik appeared in 1921, the test is the classic projective method, and it is used to assess cognition and personality and to help diagnose psychological conditions, especially psychosis (Section 10.3.3).

  9. Which of the following is an association technique of psychological testing? (RML 2022)

    (a) Sentence completion test (b) Rorschach inkblot test (c) Thematic apperception test (d) Draw-a-person test

    Answer: (b) Rorschach inkblot test. Association techniques ask for the first word, image or percept a stimulus calls up, and the Rorschach is the founding member of the family (Section 10.2.2). Sentence completion is a completion technique, the TAT a construction technique and the Draw-a-Person an expression technique.

  10. Which testing method uses ambiguous stimuli to uncover unconscious thoughts? (RML 2025)

    (a) Projective tests (b) Personality inventories (c) Standardized intelligence tests (d) Observation (direct) tests

    Answer: (a) Projective tests. This is the projective hypothesis in one line: an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus makes the person supply the structure, revealing what he could not or would not say (Section 10.1.3). Personality inventories use clear items with fixed answers and a key, intelligence tests present structured problems with correct answers, and direct observation records behaviour itself with no test stimulus at all (Section 10.1.6).

  11. What types of cards are included in the Rorschach test? (RML 2025)

    (a) Only black and white cards (b) Only colored cards (c) Five black and white and five multi-colored cards (d) Ten multi-colored cards

    Answer: (c), the keyed option, but its wording is loose. The five-five split is right and (c) is the only option with it; "only black and white", "only coloured" and "ten multicoloured" are all false. Strictly, the five achromatic cards (I, IV, V, VI, VII) are black and grey, and of the five chromatic cards only three (VIII, IX, X) are fully multicoloured, while two (II, III) are black and red (Section 10.3.4). Choose (c), and know the exact composition: 5 achromatic, 2 black and red, 3 multicoloured.

  12. What is the main purpose of the Rorschach inkblot test? (RML 2025)

    (a) To diagnose physical illness (b) To measure thought disorder and identify mental illness through perception of inkblots (c) To assess intelligence quotient (d) To evaluate memory recall

    Answer: (b). Rorschach's own clinical interest centred on schizophrenia, and the test is still used as one source of evidence on thought processes, thought disorder and psychopathology, especially psychosis (Section 10.3.3); form quality and the thought-disorder scores are its best-supported variables (Section 10.10.4). It is not a test of physical illness or of memory, and no system turns a Rorschach record into an IQ.

  13. What is the Enquiry Phase in the Rorschach test? (RML 2025)

    (a) Phase where the cards are randomly shuffled (b) Phase involving detailed discussion and clarification of each response without leading answers (c) Initial exposure to inkblots without recording responses (d) Phase that involves scoring the responses only

    Answer: (b). After all ten cards the examiner reads each response back and asks, in essence, only "Where is it?" and "What makes it look like that?", following the person's own key words without leading questions, to establish location and determinants (Section 10.3.6). The cards are always shown in the fixed order I to X, every word is recorded verbatim from Card I onwards, and coding is done afterwards, away from the client.

  14. Who developed the Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) and in which decade? (RML 2025)

    (a) Sigmund Freud in the 1920s (b) Henry A. Murray and Christina D. Morgan in the 1930s (c) Carl Jung in the 1940s (d) B.F. Skinner in the 1950s

    Answer: (b). Christiana D. Morgan (the paper's "Christina" is a misspelling) and Henry A. Murray of the Harvard Psychological Clinic published the TAT in 1935, with the final set and Murray's manual in 1943 (Section 10.6.1). Freud devised no picture test, Jung's test was word association (1904 to 1910), and Skinner was studying operant conditioning.

  15. How many picture cards are usually used in a typical TAT assessment session? (RML 2025)

    (a) 1 to 3 cards (b) 5 to 12 cards (c) 20 to 31 cards (d) Over 50 cards

    Answer: (b) 5 to 12 cards. The full set has 31 cards (30 pictures and the blank card 16), and Murray gave one person 20 in two sessions of ten, but clinicians today select about 5 to 12 cards for a single session, 8 to 12 being the figure most often quoted (Section 10.6.2). One to three is too few for a theme to recur, 20 to 31 describes the whole kit, never one sitting, and no version has over 50.

  16. What is the main purpose of analyzing the stories a participant tells during the TAT? (RML 2025)

    (a) To measure IQ (b) To reveal unconscious thoughts, conflicts, and motivations (c) To assess vocabulary skills (d) To test memory retention

    Answer: (b). The stories are read through the hero, his needs, the press, the thema and the outcome for needs and conflicts the person may not be able or willing to report (Sections 10.6.3 and 10.6.6). Murray's instruction calls the test "a test of imagination, one form of intelligence" only as a disguise; a long, fluent story is not scored as IQ or vocabulary, and the examiner does not test memory for the pictures.

  17. Which is a key difference between the TAT and the Rorschach Inkblot Test? (RML 2025)

    (a) TAT uses ambiguous social scenes with characters; Rorschach uses symmetrical inkblots (b) TAT measures IQ; Rorschach measures personality (c) TAT requires yes/no answers; Rorschach requires storytelling (d) Both tests are identical in method and purpose

    Answer: (a). The TAT shows recognisable people in ambiguous situations and asks for stories; the Rorschach shows symmetrical inkblots and asks for percepts (Section 10.6.6). Both are personality techniques, neither asks for yes or no answers (those belong to inventories), option (c) also puts the storytelling in the wrong test, and the two differ at every step of method and purpose.

  18. What is the main advantage of projective tests, like the Rorschach inkblot test? (RML 2025)

    (a) They allow people to use defence mechanisms of projection (b) They avoid self-report biases (c) They have superior re-test reliability (d) They provide numerical quantitative data

    Answer: (b) They avoid self-report biases. Because the purpose is disguised and there are no right answers, social desirability, faking and defensiveness have less to work with (Section 10.1.7). Option (a) twists the theory: the test is meant to see past defences, and the projection it relies on is everyday perception, not a defence handed to the client. Superior retest reliability and numerical data are strengths of objective inventories; projective retest figures are modest (Section 10.10.2). Remember too that informed people can still fake (Albert, Fox and Kahn, 1980).

  19. Confabulatory responses on Rorschach Psychodiagnostics are indicative of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Impairment in reality testing (b) Extreme lack of empathy (c) Regressed ego (d) Cognitive immaturity

    Answer: (a) Impairment in reality testing. A confabulated whole response (DW) seizes on one detail and stretches the percept over the whole blot without checking the fit, so the conclusion is no longer tested against the stimulus; it almost always carries poor form quality, and the later Comprehensive System codes it W with minus form quality and the special score CONFAB (Sections 10.4.2 and 10.5.2). Empathy is read, if at all, from human movement and human content; "regressed ego" is a psychoanalytic reading of a whole record, not of this one sign; cognitive immaturity shows in vague (DQ v) responses.

  20. Absence of 'Human' Response on Rorschach Psychodiagnostics Test indicates: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Apathy (b) Poor interpersonal orientation (c) Lack of empathy (d) All the above

    Answer: (b) Poor interpersonal orientation. Human contents, above all whole, real human figures (pure H), are taken to show interest in and understanding of people, so a record with none is read as poor interpersonal orientation (Section 10.5.2). Apathy would be read from a short, constricted record with few movement or colour responses, and "lack of empathy" is too narrow, so "all the above" fails as well.

  21. Identify the correct option for Projective tests of Personality: i) Rorschach & TAT, ii) Eysenck Personality Questionnaire, iii) 16 PF, iv) MCMI-III. (Amity 2023)

    (a) Both i and ii (b) Only i (c) i, ii and iv (d) None

    Answer: (b) Only i. The Rorschach and the TAT present ambiguous stimuli and leave the response open; the Eysenck Personality Questionnaire, the 16PF and the MCMI-III are objective inventories with fixed items, fixed answers and a scoring key (Section 10.1.6 and Table 10.3).

  22. The Rorschach Inkblot Test has: (Amity 2023)

    (a) 3 chromatic and 7 achromatic cards (b) 5 chromatic and 5 achromatic cards (c) 10 inkblots and 1 blank card (d) 3 achromatic and 7 chromatic cards

    Answer: (b) 5 chromatic and 5 achromatic cards. Cards I, IV, V, VI and VII are achromatic; II and III add red and VIII, IX and X are multicoloured, so five are chromatic (Section 10.3.4). "3 chromatic and 7 achromatic" forgets that the red of Cards II and III is a hue, "3 achromatic and 7 chromatic" is wrong twice over, and the blank card belongs to the TAT (card 16), not the Rorschach.

  23. In the Rorschach Inkblot Test, a response that is determined primarily by the shape or form of the blot, with little consideration for color or movement, is scored as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) FC (Form-Color) (b) F (Form) (c) M (Human Movement) (d) C (Pure Color)

    Answer: (b) F (Form). A pure form response is determined by the shape or outline of the blot alone, and F is coded only when nothing else contributes (Section 10.4.4). FC is form with colour added, M is human movement, and C is colour with no form at all.

  24. A clinician is interested in understanding a client's characteristic way of thinking, feeling, and behaving in close relationships, by having them tell stories about ambiguous pictures. The MOST appropriate test to administer would be the: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Rorschach Inkblot Test (b) Thematic Apperception Test (TAT) (c) Sentence Completion Test (d) Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI)

    Answer: (b) Thematic Apperception Test (TAT). Only the TAT asks for stories about pictures of people, and with a relationship-focused scoring system such as Westen's SCORS it is the standard choice (Section 10.6.6). The Rorschach uses inkblots and asks for percepts, sentence completion uses written stems with no pictures, and the MMPI is a true-false inventory with neither pictures nor stories.

  25. In the Rorschach Inkblot Test, a response that is determined primarily by the chromatic color of the blot (for example, "this is a blood stain because it's red") is scored as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) FC (Form-Color) (b) CF (Color-Form) (c) C (Pure Color) (d) Cn (Color Naming)

    Answer: (c) C (Pure Color). Colour with no reference to shape is pure colour (Section 10.4.5). FC has a clear shape with colour added, CF has colour leading with a vague shape still present, and Cn merely names the colours ("red, green") without seeing anything.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The Rorschach has ten symmetrical cards (not 12, 15 or 20, and no blank card): five achromatic (I, IV, V, VI, VII), two black and red (II, III) and three multicoloured (VIII, IX, X), so five chromatic and five achromatic. asked 5 times
  2. The Rorschach is the inkblot test (not the MMPI, MBTI or TAT): it assesses personality and thought processes, helps measure thought disorder and identify mental illness, and in the projective tradition reveals unconscious intentions; it does not measure IQ, memory, dreams, physical illness or brain size. asked 4 times
  3. Projective versus objective: the Rorschach and the TAT are projective; the 16PF, MMPI-2, NEO-PI-R, EPQ, MBTI and MCMI-III are objective self-report inventories. The TAT is a projective personality test, not an objective, achievement, intelligence or neuropsychological test. asked 4 times
  4. The projective hypothesis: faced with an ambiguous, unstructured stimulus, a person imposes his own structure and so reveals needs, conflicts and unconscious processes. Projective tests claim to reveal unconscious processes, not intellectual attainment, parenting style or career aptitude; Lawrence K. Frank named "projective methods" in 1939. asked twice
  5. Lindzey's five families (1959): the TAT is construction and the Rorschach association; sentence completion and the Rosenzweig P-F Study are completion, the Szondi is choice or ordering, and the drawings are expression. asked twice
  6. The TAT's theory: Murray's need-press theory of psychogenic needs and environmental press, and the assumption that people project their needs and conflicts onto ambiguous pictures; not personal constructs (Kelly), operant conditioning (Skinner), cognitive distortions (Beck) or a single general intelligence factor. asked 3 times
  7. Hermann Rorschach (1884 to 1922), a Swiss psychiatrist, published Psychodiagnostik with its ten cards in 1921; Exner wrote the Comprehensive System, Murray the TAT and Jung the word association test. asked twice
  8. The TAT was published by Christiana D. Morgan and Henry A. Murray of the Harvard Psychological Clinic in 1935 (the 1930s), with the final set and Murray's manual in 1943; not Freud, Jung, Skinner, Rorschach, Exner or Bellak. asked twice
  9. For a client's ways of thinking, feeling and behaving in close relationships, through stories about ambiguous pictures, the test of choice is the TAT, not the Rorschach, sentence completion or the MMPI. asked twice
  10. Exner's Comprehensive System (John E. Exner Jr, 1974), built from the best-supported parts of the five American systems, is the major scoring system for the Rorschach, not for the MMPI, the TAT or the Wechsler scales, and not Beck's, Klopfer's or Piotrowski's. asked twice
  11. There is no "Murray system" for the Rorschach: the recognised systems are those of Beck, Klopfer, Hertz, Piotrowski, and Rapaport and Schafer, then Exner's Comprehensive System and R-PAS (Meyer and colleagues, 2011). asked twice
  12. Word association is projective: it is a founding member of Lindzey's association family, and Galton's word association (1879) is usually called the first projective technique. RML 2021 keyed it "not projective"; by the standard classification word association, the Rorschach, the TAT and sentence completion are all projective. asked once
  13. House-Tree-Person: John N. Buck, 1948; an achromatic (pencil) then a chromatic (crayon) phase, each followed by the post-drawing interrogation; not Aaron Beck, David Burns or Karen Machover. asked once
  14. The inquiry (enquiry) phase follows the response phase: each response is read back and clarified for location and determinants without leading questions; the cards are never shuffled, and coding comes afterwards. asked once
  15. TAT cards: 31 in the set (30 pictures and blank card 16); Murray gave one person 20 in two sessions of ten; a typical session today uses about 5 to 12 selected cards. asked once
  16. The purpose of analysing TAT stories is to reveal unconscious thoughts, conflicts and motives, not IQ, vocabulary or memory. asked once
  17. TAT versus Rorschach: the TAT uses ambiguous social scenes with characters and asks for stories; the Rorschach uses symmetrical inkblots and asks for percepts. asked once
  18. The main advantage of projective tests is that they avoid self-report biases (social desirability, faking, defensiveness); not "they let people use the defence of projection", not superior retest reliability, not numerical data. asked once
  19. Confabulatory (DW) responses, one detail generalised to the whole blot, indicate impaired reality testing; not lack of empathy, regressed ego or cognitive immaturity. asked once
  20. Absence of human (H) responses indicates poor interpersonal orientation; not apathy, not simply lack of empathy, and so not "all the above". asked once
  21. F, pure form: a response determined by the shape of the blot alone, with no colour or movement; not FC, M or C. asked once
  22. C, pure colour: a response determined by chromatic colour alone ("blood, because it's red"); FC is form-colour, CF colour-form and Cn colour naming. asked once
  23. The Comprehensive System gives no IQ score: it codes location (W, D, Dd, S), developmental quality, determinants, form quality, content, populars, organisational activity (Z) and special scores. asked once
  24. Many W responses in the Comprehensive System show organisational and integrative effort; reality testing is read from form quality, emotional reactivity from the colour responses. asked once
  25. Draw-a-Person (Karen Machover, 1949), used projectively, rests on the body-image hypothesis: the drawing reflects the person's self-concept and unconscious attitudes, not fine motor skill alone, academic achievement or specific lesions. asked once
  26. Children's Apperception Test (Leopold and Sonya Bellak, 1949): 10 cards of animals in human situations, for children of about 3 to 10; the CAT-H (1965) uses human figures and the CAT-S (1952) is a supplement. asked once
  27. Rosenzweig Picture-Frustration Study (Saul Rosenzweig, 1945): 24 cartoon frames with an empty speech balloon for the frustrated person, scored for direction (extrapunitive, intropunitive, impunitive) and type (obstacle-dominance, ego-defence, need-persistence). asked once
